# pg_warren — Index-Free-Adjacency Traversal and Graph Compute for PostgreSQL

**Technical note and build brief — v0.6, 19 October 2026**

> This document is intended to be dropped into an empty repository as the founding brief for an engineering agent. It describes *what* to build, *why* each design decision was taken, and the order in which to build it. Where a decision is deliberately left open, it is flagged in §14. Read the whole document before writing any code.
>
> Changes from v0.1: stable dense IDs across versions (§5.2), multi-version catalog with staging and retention (§5.1, §7), compute layer — vectors, semiring operations, vertex programs, eigensolver (§6), change-detection workflow (§8), `build_reverse` defaults to true, edge relations promoted into v1, milestones revised.
> Changes from v0.2: the query paradigm and the SQL/warren seam made explicit (§9.1); `warren.walk`, an in-engine typed path-pattern traversal with a stable step IR, added to v1 (§9.2); SQL/PGQ-style sugar recorded as the only language direction worth considering (§10).
> Changes from v0.3: §6 rewritten around "SQL all the way down" — the BSP runner and any program DSL are dropped; control flow lives in PL/pgSQL; three tiers of expressiveness (§6.1); `vector_map` takes SQL expression syntax with a compiled fast path (§6.3); `warren.gather` for tier-2 Pregel with SQL as the vertex language and a `Message` trait in the core (§6.4); contraction / project / lift for community-stateful computation (§6.6). Appendix A adds worked use cases with expected outcomes for test derivation.
> Changes from v0.4: §4A added — prior art as of October 2026, with a mandatory pre-M1 code reading of pgGraph and OneSparse. The policy is to build everything ourselves and to read prior art for the lessons it embodies, not to take it as a dependency.
> Changes from v0.5: the §4A prior-art reading is done (`docs/prior-art-notes.md`). Its outcomes: the §4A table is corrected; catalog dump registration, `PUBLIC` revocation, graph ownership and the access model (§5.1, §7); load-time segment validation (§5.3); one pinned snapshot and no source-table locks during builds, counting-sort CSR construction and a reverse CSR built by transpose (§5.4); a Rust-allocated, `Arc`-owned cache (§5.5); GraphBLAS operation naming (§6.3); error and interrupt discipline (§6.9); the bidirectional-BFS meeting rule, visible truncation and canonical component IDs (§9.3); toolchain, lint and CI rules (§11–§13); evidence recorded under §14 items 2, 4 and 16.

---

## 1. Summary

`pg_warren` is a PostgreSQL extension that provides **index-free adjacency (IFA) traversal and whole-graph computation** over graphs whose nodes and edges live in ordinary Postgres tables.

It is **not** a graph database. It does not replace Postgres storage, does not introduce a query language, and does not try to teach the planner about graphs. It is a *graph index plus compute engine*: a compact, memory-resident adjacency structure derived from relational tables, queried through SQL-callable traversal functions, and usable as a sparse matrix for vertex-centric and spectral computation. Think "pgvector for graphs" rather than "Neo4j inside Postgres".

The name: a *warren* is a dense network of interconnected burrows. Traversing one is a matter of following tunnels, not consulting a map.

## 2. Motivation

Postgres already handles shallow graph queries well. An indexed `edges(src, dst)` table plus a recursive CTE will do 1–3 hop neighbourhood queries at speeds comparable to native graph databases.

Two things it does badly:

**Deep traversal.** BFS to depth 6+, reachability over millions of nodes, shortest paths across a large component. The relational approach does one B-tree lookup per visited node, each costing hundreds of nanoseconds to microseconds and touching scattered pages. A CSR-style adjacency structure does the same traversal as contiguous array reads, close to memory bandwidth — one to two orders of magnitude faster.

**Global computation.** PageRank, connected components, label propagation, community detection, embedding propagation, spectral analysis of the adjacency or Laplacian matrix. These are iterative, touch every vertex many times, and are hopeless in SQL. Today teams export to NetworkX/igraph/GraphBLAS in application code, or stand up a separate graph system, and lose the single-source-of-truth property of keeping everything in Postgres.

The target users are teams building large, evolving knowledge systems on Postgres — relational graph tables, pgvector embeddings, full-text — who need an **escape valve** for deep traversal and whole-graph analytics without leaving the database or weakening its consistency guarantees for everything else.

### Design principles

**The governing principle: meet Postgres users where they already are.** People who keep their data in Postgres should get a high-performance escape valve for graph workloads without leaving Postgres and without fighting it. Their data stays in their tables, their logic stays in SQL, and their transactions, backups and replicas cover the graph as well. Each principle below is a consequence of this one. When a design choice is unclear, choose the option that asks the user to change least about how they already work. Speed comes from the engine underneath, not from moving the user somewhere else.

1. **Derived, not authoritative.** The graph structure is built *from* relational tables. The tables remain the source of truth. We never have to solve concurrent mutation of a pointer-rich structure under MVCC.
2. **Compose with SQL, don't replace it.** Traversal functions take node IDs in and return sets of node IDs out; compute functions take and return per-node vectors. Users join those back to their real tables. Postgres does what it's good at on either side; `pg_warren` does what it's good at in the middle.
3. **One substrate, two uses.** CSR serves both as an adjacency structure for traversal and as a sparse matrix for linear algebra. Everything in today's compute layer reduces to sparse-matrix × dense-vector (or dense-matrix) operations, which CSR does natively; §6.11 widens this to sparse × sparse.
4. **Versions are first-class.** A graph build is an immutable snapshot with a stable node-ID space, so builds can be compared with one another. This is what makes change detection possible.
5. **Honest consistency semantics.** Users opt in to staleness, and the extension makes staleness observable.
6. **SQL all the way down.** No query language and no vertex-program language. Control flow is the user's PL; per-row expressions are SQL expressions; the engine is kernels behind ordinary functions.
7. **Small surface area.** Resist feature creep until the core is fast and boring.
8. **Two styles over one substrate.** Vertex-centric (Pregel-style: `gather`, the library, PL/pgSQL loops) is the first-class, approachable way to write a graph computation and stays so. Sparse linear algebra is the power user's escape hatch for when that is not fast enough. Both run over the same CSR and versions, and both should make the best use of the hardware present, CPU threads now and GPUs later (§6.11). The escape hatch is Postgres-shaped as well: more `warren.*` functions over named matrices in the catalog, as vectors are now, not a matrix datatype or a new language.
## 3. Scope

### In scope (v1)

- Named graphs defined over user tables (or arbitrary SQL) producing a node set and an edge set, with an optional edge relation/type and an optional `float8` weight.
- Directed graphs; undirected is modelled as directed with both edge directions materialised.
- Full rebuild of a graph as a new **version**; stable dense node IDs across versions; configurable retention; staged (unpromoted) versions.
- Persistent storage of built versions in extension-owned tables (survives restarts, included in backups, replicates to standbys).
- Forward and reverse CSR (reverse on by default).
- In-memory cache loaded on first use per backend.
- Traversal primitives: BFS neighbourhood (k-hop), reachability, unweighted and weighted shortest path, connected components; node and relation filters.
- **Typed path-pattern walks** (`warren.walk`): an ordered list of steps, each with relation, direction, per-hop node filter and hop range, executed entirely in-engine, returning end nodes or full paths. The step list is a stable intermediate representation (§9.2).
- **Compute layer (§6):** per-node dense vectors/matrices; semiring SpMV and a SQL-expression `vector_map` (tier 1); `warren.gather` with SQL-typed messages and `collect` (tier 2); an algorithm library written in PL/pgSQL over the primitives; a Lanczos eigensolver for top-*k* eigenpairs of adjacency and Laplacian; spectral and structural distance between versions. No runner, no program DSL: control flow is the user's PL.
- Multi-threaded compute within a backend over extension-owned memory.

### Out of scope (v1) — but keep the door open

- Incremental/streaming updates to the CSR (§10).
- Shared-memory (DSM) cache (§10; brought into 1.0's scope as M9, §14 item 25, and designed in §5.6) and a dedicated compute background worker (§6.9; 1.x).
- Graph contraction / project / lift for community-stateful computation (§6.6 — done in 0.4.0; catalog shape fixed in v1).
- User-defined vertex programs via WASM (§6.8 — v1.2+).
- Node properties beyond vectors the user explicitly materialises.
- Any query language (Cypher, GQL, Gremlin), pattern matching with selectivity-aware planning.
- GPU execution.
- Replacing heap storage via the Table Access Method API. Explicitly rejected: see §4.
- Being the first derived-CSR graph index for Postgres. It is not; see §4A. The aim is to be the first *versioned, compute-capable* one.

## 4. Why not a Table Access Method?

The obvious "proper" way to get IFA inside Postgres is a custom TAM with its own page format where nodes hold inline adjacency lists. Considered and rejected:

- **MVCC breaks physical pointers.** TIDs are not stable across `UPDATE`; every edge pointing at a node would need forwarding or version chains. Solving this is most of the work of building a storage engine.
- **The planner doesn't know what to do with it.** A TAM only changes how tuples are stored; a self-join is still planned as a join. The speed-up only materialises if traversal is driven by custom functions anyway.
- **Infrastructure tax.** WAL, vacuum, TOAST, parallel scan, logical decoding, visibility — all must be supported or deliberately stubbed.
- **It is the wrong shape for global compute.** Spectral and vertex-centric algorithms want a dense, contiguous matrix representation, not tuples on pages. A TAM would still need CSR built beside it.

The derived-structure approach captures the benefit for the target workloads at perhaps a tenth of the engineering cost. If demand emerges for transactionally-consistent graph storage, the TAM route can be revisited with a working traversal and compute library in hand.

## 4A. Prior art, and standing on it

Surveyed October 2026. This section exists because the traversal-index half of this design is no longer novel, and the implementing agent should know what already exists before writing a line of the core. The policy is explicit: **`pg_warren` is built in full, in this repository, with no runtime dependency on any of the projects below.** A composite of three extensions with three persistence models and two allocators would be worse than any of its parts. Prior art is read, not imported: its value is that other people have already made and fixed mistakes we have not made yet, and reading their code surfaces those solutions before we need them. Where a specific idea or technique is adopted, the source is credited in a comment and in `NOTICE`.

| Project | What it is | Overlap with this note | Divergence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **pgGraph** (Evokoa; PGXN; Apache-2.0; Rust/pgrx 0.19; PG 14–18; 1.2.1 released 2026-09-23) | Compiles relationships from ordinary tables into forward and reverse CSR, persisted as `.pggraph` artifacts. Each backend loads a validated copy, which Linux backends can share through a sealed memfd. Source tables remain the truth. Its interface is SQL functions (`graph.traverse`, `graph.shortest_path`, `graph.search`) plus bounded GQL/openCypher profiles (`graph.gql`, `graph.cypher`). It also has trigger-based sync into backend-local overlays, RLS/tenant-scoped results, union-find components, integer-weighted Dijkstra, depth/node/frontier breakers and pagination. | §5 (derived CSR, forward+reverse, per-backend cache) and §9 (functions, not a language) almost exactly. Same toolchain. | Persistence is file artifacts, not transactional tables, so it conflicts with the staging and rollback semantics §7–§8 depend on. Generations are published through MVCC catalog rows that point at files. It has no user-visible versions, no stable dense IDs across rebuilds, no standby support, and refuses builds inside a transaction holding source write locks. Weights are `u32` integers, and node and edge counts are capped at 2³². It has no vectors, linear algebra or spectral work. |
| **OneSparse** (Michel Pelletier; Apache-2.0; C; PG18 only; 1.0.1) | Binds SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS into Postgres as `matrix`/`vector`/`scalar`/`graph` expanded-object types with semiring operators; BFS, SSSP, PageRank, triangle centrality, betweenness, square clustering. Depends on libgraphblas and LAGraph. | §6.3 (semiring SpMV) and parts of §6.7. Has done the SuiteSparse binding work that §6.10 decided against (read, not imported). | Matrices are a datatype, not a catalog of named graphs built from tables. They load from SQL (`matrix_query`, `matrix_agg`) with caller-supplied integer indices, from large objects, or from server files. A stored matrix is one varlena (≤1 GB) and is deserialised on every call. There are no versions, stable IDs, staging, `gather` with variable-width messages, or contraction. Kernels cannot be cancelled. It is PG18-only because it relies on PG18's `SupportRequestModifyInPlace` for in-place PL/pgSQL updates, a hard constraint against the PG 15–18 target. |
| **pg_igraph** (ineron; PGXN; MIT; C; PG 14+; 1.1.0, 2026-06-11) | Cypher-like language embedded in SQL; edges stored both directions in hash-partitioned tables with covering indexes; adaptive switch from SPI to in-memory hash maps for large frontiers. Requires pg_ilib. | Multi-hop pattern matching and shortest path. | Table-backed with per-query in-memory structures, and a bespoke language — the opposite of §9.1's paradigm. |
| **Apache AGE** | openCypher on Postgres heap tables and indexes. | None at the storage level. | Query language layer; no IFA, no compute. |
| **pgRouting** | Dijkstra, components, etc. over an edge SQL, building a Boost graph in memory per call. | Algorithm vocabulary; `pgr_*` function conventions. | No persistence across calls; rebuilds the graph on every invocation. |
| **DuckPGQ / Kùzu / PuppyGraph** | SQL/PGQ or Cypher engines outside Postgres (DuckDB extension, embedded DB, external engine over Postgres). | SQL/PGQ syntax as the eventual sugar (§10). | Not in-database. Note: SQL/PGQ was removed from PostgreSQL's 19 release branch, so there is no in-core implementation to target. |

### Mandatory pre-M1 code reading

*Done in M0. The record is [`docs/prior-art-notes.md`](docs/prior-art-notes.md), and its outcomes are folded into this note (v0.6). The brief is kept for reference.* Before M1, the agent reads the pgGraph and OneSparse sources end to end and writes `docs/prior-art-notes.md`: a short, specific record of what each project got right, what it got wrong, and what `pg_warren` will do differently and why. §14 item 16 records that the reading was done. What to look for:

1. **pgGraph, structure and loading.** How the CSR is laid out and aligned; how the per-backend snapshot is mapped and invalidated; how `graph.build()` streams from SPI and sorts (tuplesort? in-memory?) and what it does with dangling edges and duplicates; how it bounds frontier and depth and paginates; where its issue tracker and changelog show bugs — these are the mistakes already made. Compare each against §5.3–§5.5 and §9 and change this note where theirs is better.
2. **pgGraph, persistence.** Understand precisely *why* they chose file artifacts over tables (load speed? WAL volume? TOAST cost?) and whether those reasons threaten §5.1's table-backed design at scale. If the numbers say TOAST loading is too slow, §14 item 2's mmap cache moves forward — but the transactional table store stays, because §8 depends on it.
3. **pgGraph, pgrx usage.** How they handle memory contexts, SRF iteration, interrupt checking, extension upgrades and multi-version CI. Copy the patterns that work; this is the fastest way to avoid pgrx-specific traps.
4. **OneSparse, kernels.** How SuiteSparse's semiring dispatch, masks and accumulators are exposed, and what their performance envelope looks like on PG18. `pg_warren`'s native kernels (§6.3) should match its operation vocabulary where sensible so that users moving between the two find familiar names, and its benchmarks give a target for tier 1.
5. **OneSparse, types.** How a sparse matrix is passed through the executor as a datatype (expanded objects, TOAST, in-place modification). `pg_warren` deliberately does not do this — vectors are named, not passed — but the trade-offs they hit inform §14 item 13 (gather sources) and the vector persistence design.
6. **Positioning.** The README states what `pg_warren` adds over pgGraph and OneSparse — versions with stable IDs, staging and change detection, one CSR serving traversal and compute, SQL-only expressiveness, contraction — and credits ideas taken from either.

### What remains novel

Versioned snapshots with a stable dense-ID space; staged builds inside a transaction with rollback semantics; structural and spectral distance between versions; `gather` with SQL-typed messages over the same CSR used for traversal; contraction/project/lift as first-class operations; and the "SQL all the way down" compute model. None of these has a counterpart in the projects above. The traversal core, by contrast, is well-trodden ground, which is exactly why reading the trodden path first is cheap insurance.

## 5. Graph storage architecture

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ User tables     nodes(id, ...)   edges(src, dst, rel?, w?)   │
└───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                │  warren.create_graph(...)   warren.refresh(name, promote)
                ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Build pipeline (SPI, one snapshot)                           │
│  1. node_sql → match against prior id_map, append new dense  │
│     IDs, tombstone missing                                   │
│  2. edge_sql → remap to dense IDs, counting-sort by src      │
│  3. forward CSR per relation; reverse CSR by transpose       │
│  4. write segments to warren.segments under a new version    │
│  5. promote version (or leave staged)                        │
└───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Persistent store                                             │
│  warren.graphs     catalog: name, definition, current_version│
│  warren.versions   one row per build: stats, lsn, staged     │
│  warren.segments   (version_id, segment, chunk_no, bytea)    │
│  warren.vectors    named per-node vectors bound to a version │
│  (a graph may also be derived by contraction of another)     │
└───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                ▼  loaded on first use, keyed by version_id
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Backend-local cache  (CSR + vectors; M9: DSM shared, §5.6)   │
└───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                ▼
┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
│ Traversal functions         │ Compute layer                  │
│ bfs, walk, reachable,       │ vectors, spmv, vector_map,     │
│ shortest_path, dijkstra,    │ gather, eigen, contract/       │
│ components                  │ project/lift, version distance │
└─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 5.1 Catalog

```sql
CREATE TABLE warren.graphs (
    graph_id         serial PRIMARY KEY,
    name             text UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    owner            regrole NOT NULL,  -- set to current_user by create_graph; never user-writable (§7)
    readers          regrole[] NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',  -- roles (and their members) allowed to query (§7)
    source           text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'query' CHECK (source IN ('query', 'contraction')),  -- §6.6
    node_query       text,              -- SELECT <id> ...                       (source = 'query')
    edge_query       text,              -- SELECT <src>, <dst> [, <rel>] [, <weight>] ...
    search_path      text NOT NULL,     -- the creator's search_path; the source queries always run under it (§5.4)
    id_type          regtype NOT NULL,  -- int4 | int8 | uuid  (text: v1.1)
    directed         boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    build_reverse    boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    weighted         boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    has_relations    boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    retain_versions  int NOT NULL DEFAULT 2 CHECK (retain_versions >= 1),  -- promoted versions kept
    current_version  bigint,                       -- one of this graph's versions (checked by trigger, below); NULL until first promote
    store_merged     boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true, -- store the merged CSR of several relations (M7, §5.3)
    node_order       text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'id' CHECK (node_order IN ('id', 'locality'))
                     -- how dense ids are ordered at the first build and at compact (0.4.0, §5.2, §14 item 22)
);

CREATE TABLE warren.versions (
    version_id   bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
    graph_id     int NOT NULL REFERENCES warren.graphs ON DELETE CASCADE,
    seq          bigint NOT NULL,          -- monotonic per graph
    staged       boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    built_at     timestamptz NOT NULL,
    built_lsn    pg_lsn NOT NULL,
    node_count   bigint NOT NULL,          -- live nodes
    id_space     bigint NOT NULL,          -- dense ID high-water mark (incl. tombstones)
    edge_count   bigint NOT NULL,          -- stored edges summed over relations, after deduplication; undirected edges count twice
    dropped_edges bigint NOT NULL,         -- edge rows dropped: a NULL relation/weight the graph needs, or a NULL, unknown or tombstoned endpoint
    deduplicated_edges bigint NOT NULL,    -- parallel edges folded by deduplication (§14 item 4)
    target_width  smallint NOT NULL CHECK (target_width IN (4, 8)),  -- 4 (u32 targets) or 8 (u64), §5.3
    compacted_from bigint REFERENCES warren.versions ON DELETE SET NULL,  -- set by warren.compact: new ID space (§5.2)
    bytes        bigint NOT NULL,
    label        text,                     -- optional user label, e.g. 'corpus-2026-10-17'
    parent_version bigint REFERENCES warren.versions ON DELETE SET NULL,  -- set when derived by warren.contract (§6.6)
    label_vector   bigint,                    -- the label vector it was contracted on (§6.6; no foreign key: a trigger clears it, below)
    UNIQUE (graph_id, seq)
);

CREATE TABLE warren.segments (
    version_id  bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES warren.versions ON DELETE CASCADE,
    segment     text   NOT NULL,   -- 'id_map' | 'tombstones' | 'rel_dict' | 'offsets:<r>' | 'targets:<r>' | 'weights:<r>' | 'rev_offsets:<r>' | ...  (<r>: rel_dict index, or 'merged'; §5.3) | 'membership' (a contraction's, §6.6)
    chunk_no    int    NOT NULL,
    data        bytea  NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (version_id, segment, chunk_no)
);
ALTER TABLE warren.segments ALTER COLUMN data SET STORAGE EXTERNAL;

CREATE TABLE warren.vectors (
    vector_id   bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
    graph_id    int NOT NULL REFERENCES warren.graphs ON DELETE CASCADE,
    version_id  bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES warren.versions ON DELETE CASCADE,
    name        text NOT NULL,
    dtype       text NOT NULL,     -- 'f32' | 'f64' | 'i64' | 'bool'
    width       int  NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,   -- >1 for matrices (N × width, row-major)
    length      bigint NOT NULL,   -- = version.id_space
    created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    UNIQUE (graph_id, version_id, name)
);
CREATE TABLE warren.vector_chunks (
    vector_id bigint REFERENCES warren.vectors ON DELETE CASCADE,
    chunk_no  int,
    data      bytea NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (vector_id, chunk_no)
);
```

Chunks are fixed-size (8 MiB, `CHUNK_SIZE` in `pg_warren/src/build/chunk.rs`; 1 KiB in `pg_test` builds so that small test graphs span several chunks), numbered from 0 per segment, so a multi-GB graph is many TOASTed rows. Reading a segment concatenates its chunks in order and rejects a gap. Because all of this is ordinary tables, it is WAL-logged, backed up, replicated to standbys, and — crucially for §8 — **transactional**: a version built inside a transaction that then rolls back simply disappears.

The bootstrap SQL registers every `warren.*` table and sequence with `pg_extension_config_dump`. Without that, `pg_dump` omits extension-member data, and graph definitions, versions and vectors would silently be missing from logical dumps.

**Dump and restore (M7).** The tables' constraints come from `CREATE EXTENSION`, before a restore loads their data. `pg_dump` therefore orders that data by foreign key, and it cannot order tables in a foreign-key cycle (it warns, and the restore fails on the first row that references a later table). The catalog's foreign keys between tables therefore form no cycle; a table may still reference itself (`compacted_from`, `parent_version`), since that is checked at the end of its `COPY`. `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` would not help: `psql` restoring a plain dump, and `pg_restore` without `--single-transaction`, commit each `COPY` on its own. Up to 0.2.0, `graphs.current_version` and `versions.label_vector` were foreign keys that closed two cycles (with `versions.graph_id` and `vectors.version_id`), so no dump of a database with a promoted graph restored. Since 0.3.0 neither is a foreign key. Two `BEFORE` triggers check what the `current_version` key checked on updates and deletes. `UPDATE … current_version` must name one of the graph's own versions, which is stricter than the key, since that allowed another graph's. The current version cannot be deleted, except with its graph. They are not equivalent to a key, in two ways:
- *Inserts are not checked.* That is what lets a restore, which only inserts, load the tables in any order, so a restored or hand-written row can name any version. `GraphDefinition::find` (and `lock`) therefore read `current_version` together with the version it names, joined on both its id and the graph's. A stored value that is not one of the graph's versions is kept apart (`stray_current_version`) and never used as the current version. `lock` takes the graph's row lock and re-reads that join in a second statement. With both in one statement, a lock that waited would re-check only the graph's newest row under READ COMMITTED (EvalPlanQual), keep the version joined before the wait, and report a just-promoted version as stray (harness test `lock_reread_race`). Every query function, `refresh` and `compact` refuse such a graph with 55000 (`graph "…" records current version …, which is not one of its versions …; promote one of its versions`). Before this, `compact` copied another graph's version into the graph, and `refresh` built on another graph's id map. `promote` still works and is the remedy. `info` reports the stored value, with no staleness for it.
- *Direct DML.* A direct `DELETE FROM warren.versions` now waits on the graph's row (`FOR SHARE`), so it can deadlock with a `promote` or `drop_version` of the same graph. Lock the graph's row first (`SELECT … FROM warren.graphs WHERE graph_id = … FOR UPDATE`), or use `drop_version`.
- *Locking is the triggers' own.* The update trigger reads the version `FOR KEY SHARE`, as a key's check would, so a concurrent delete waits and then fails. The delete trigger reads the graph's row `FOR SHARE`, so it sees the latest committed `current_version`, not the snapshot's. A promotion committed after a REPEATABLE READ transaction's snapshot then makes its delete fail with `serialization_failure`, and under READ COMMITTED with 23503. An earlier version read the graph from the snapshot, and a REPEATABLE READ delete removed a version promoted after the snapshot (found in the M7 security review; harness test `current_version_race`).

`label_vector` is NULL except on a contraction's versions (0.4.0, §6.6); it is protected without closing a cycle again (`bootstrap.rs` has a test that fails on any cycle): a trigger clears it when its vector is deleted. A restore needs no flags, but it does need the roles that `owner`, `readers` and `created_by` name, since `regrole` values are dumped by name (`pg_dumpall --roles-only` first, on another cluster). `pg_dump` still warns `there are circular foreign-key constraints on this table: versions`, for the self-references, and hints at `--disable-triggers`. Ignore that hint: the self-references need no order, and disabling triggers would skip the checks above. `test/dump.py` (CI, every major) fails on any other warning from the dumps or the restores. It builds graphs with promoted, compacted and staged versions and the vectors of two roles. It dumps them in plain and custom formats and restores each through `psql` and `pg_restore`, then compares `info`, `bfs` and `neighbours` on every version and every vector's values, and builds a new version and vector on the restored catalog. A non-superuser's dump is in §7.

The bootstrap SQL also runs `REVOKE ALL … FROM PUBLIC` on every `warren.*` table and sequence. No role except the catalog owner (the role that owns them, §7) can read or write the catalog, segments or vectors directly; all access goes through `warren.*` functions. This is what makes the access check in §7 meaningful: segment and vector bytes are reachable only through functions that perform it. The schema itself grants `USAGE` to `PUBLIC`, so any role can call `warren.*` functions (each checks access itself).

### 5.2 Node identity — stable dense IDs

CSR wants dense `0..N-1` integer IDs. User tables have `bigint`, `uuid`, etc.

**Dense IDs are stable across versions of a graph.** This is the key change from v0.1 and the property that lets vectors computed on version *n* be compared with vectors on version *n+1*.

- The first build assigns dense IDs `0..N-1` in `id_type` sort order, or, for a graph whose `node_order` is `'locality'`, in locality order: those ids are provisional, and the build renumbers them by a permutation computed from its edges before it builds the CSRs (§5.4, §14 item 22).
- Each subsequent build starts from the previous promoted version's `id_map`. Nodes present in both keep their dense ID. New nodes are appended at the high-water mark in sort order. Nodes that have disappeared keep their dense ID but are marked in a `tombstones` bitmap; their adjacency rows are empty. Dense IDs are never reused. A node that reappears after being tombstoned gets its old dense ID back and its tombstone cleared.
- `id_space` (high-water mark) therefore grows monotonically; `node_count` is live nodes. Vectors are allocated at `id_space` length; tombstoned slots hold zero/NaN per dtype.
- `warren.compact(name)` is an explicit operation that renumbers to remove tombstones, producing a new version with a **new ID space** that is incompatible with earlier versions for vector comparison (recorded as `compacted_from` on the version row; `warren.distance` refuses to compare across a compaction boundary). Run it when the tombstone fraction is high and no cross-version comparisons are pending. *Implemented (M2, `warren_core::version::compact`):* live nodes get dense IDs `0..node_count` in `id_type` order (as a first build would; *0.4.0:* in the graph's `node_order`, or the one `compact(name, node_order)` is given, §14 item 22), the relation dictionary is unchanged, every CSR is rebuilt from the per-relation forward edges through the normal build (weights, `build_reverse` and whether the merged CSR is stored kept; `deduplicated_edges` is 0, since nothing is folded), and the old→new mapping is returned so bound vectors can be carried across. *SQL (M2):* `warren.compact(name) RETURNS bigint` compacts the current version, records the result as a new version with `compacted_from` set (its `dropped_edges`, `deduplicated_edges` and `built_lsn` carried over from the source, since it holds the same edges as of the same snapshot), promotes it with retention, and returns its id; later refreshes continue in the new ID space. **Lineage (M2):** a version whose `seq` is below that of the graph's latest compacted version (`compacted_from IS NOT NULL`) is in a replaced ID space: `promote` refuses to make it current, and `refresh` refuses to build on it, with `object_not_in_prerequisite_state` naming the compaction. A graph therefore never returns to an ID space a compaction replaced, which vectors and `distance` (aligned by dense ID) rely on. *Implemented (M4, `vector::move_vectors`):* `compact` **moves** every persisted vector bound to the source version into the compacted one (decided in M4 review): each is rewritten under the same name and `created_by` with its rows gathered by the old → new renumbering (`Renumbering::old_dense_ids`), so every live node keeps its value and tombstoned rows are dropped, then deleted from the source; one vector is held at a time, and a `NOTICE` names the vectors moved. Moving rather than copying lets retention clear the old id space: after a compaction the source cannot be promoted, refreshed from or compared across (`diff`, `distance`, `vector_norm_diff` refuse), so vectors left on it would only pin dead storage. Transient vectors (§6.2) are not moved; they stay keyed to the source until freed. Dropping the source version later sets `compacted_from` to NULL; `diff` then still refuses through `warren-core`'s dense-ID check.
- `id_map` is stored as (external ID, dense ID) pairs sorted by external ID so external→dense is a binary search; an inverse array gives dense→external in O(1).
- Traversal and compute functions accept and return **external IDs** by default; vector materialisation to tables goes through `id_map`.

### 5.3 CSR layout

Per relation *r* (a single implicit relation, named `''` in `rel_dict`, if `has_relations = false`). In segment names *r* is the relation's index in `rel_dict`, which is append-only across versions, so a relation keeps its segment names; the merged CSR's segments use `merged` (`offsets:merged`, …) and are stored when there are no relations (an empty CSR), or several and the graph has `store_merged` (M7, below):

| Segment | Type | Length | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `offsets:r` | `u64[]` | id_space+1 | `offsets[i]..offsets[i+1]` indexes `targets` |
| `targets:r` | `u32[]` / `u64[]` | M_r | dense destination IDs, sorted within row |
| `weights:r` | `f64[]` | M_r | if `weighted` |
| `rev_offsets:r`, `rev_targets:r`, `rev_weights:r` | | | if `build_reverse` |
| `id_map` | per `id_type` + `u64` | N | sorted by external ID |
| `tombstones` | bitmap | id_space | |
| `rel_dict` | text[] | R | relation names → index |

`u32` targets when `id_space < 2³²`, else `u64`; width recorded in `versions.target_width`. Sorted targets allow `has_edge` by binary search and make set-intersection algorithms (triangles, Jaccard) straightforward.

Per-relation CSRs are stored separately so a traversal or SpMV can be restricted to a relation mask without filtering; a "merged" all-relations CSR is built additionally if `has_relations` and `store_merged` (it is the common case for global computation). An M1 benchmark checks this choice against the alternative of a single merged CSR with a parallel adaptive-width (`u8`/`u16`/`u32`) relation array, which pgGraph uses and which needs about half the storage when `has_relations`. **Decided (M1): keep per-relation CSRs plus the merged CSR.** `benches/relation_layout.rs` (100K nodes, 1M weighted edges, 5 relations, single thread, 4-vCPU Xeon) measured:

| | per-relation + merged | one CSR + `u8` relation array |
|---|---|---|
| forward bytes | 28.8 MB (16.0 MB without merged) | 13.8 MB |
| build, forward + reverse | 104 ms | 40 ms |
| BFS, 1 / 2 / 3 / 5 of 5 relations | 2.0 / 4.9 / 7.2 / 5.3 ms | 5.4 / 8.7 / 9.3 / 6.0 ms |
| weighted row sums, 1 / 2 / 3 / 5 of 5 | 0.88 / 1.8 / 2.7 / 1.7 ms | 3.0 / 5.0 / 4.4 / 2.2 ms |

Masked traversal and SpMV are 1.1–3.4× faster per relation, which is the hot path; the cost is about 2× storage and a build that stays well inside the §12 `refresh()` budget. Revisit if the memory cap (not speed) becomes the binding constraint; dropping the stored merged CSR is the first lever (done in M7, below).

**Decided (M7): the stored merged CSR is optional, per graph, and stored by default.** `create_graph(…, store_merged boolean DEFAULT true)` records the choice in `graphs.store_merged`; it cannot be changed afterwards and `compact` keeps it, so it is every version's layout: the loader takes it from the graph and, with several relations, a missing merged segment on a graph that stores it is `SegmentMissing` (data corrupted), not a version that merges on the fly. (A per-version record would be needed only if the choice became changeable.) Without the merged CSR, all-relations reads merge the relations' rows on the fly behind one domain interface: `GraphCsr::all_relations()` gives the all-relations (merged) rows as `warren_core::graph::CsrRows`, which reads either one CSR (the stored merged CSR, or a graph's only relation) or every relation's rows merged as they are read. A merged row is a k-way merge of the relations' sorted rows (typed slices, held inline for up to 8 relations, so a row allocates nothing below that): ascending and distinct, exactly the stored merged CSR's row. Kernels do not see the layout: traversal (`Adjacency`), `components`, `triangles` (whose scratch, charged to `warren.max_compute_memory`, therefore holds each edge once in both layouts), `gather`'s pull rows, `diff`'s `all_relations` delta and degree shifts and the `degree` distance read `CsrRows`; products (`spmv`, `spmm`, and so `eigen` and `distance('spectral')`) and `gather`'s push read `GraphCsr::selected(All)`, which is then every relation's CSRs, and fold an edge in several relations into one entry with its weights summed in relation order, as the build sums them into the merged CSR (a product sorts each such row in a scratch whose capacity is charged to `warren.max_compute_memory` and reserved with `try_reserve`: 16 bytes per entry of the longest merged row, per worker thread, at most 16 B × R × the largest per-relation degree. A stored merged CSR needs no scratch. Merging those rows with their weights through `CsrRows` would avoid it, but measured slower than the sort for weighted rows, so the sort and its scratch stay). Results are identical either way up to NaN payloads (bit for bit otherwise: row order, weights, `last_stats()` work counts and deduplicated-edge semantics included; a 3,000-case review proptest found only NaN sign bits differing). Covered by property tests in `warren-core` (the merged view against the stored merged CSR over 1–12 relations, `u32` and `u64` targets, with and without reverse CSRs; traversal rows; products with fractional weights; gather, triangles and their memory charge, compaction, diff, eigen) and pg_tests calling every all-relations SQL function on both layouts (weighted with reverse CSRs, and unweighted without; cross-graph distance between the two). Its cost, from `benches/relation_layout.rs` (`relation_layout/merged`: the same 1M weighted edges over 100K nodes split over 1–5 and 10 relations, forward and reverse CSRs, through the real kernels on one thread; stored / on the fly, median ms). The 4-vCPU Xeon was shared with other builds: a rerun under heavier load moved single cells by 30–65%, so read the ratios within a run, not the absolute times, and treat even those as about ±30%:

| relations | MB (forward + reverse) | build | bfs_out | bfs_both | spmv_forward | spmv_reverse | gather_sum | components |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 25.6 / 25.6 | 60.6 / 57.6 | 12.7 / 12.5 | 25.8 / 27.6 | 2.4 / 2.5 | 2.6 / 2.5 | 19.9 / 20.8 | 7.4 / 7.6 |
| 2 | 52.8 / 27.2 | 91.4 / 57.8 | 12.7 / 26.2 | 26.9 / 55.2 | 2.5 / 11.5 | 2.5 / 11.6 | 20.5 / 30.4 | 6.4 / 17.5 |
| 3 | 54.4 / 28.8 | 97.8 / 58.8 | 11.9 / 30.6 | 27.7 / 62.9 | 2.5 / 14.8 | 2.5 / 15.2 | 19.6 / 33.3 | 6.5 / 20.3 |
| 4 | 56.0 / 30.4 | 100.1 / 58.9 | 12.2 / 33.4 | 24.6 / 71.1 | 2.6 / 17.5 | 2.5 / 17.6 | 19.4 / 35.9 | 6.4 / 23.7 |
| 5 | 57.6 / 32.0 | 105.3 / 61.8 | 12.0 / 37.6 | 24.9 / 79.0 | 2.5 / 20.5 | 2.6 / 20.9 | 20.9 / 39.3 | 6.3 / 28.5 |
| 10 | 65.6 / 40.0 | 128.3 / 73.8 | 12.8 / 77.9 | 25.0 / 163.1 | 2.5 / 27.9 | 2.6 / 28.7 | 19.8 / 84.1 | 6.5 / 66.9 |

Not storing it saves the merged CSR's bytes (44% of a five-relation weighted graph's CSRs, 39% of a ten-relation one's) and 35–45% of the build time, but every all-relations read pays a merge: at 2–5 relations BFS 2.1–3.2× slower, components 2.7–4.5×, `gather` 1.5–1.9×, and SpMV, whose stored inner loop is a single slice walk, 4.6–8.3×; at 10 relations BFS 6–6.5×, components 10×, SpMV 11× and `gather` 4×. The cost grows with the relation count R, independently of how the edges spread: a merged row visits R rows (most of them empty in a sparse graph), each step of the merge scans every non-empty row's head, above 8 relations each row's merge allocates a `Vec` for the rows, `degree` is O(deg·R) rather than O(1), and `edge_count` is O(N·R + M) rather than O(1). (Only `diff`'s `all_relations` delta calls `edge_count` on the all-relations rows, once per version.) (With one relation both layouts are the same code; the 1-relation row shows the noise.) A first merge that allocated a vector of peekable iterators per row was up to 15% slower again; for weighted product rows, the k-way merge was slower than sorting each row's entries, the products' existing path for several selected relations, so products keep the sort. At the §12 scale that is the difference between meeting and missing the `pagerank` and `components` rows, so speed stays the default and memory is the opt-in: `store_merged := false` is for a graph whose merged CSR would not fit the memory limits (a five-relation weighted 10M/100M graph, §12), or whose all-relations work is rare next to relation-masked work.

Every per-relation CSR has a full `id_space + 1` offsets array, so offsets cost 8·(N+1)·(R + 1) bytes per direction (8·(N+1)·R without the merged CSR) regardless of edge count (1,000 relations over 10M nodes would need 80 GB forward). `GraphCsr::build_within` computes this (`GraphCsr::offsets_bytes`) and refuses the build before allocating when it exceeds the caller's budget; the adapter passes its memory cap (§14 item 17). Sparse per-relation offsets are the longer-term lever.

Alignment: every segment starts on a 64-byte boundary when loaded.

Every segment is validated on load, before any unchecked slice is formed: alignment and lengths, `offsets[0] = 0`, offsets monotonic, `offsets[id_space] = M`, every target `< id_space`, and `id_map` sorted. The check is O(N + M), which is cheap next to the detoast. The segment decoder has a fuzz target (§12). Each segment is encoded as a 16-byte little-endian header (magic `WRSG`, format, kind, element width, element count) followed by its payload, so a decoder can reject a segment of the wrong kind or width before reading it.

### 5.4 Build pipeline

`warren.refresh(name, promote bool DEFAULT true, label text DEFAULT NULL)`:

Before the build, `refresh` checks that the caller is a member of the owner role (§7) and locks the graph's `warren.graphs` row `FOR UPDATE`, so refreshes of one graph run one at a time, and re-reads `current_version` under that lock.

1. Pin one snapshot for the whole build: `CommandCounterIncrement()`, then `PushActiveSnapshot(GetTransactionSnapshot())`, popped on the normal path. Transaction or subtransaction abort releases it on error. Both queries run as **read-only** SPI cursors (`SPI_cursor_open_with_args(…, read_only = true, …)`) under that snapshot, in Postgres's read-only transaction state (*M5:* so a function they call cannot write either; an integrity guard, not a sandbox, §7). Under READ COMMITTED, non-read-only SPI statements would each take a fresh snapshot, and `node_query` and `edge_query` could disagree. The queries execute with the graph owner's privileges and under the `search_path` captured from the creator by `create_graph` (`graphs.search_path`), so a caller cannot redirect their unqualified names (`SetUserIdAndSecContext`, as `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW` does; see §7 for both identity switches in `refresh`). No locks are taken on source tables beyond the `AccessShareLock` the queries themselves take; pgGraph, by contrast, takes `SHARE NOWAIT` on every source. Record `pg_current_wal_lsn()`. Each query's result columns are checked against the definition on every refresh (ids of the graph's `id_type`, then `text` relation and `float8` weight per the flags), so a source whose types changed since `create_graph` fails with `datatype_mismatch`.
2. Stream `node_query` through the cursor into `warren-core`. Sort the external IDs in memory, and merge-walk against the previous promoted version's `id_map` (if any) to assign dense IDs per §5.2. NULL node ids are ignored.
3. Stream `edge_query`. Map `src` and `dst` to dense IDs in `warren-core` (binary search over the typed `id_map`, never text keys or SQL joins). Map `rel` through `rel_dict`, appending new relations. Edges with a NULL, unknown or tombstoned endpoint, or with a NULL relation (if `has_relations`) or NULL weight (if `weighted`), are dropped and counted in `dropped_edges`. If `directed = false`, emit both directions with the same weight (a self-loop once).
4. Build the forward CSR with no comparison sort, since dense IDs are already known: counting-sort the edges by `src`, a group of relations at a time, straight into each relation's CSR arrays, then sort each row by `dst` and deduplicate parallel edges within the row (sum weights if weighted, in input order; count in `deduplicated_edges`; see §14). Build the merged CSR, last, if there are multiple relations and the graph has `store_merged` (a target reached through several relations appears once, weights summed in relation order); with one relation, its CSR is the all-relations CSR, and without the merged CSR all-relations reads merge the relations' rows on the fly (§5.3). Build each reverse CSR by **transposing** its forward CSR as soon as that is complete: count in-degrees, prefix-sum, scatter while scanning sources in order. That is O(N + M), and rows come out sorted by source. Each CSR is written (step 5) and freed before the next relation is built (memory discipline, below).
5. Insert a new `versions` row with `staged = true` (`seq` is the graph's highest plus one) before the CSRs are built, write each CSR's segments in chunks under it as step 4 completes them, then `id_map`, `tombstones` and `rel_dict`, and record the version's stats (its edge counts are known only once every relation is deduplicated) and their total `bytes`.
*0.4.0, the first build of a `'locality'` graph* (no current version to build on, §14 item 22): between steps 3 and 4, once the edge list holds every edge in the provisional (id-order) dense ids, `Permutation::locality` orders them, and the edge list (in place) and the id map (both directions and its tombstones) are renumbered by it. The CSRs, per relation and merged, forward and reverse, with their weights and deduplication, are then built once, as for any build, from the renumbered edges; nothing else is keyed by dense id yet (a first build has no vectors). The order's scratch and the permutation are charged to `warren.max_build_memory` before `BuildPlan::new`, and freed before the plan's check, so a build refused for memory still writes nothing. Refreshes that build on a current version keep its ids and append (§5.2), whatever the graph's order.
6. If `promote`: set `graphs.current_version`, set `staged = false`, and delete promoted versions beyond `retain_versions` (never delete a version that has named vectors bound to it unless `warren.drop_version` is called explicitly — see §7).

Because the build runs under the caller's snapshot, **it sees the caller's uncommitted writes**. This is the mechanism behind staged evaluation (§8).

Memory discipline: a 100M-edge graph (~1.2 GB forward + ~1.2 GB reverse) should build in under 4 GB of backend RAM. *M7 (build-peak):* **every graph-sized buffer a build holds is charged to `warren.max_build_memory`** (`warren_core::graph::BuildMemory`; a `BuildBuffer`'s capacity is charged before it is reserved with `try_reserve_exact` and released when it shrinks or drops), and a build that would exceed it fails with `configuration_limit_exceeded` (53400): the node ids, the base version's id map and relation dictionary while they load and decode, the rebuilt id map in both directions and its tombstones (reserved exactly, once its new ids are merge-counted), the relation dictionary as edge rows add names (each new name's two copies and the tables' growth, reserved fallibly: a relation column holding a name per row grows it with the edges), the per-relation edge counts and row headers, the edge list as it grows, each relation's rows, the sort scratch, the transposes, the merged CSR, and the segment writer's buffers (the chunk it encodes into, and four chunks for the copies Postgres holds while it inserts one: the `bytea` parameter, the heap tuple, TOAST's compression buffer and slices, measured below). `refresh` reports the most it held at `DEBUG1`. The refusal is up front where it can be: after the edges are read, `BuildPlan::new` checks every edge, counts each relation's edges and the longest row, groups the relations, and estimates the build's peak by following its allocations in order with every relation at its edge count before deduplication (an upper bound); `refresh` checks that estimate before it writes the version row, so a build refused for memory leaves nothing behind (a unit test sweeps budgets and checks that every refusal precedes the first write). Earlier steps (node ids, id map, edge list growth) are refused at their reservation. The offsets check of §14 item 17 is kept as well, before the edge query runs and again whenever an edge row adds a relation, so a relation column with a name per row is refused while the edges are read (the PR #17 review's repro, 3M rows over 1,000 nodes under a 64MB budget: refused in 0.2 s, 5 MB past the backend's baseline with `work_mem = 4MB`, where it took 445 MB before). The pipeline: the edge list stores endpoints at the target width (20 B per weighted edge below 2³² nodes: `u32` source, target and relation, `f64` weight; the relation column is dropped at once for a single relation), reserved once for the planner's row estimate when that fits the budget (else it grows as rows arrive, by as much as still fits near the limit, and is shrunk to fit when the intake ends). Relations are built in groups, a pass over the edge list each: `BuildPlan::new` tries 1, 2 and 4 cost-balanced groups, then the finest split (groups of about an eighth of all the forward CSR bytes, so at most about eight passes), and takes the fewest whose estimated need stays within half of what remains of the budget besides the segment writer's buffers, or within the finest split's need if that is more. A graph small for its budget is built in one pass, as fast as before streaming; a large one holds no more than the finest split; and half of what remains is left for the memory the build does not account (Postgres's own, below). In each group, a stable counting sort by source scatters the group's edges straight into its relations' CSR arrays (counts go to `offsets[source + 2]`, so the offsets serve as the scatter cursors and end up the CSR's; no cursor array and no intermediate rows), each row is then sorted and its parallel edges folded in place (weighted rows sorted stably in a scratch buffer of the longest row, charged twice for the sort's own buffer), and each relation's forward CSR is transposed (the same trick) and handed with its reverse to a `CsrSink`, which in `refresh` writes their segments chunk by chunk (`encode_part`: a segment is never copied whole) and frees them. The last group frees the edge list column by column as it scatters, unless the merged CSR follows: it is built last from the edge list itself, scattering targets, then each entry's relation, then the weights, freeing each input column as it goes (28 B per weighted edge at its peak), and folds each row's weights per relation in input order, then across relations in index order, exactly as before (a proptest with order-sensitive weights pins it). The version row is inserted before the segments stream under it, and its edge counts and bytes are recorded once they are written. Peak, as charged by the build itself at 10M nodes and 100M edges (`int8` ids, u32 targets, reverse CSRs, soak.py's relations and weights, a 4 GiB budget, the segment writer's buffers included: the real build run in `warren-core`, not in SQL): **1.56 GB** for one unweighted relation (was 2.00 before streaming), **3.24 GB** for five weighted relations without the merged CSR (was 3.76) and **3.24 GB** with it (was 7.28); both five-relation builds take four groups, where the finest split would hold 2.89 and 3.12 GB. A counting allocator (`warren-core/tests/build_memory.rs`, 20K nodes and 200K edges, directed and undirected, reserved and grown, from a base version with tombstones, and one relation per row) checks that the charge covers every allocation to within a fixed 4 kB and that the plan covers the charge. *What a backend holds past the charge* was measured through SQL for b-merged (peak RssAnon past the backend's baseline against the `DEBUG1` peak, the §12 machine and settings, `work_mem = 64MB`): 30.4 MB at 1M nodes and 10M edges and 30.4 MB at 3M and 30M, so flat in the edges (31.1 and 31.1 MB with glibc's mmap and trim thresholds fixed at 1 MB, so not freed heap memory glibc kept); with 1 MiB chunks it fell to 19.4 MB, so about 11 MB of it was the copies of the chunk being inserted beyond the two then charged, which the writer now charges as four. What remains, about 18 MB, is Postgres's own (executor, SPI, catalogs), plus the edge query's own execution memory, which `work_mem` bounds per plan node (a function scan's tuplestore took about 63 MB more at `work_mem = 64MB` than at 4MB in the repro above). So a 10M/100M refresh should peak at about 1.6, 3.3 and 3.3 GB of backend memory, within the 4 GiB default; the M7 full-scale soak measured 1.55, 3.26 and 3.26 GB (RssAnon, §12). The budget is the build's alone: a backend's cache (§5.5) can hold versions beside it, among them the version the refresh starts from, whose id map the build reads from its segments again rather than from the cache, so a backend's peak during `refresh` is up to `warren.max_build_memory` plus what its cache holds. Before M7's fix the edge reader also held about 17 B of Postgres memory per row of a graph with relations, until the refresh ended: pgrx's typed getter copies a `text` datum into the parent of the current memory context (about 1.7 GB at 100M edges, which is why the soak measured 5.5 and 9.0 GB against estimates of ~3.7 and ~5.8 GB); the relation is now read in place from the tuple (`source::text_in_place`), each batch in a memory context reset after it. Build time matches the builder before streaming when relations fit one group (`relation_layout/merged/build`, 1M weighted edges, one thread, main and this branch back to back, stored / on the fly): one relation 59 / 63 → 49 / 47 ms, five 120 / 69 → 122 / 66 ms, ten 136 / 79 → 136 / 88 ms. With a fixed eight-way split it was 137 / 83 ms at five relations. An external-sort spill is added only if measurements show it is needed.

### 5.5 The cache and a backend's memory

*Describes the code as of M9 (0.5.0, §5.6 step 7). §14 items 18, 20 and 31 record the decisions, §12 the measurements.* A loaded version lives in one of two places: a backend's own, **private** cache, or the cluster's **shared** cache (§5.6), one copy mapped by every backend that reads it. Query functions, `LoadedVersion` and `warren-core` see no difference.

**Opening a version (`cache::open`).** Every query function resolves its graph through one saved plan (`GraphDefinition::find`), checks reader access (§7), and takes the version asked for (which must belong to the graph) or the current one, re-read on every call under the statement's snapshot (so a promoted build is read by the next statement, REPEATABLE READ callers keep the version their snapshot saw, and a graph never promoted is `object_not_in_prerequisite_state`). `cache::cached` then looks the version up in the private cache, then asks the shared cache, which finds, waits for or loads it; the shared cache is skipped (and the version loaded privately) when `warren.shared_cache_memory` is 0, the registry is unavailable (§5.6, Fallback), the version was created by the current transaction (a staged build queried before commit, §8), or admission refuses it. The extension's `pg_extension` oid, part of the shared key, is looked up once per backend and forgotten when a backend processes a change to `pg_namespace` (`cache::installation`; dropping the extension drops its schema): at its next command in the backend that made it, and in others at their next processing of shared invalidations, which can come mid-transaction (at a lock acquisition): version, graph and vector ids restart with a new installation, so when it changes, the backend also drops everything its private caches hold.

**Loading (`cache::load`, `cache::region`).** A version is loaded into one region, sized exactly from its stored segments' lengths before anything but the relation dictionary is read: a 64-byte header and a table of contents, then every array (both directions of the id map, the tombstones, each CSR's offsets, targets and weights) at a 64-byte-aligned offset. A private region is a single zeroed Rust allocation (`alloc_zeroed`, so `calloc` hands out fresh zero pages rather than `memset`ting them; failure is 53200, never an abort); a shared one is a DSM segment. Each segment's chunks are read one at a time through a read-only cursor, each detoasted into a memory context reset after it, its header checked as it arrives, and copied straight into place (`int4` id-map pairs, and every pair on a big-endian target, re-laid-out pair by pair); the id map's second direction is built in place; then the version is validated once (`IdMap::from_segments`, `Tombstones::from_segment`, `Csr::from_segments`, `GraphCsr::from_parts`, `GraphVersion::new`). Arrays are read through `SegmentBuffer` views that keep the region alive, so a region is freed when its last view drops. Beside the version, a load holds one chunk and the relation dictionary (*M7 soak:* a 1.2 GB version's load peaked at 3.2 GB when every chunk was held in SPI memory and in Rust, and at 1.69 GB once chunks streamed into a buffer per segment; with M9's region it holds no segment buffer). Cold loads run at about a second per GB.

**The private cache (`cache::versions`, `vector::cache`, `vector::transient`).** A map `version_id → Arc<LoadedVersion<E>>` (type-erased, one entry per version whatever its id type); persisted vectors are cached the same way by `vector_id` (a vector row is never updated, so an id names one immutable vector), unless the shared cache holds them (§5.6, §14 item 38: a read that misses the private vector cache asks it before loading privately); transient vectors (§6.2) live beside them per role. Callers hold `Arc` clones, so evicting a version a scan is reading is safe; the cache keeps a `Weak` to an evicted version a call still holds, with its bytes, so a lookup meanwhile shares that copy rather than load a second, and the bytes stay counted until the call lets go. Loading a graph's current version drops the graph's other cached versions that the statement has not read (an explicit `version :=` reloads them), so a pooled backend holds one copy per graph after promotions. A cache miss first **prunes**: cached versions (and held copies) of versions that no longer exist under the statement's snapshot are dropped, and so are transient vectors of such versions, but only in a transaction without a transaction id (any transaction that deletes a version has one, and its deletions may roll back; transient vectors cannot be reloaded); `warren.evict` and a refused transient write prune too, and the prune also evicts the shared versions that no longer exist in the latest committed state, whatever the statement's snapshot sees (§5.6).

**`warren.max_cache_memory`** (kB, default `4GB`, minimum `64kB`, `USERSET`: it bounds only the caller's backend, and eviction costs only reloads) bounds what the private cache holds between statements: privately loaded versions (their arrays and decoded relation dictionary, from the buffers' capacities: `warren-core`'s `bytes()`; the region's header, table, padding and encoded dictionary go uncounted), cached persisted vectors, and transient vectors of every role. Shared versions are not counted (§5.6). Every lookup marks its entry used and ends by trimming the cache (which only sums its bytes while within the cap): unpinned versions and persisted vectors are evicted, first any that could never fit beside what cannot be evicted, then the least recently used (a pure policy, `cache::lru`). Transient vectors cannot be reloaded, so they are never evicted: keeping one that would take transient vectors alone past the cap is refused with 53400 before anything changes (one no larger than the vector it replaces is always kept, and the message gives the caller's role's share). Lowering the cap takes effect at the next lookup; `warren.evict` frees at once.

**The statement pin (`cache::statement`).** Every version and persisted vector a top-level statement reads or writes is pinned until the statement ends or its top-level transaction does, whichever comes first, so a statement making many `warren.*` calls (a §6.7 library run) loads each once. A private entry is pinned in the backend's statement set; a shared version is pinned in the registry (its slot's `pins`), so no backend evicts it until the statement ends, and the statement reads it again without the registry. The statement's end is a reset callback on `MessageContext` (reset before each client message is read, after an error too: each extended-protocol `Execute` is a statement, and several statements sent as one simple-protocol string are one, as for `statement_timestamp()`), and the transaction's end a callback on `TopTransactionContext`, so a `CALL` or `DO` that commits is pinned per transaction and a background worker behaves as a client. Each callback is registered at most once per life of its context and ends whatever pin is open, which can only have begun within the message or transaction ending. The end runs inside `AtCommit_Memory` at a transaction's end, so it is pure Rust: it clears the statement set, decrements the shared pins (atomics, without the registry's lock) and trims the private cache. Only at a message's end does it also release the mappings of evicted shared versions, which takes the registry's lock (§5.6). Rejected: executor-nesting hooks (§14 item 20).

**In use and idle.** A pinned private entry is *in use* while a call holds it (its `Arc` is shared beyond the cache's) and *idle* otherwise (`pagerank`'s version between its calls). Trimming evicts only unpinned entries, so an idle pinned version survives the scratch a call writes; idle pinned entries give way only under pressure, least recently used first: to a load that needs the room, and to a charge to a call's compute budget. So a statement that reads more distinct versions than the limits hold, one after another (`prewarm` over a list of graphs, a nightly `CALL` scoring every graph), works, reloading what it reads again.

**The compute budget.** `warren.max_compute_memory` (§6.2) bounds what one call holds. A call's budget (`ComputeMemory::reclaiming`, made after the call has opened its versions) starts with what the private cache holds past its cap: what it cannot evict (transient vectors, pinned entries in use, evicted versions calls hold) plus its idle pinned entries, charged as held. Making the budget evicts nothing. When a charge would fail, the budget's reclaim hook (`cache::bound`'s `IdlePins`) evicts, least recently used first, only the idle pinned entries it counted when it was made that are still cached and idle, credits exactly their bytes, and the charge is retried (it is not called when evicting them all would still leave it short). The hook touches the cache, so it runs only on the main thread (§6.9); kernels whose pool-side buffers are allocated whole reclaim ahead on the main thread (`ComputeMemory::reclaim_for`: `triangles`' adjacency, `gather`'s outbox and inbox, the structural kernels', centralities' and similarities' scratch), and `warren-core`'s tests fail any kernel that charges on the pool more than it reclaimed for. What grows on the pool unforeseeably (`gather`'s parts, a multi-relation product's row scratch) gets only the room left, and is refused with 53400 even where evicting idle entries would have made room: the price of a strict bound without routing worker charges to the main thread. A read of a vector the cache already counts is not charged again. Shared versions and vectors a call reads are charged to no call and reclaimed by none: evicting them would free no memory of the caller's.

**Admitting a private load.** Before a version is loaded privately, the bytes it adds (`warren.versions.bytes` plus the id map's second direction, `id_space` × the id type's size; one indexed read that assigns no transaction id) are checked: if what the private cache cannot evict would then exceed `warren.max_cache_memory` and `warren.max_compute_memory` together, the load is refused with 53400 before anything is read, naming both settings; idle pinned entries are evicted first, so only what is in use causes a refusal. Unpinned entries are then trimmed to the cap less the version's bytes. A version larger than the cap is held for the statement that reads it, its excess charged to the statement's calls, and reloaded by each later statement, with a `NOTICE` once per pin. **Two-version calls** (`diff`, `distance`, `vector_norm_diff`; `cache::cached_pair`) take whichever versions the private or shared cache holds first, pinning them, so a load for the partner cannot evict them; then they load the missing ones into the shared cache if it admits them; and the rest are admitted together before either is loaded privately (before M7, `diff` loaded both outside every limit: 12.8 GB, OOM-killed, for two 6.0 GB versions).

**`warren.prewarm(name, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint`** loads a version (readers only), for pooled connections, and returns its id. **`warren.evict(name) RETURNS bigint`** (§14 item 34) drops the caller's private versions, cached vectors and transient vectors of the graph, releases its mappings of the graph's shared versions and persisted vectors that no call holds, and, if the caller is a member of the graph's owner role, evicts the graph's shared versions and vectors that no statement pins (a mapped one drains until its mappers release it, §5.6); it returns how many private entries and shared versions and vectors it freed (mappings are not counted: a mapping is of a shared version, not the caller's memory). It first prunes, uncounted. Anyone may call it: without ownership it changes only the caller's backend.

**The peak bound.** A backend's private memory stays within `warren.max_cache_memory` plus `warren.max_compute_memory` (plus `warren.max_build_memory` while a `refresh` or `contract` runs in the statement, §5.4, §14 item 20), and, while a version loads, one chunk and the relation dictionary. Not bounded by any setting: a traversal's visited set and frontier, and calls running at once in one statement (a streaming set-returning function beside another call), each budgeted against the same cache. The cluster holds at most `warren.shared_cache_memory` of shared versions beside every backend's private memory (§5.6); Linux reports those pages in each mapping process's `RssShmem`, so measurements of shared memory use PSS, the cgroup's `memory.current` and `/dev/shm`. A cap below one statement's working set makes every statement reload (*M7 soak:* `pagerank` on a version 30 MB over the cap beside its scratch, evicted after every call before the pin, took more than 15 minutes instead of 32 s); for large graphs raise `warren.max_cache_memory`, or let the shared cache hold them.

M9 (0.5.0) moves loaded versions into dynamic shared memory so backends share one copy and parallel workers can see it (§5.6).

### 5.6 Shared cache (M9 design)

*Designed 2026-10-21 for M9 (0.5.0); steps 1–7 of the work breakdown below are done, the rest is design. The decisions are §14 items 26–31, and items 32–39, decided by the owner the same day as recommended.* Today every backend that reads a version holds its own copy (§5.5): 50 pooled connections reading a 1.2 GB version hold 60 GB and load it 50 times, a version larger than one backend's `warren.max_cache_memory` is reloaded by every statement (the soak's 6.0 GB b-merged case: 23 s a statement, §12), and parallel workers cannot read graphs at all (§9.3). M9 keeps one copy of a version per cluster, mapped by every backend that reads it. It changes where a loaded version lives and nothing about what it is: versions are immutable, so sharing needs no invalidation beyond eviction, and `warren-core`'s kernels and every query function's semantics are unchanged. Persisted vectors, immutable per `vector_id`, share the same way (§14 item 38, step 7). Not in M9: the background compute worker (§6.9), sharing transient vectors (they are per role, per backend and non-transactional by design, §6.2), and sharing anything mutable.

**Mechanism (§14 item 26): one DSM segment per shared version, found through a cluster registry.** A version loaded into the shared cache gets its own dynamic shared memory segment (`dsm_create`), sized exactly for its layout (below), filled by the backend that loads it straight from `warren.segments`, validated once, and then pinned (`dsm_pin_segment`) so it outlives every backend until it is evicted. Other backends attach by handle (`dsm_attach`) and pin the *mapping* to the session (`dsm_pin_mapping`), so no resource owner unmaps it at a transaction's end while an `Arc` still points into it; pg_warren detaches it itself, later (below). Reasons:
- *A version is one immutable object with one lifetime*, which is exactly a DSM segment: created whole, never written after it is published, freed whole. When it is evicted, `dsm_unpin_segment` drops the pin and the kernel frees the pages when the last backend detaches. Nothing is freed piecemeal, so nothing fragments.
- *Not `dsa`*, which §10 and the M9 brief named. A dynamic shared area is an allocator over a growing set of DSM segments, for many small objects of different lifetimes. Here it would only add costs: an allocation above 1 GB needs `DSA_ALLOC_HUGE`; memory returns to the OS only when a whole segment of the area is empty, so evicting one version among several in an area can free nothing; every pointer into it is a `dsa_pointer` translated per backend; and a version would share its segments' fate with unrelated ones. The registry (below) is small and fixed-size, so it needs no `dsa` either, and nor `dshash`.
- *Not pgGraph's sealed `memfd`* (read in pgGraph 1.2.1's source, `graph/src/persistence/snapshot_cache.rs`, `docs/contributor_guide/memory-model.mdx`, `persistence-format.mdx`). Each loading backend copies its artifact into a `memfd`, seals it against write, grow and shrink, and writes a JSON *hint* (its pid, descriptor number, inode, length) into a cache directory under an `flock`ed lock file, at most 128 hints. Another backend adopts it by opening `/proc/<pid>/fd/<fd>`, checks the seals, maps it read-only, **validates the whole artifact again**, and advertises a hint of its own, so the copy survives its creator's exit. Each held descriptor is taken from Postgres's budget (`AcquireExternalFD`). The kernel frees the object when the last descriptor and mapping go. It needs no preload, and falls back to a private copy on other platforms or when anything fails. What it lacks, for pg_warren: it is Linux-only; adoption depends on `/proc/<pid>/fd` access to another process (which container hardening such as `hidepid` or a restrictive seccomp profile can deny) and on a live advertiser; concurrent cold misses make duplicate copies (their docs say so); every adopter revalidates in O(N + M); and there is **no cluster-wide bound and no eviction**: a copy lives as long as any backend holds it, so an idle pooled backend pins it indefinitely. Crash restart leaves stale hint files, which it detects and prunes. What it gets right, and pg_warren keeps: the shared copy is immutable and validated before any typed view is formed; views hold an `Arc` of the mapping, never a borrowed pointer (its `33687d6` lesson, prior-art notes §1); and a failure to share is never an error, only a private copy. Its one real advantage is size: a `memfd` lives on the kernel's internal shmem mount, so Docker's 64 MB default `/dev/shm` does not limit it, while a DSM segment of the default `posix` type does (below). Both are the same kernel shmem pages, so mapping and page-fault costs are the same, and the "measure `memfd` against `dsa` first" of the M9 brief reduces to that limit, not to speed; it is addressed by the size check and fallback below, and a `memfd` region adapter is left open (§14 item 36).

**The registry.** A fixed-size table in shared memory: a header (format, an LWLock, a logical clock, the last claim's generation) and 128 slots, a few tens of kB. Each shared version also takes one of Postgres's DSM control slots (64 plus 5 per backend, shared with parallel query), which is why the slot count is small; the byte cap, not the slot count, is the bound that matters. A slot holds the state (`empty`, `loading`, `ready`, `draining`), the key, the segment's handle and bytes, the last-used tick, `pins` (backends whose current statement pins it), `mappers` (backends that map it), the loading backend's process (its pid and `MyStartTimestamp`, since a pid is reused once its process exits) and a generation, and a condition variable. The key is (database oid, pg_warren's `pg_extension` oid, kind, id): the kind is a version or a persisted vector (step 7), the id its `version_id` or `vector_id`; versions and vectors are per database, and their ids restart after `DROP EXTENSION` and `CREATE EXTENSION`, whose extension oid differs. The segment's own header repeats the key and the format, checked on every attach. Every operation on the registry takes the LWLock exclusive, except one: the end of a statement's pin. Each slot's `pins` is an atomic counter, incremented only under the lock and decremented without it, so that the pin's end, which runs inside `AtCommit_Memory` (below), takes no lock; admission and eviction read `pins` under the lock, and since only the lock's holder increments it, a 0 they read stays 0 until they release it. Everything else in a slot, `mappers` and the tick included, changes only under the lock. A statement pins a version once, so the lock is taken once per statement and version, and the bytes held in each state are computed from the 128 slots when admission needs them rather than kept. Where the registry lives depends on the major (§14 item 27):
- **PG 17 and 18: no preload.** `GetNamedDSMSegment("pg_warren.cache.<format>", …)` (`storage/dsm_registry.h`, PG 17+; bound in pgrx 0.19.3's `pg_sys` for 17 and 18) creates it on first use by any backend, with an init callback that sets it up and takes an LWLock tranche id (`LWLockNewTrancheId`); each backend registers the tranche name when it first attaches. A trusted install (§7) gets the shared cache with nothing configured, which is what hosted providers need.
- **PG 15 and 16: only with `shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_warren'`.** `_PG_init` sees `process_shared_preload_libraries_in_progress` and installs a `shmem_request_hook` (`RequestAddinShmemSpace`, `RequestNamedLWLockTranche`) and a `shmem_startup_hook` that places the registry with `ShmemInitStruct`. Without the preload these majors keep the backend-local cache of 0.4.0, unchanged (the fallback, below); the trusted install still works, without sharing. Rejected: carving the registry from the main segment's slack with a late `ShmemInitStruct` (undocumented headroom that a busy cluster may have used up); publishing a registry segment's handle in a file or a table (it outlives a crash restart, and a stale handle can attach an unrelated segment that reused it); a background worker that owns the registry (dynamic workers need no preload, but other backends still need a way to find it).
- The format changes only when the registry's or a segment's layout does. Backends running different library builds during an upgrade agree on it, or, after a layout change, each use their own registry; the release notes of such a change say to restart, since the old registry's pinned segments are otherwise freed only by a restart.

**Loading: one loader per version; the others wait.** `cache::cached` (§5.5) first asks the registry, after `resolve` has checked access as today (the key carries the caller's own database, so no backend can reach another database's versions):
- `ready`: attach (or reuse this backend's mapping), increment `pins` once per statement, and build the backend's view (below). No catalog read beyond `resolve`'s.
- `loading`: sleep on the slot's condition variable (`ConditionVariableTimedSleep`, one second at a time, which checks for interrupts, so `statement_timeout` and cancel work while waiting), then look again. On each timeout the waiter checks that the loader still exists (a live backend with its pid and start time, from the backend status array), as a belt to the cleanup below.
- miss: take the LWLock exclusive, run the admission check (below), claim a slot as `loading` with this backend's pid and the next generation, release the lock, create the segment (`DSM_CREATE_NULL_IF_MAXSEGMENTS`, so running out of DSM control slots is a fallback, not an error), read each segment's chunks through the existing cursor straight into its place in the segment, validate (§5.3), build the id map's second direction in place, pin the segment, mark the slot `ready` and broadcast. The waiters then attach.
- A version created by the current transaction (its `warren.versions` row's `xmin` is ours: a staged build queried before commit, §8) is loaded privately: publishing it would leave an entry no other backend can see, and nothing if the transaction rolls back.
