*Crash safety.* A loader that fails with an ERROR (a cancel, a timeout, a corrupt segment) leaves its slot `loading`: an abort callback (`RegisterXactCallback`, registered in `_PG_init`; it runs after the error, in ordinary backend context) sets it back to `empty` and broadcasts, and the unpinned segment goes with the transaction's resource owner. A FATAL exit (`pg_terminate_backend`, a lost client) runs a `before_shmem_exit` callback that does the same for any slot it was loading, and decrements every `pins` and `mappers` it holds; the compute pool's own exit callback first stops any kernel still reading a mapping (§6.9). A backend killed outright, or one that crashes, makes the postmaster reinitialise shared memory, which destroys every DSM segment and the registry with it (`dsm.c`: segments are also removed at the next startup after a postmaster crash), so there is nothing to recover. A waiter that wakes to an `empty` slot becomes the loader itself: one session's cancel or timeout never fails another's call.

**Pins, mappings and eviction (§14 item 28).**
- *Pins.* A statement pins a shared version as §5.5 pins a cached one: the first use in a statement increments the slot's `pins`, and the pin's end (the `MessageContext` and `TopTransactionContext` callbacks) decrements it without the registry's lock (the one lock-free operation, above), an atomic on the registry's mapping, which is a session mapping and so always present: pure Rust, as §5.5 requires of the commit path. A slot with `pins > 0` is never evicted by any backend, so a library run's version cannot be evicted from under it by another backend's load: there is no cross-backend eviction cliff.
- *Mappings* (item 28, decided 2026-10-22: mappings are kept across messages). A backend keeps its mapping of a `ready` slot across statements and client messages, so a statement does not attach it again and take page faults on the pages it touches (§5.6, spike results: detaching after every message would add an estimated 0.15–0.25 ms to a 0.16 ms point lookup). A mapping is released only once its slot is evicted (`draining`) and no call holds it: no statement pins it, and no call (an open cursor, a streaming set-returning function, a worker thread reading through it, §6.9) holds its `Arc` or anything read through it. The release (`dsm_detach`, then `mappers` decremented under the registry's LWLock) runs at the end of the client message, from the same `MessageContext` reset callback that ends the pin. That reset runs at the top of Postgres's main loop, between messages and outside the commit path, and `dsm_detach` reports its own failures (to unmap, to destroy) at WARNING, so it and the registry's LWLock are the only Postgres calls the message's end may make (step 1 of the work below confirms from the PG 15–18 sources that nothing in `dsm_detach` raises an ERROR in normal operation, and `LWLockAcquire` raises one only when a backend holds too many LWLocks, which pg_warren's single lock cannot cause); the `TopTransactionContext` end, which runs inside `AtCommit_Memory`, stays pure Rust as §5.5 requires. Never from a `Drop`. Postgres has no hook at the end of an arbitrary message, so an idle backend releases an evicted mapping at the end of its next message that read a graph, or at its next read of the shared cache. An idle pooled backend therefore keeps the versions it read mapped (their pages are the segment's, counted once, not the backend's own); admission drains mapped slots when it needs room (The cap, below), so they do not hold the cap forever.
- *Eviction.* An evictable slot is `ready` with `pins = 0`; eviction takes the least recently used first (`cache::lru`'s policy, reused), marks the slot `draining` and unpins its segment. A `draining` slot returns to `empty` when its `mappers` reach 0, whoever decrements them last, or to `ready` when a reader revives it first (The cap, below). Loading a graph's current version marks the graph's other unpinned shared versions as evicted first, as §5.5 drops them locally; other backends reading an older version on purpose (a REPEATABLE READ snapshot, an explicit `version :=`) reload it if they need it again. The existing prune (§5.5, at a cache miss) also evicts the shared slots of versions that no longer exist: eviction is always safe, since a version is immutable and a snapshot that still sees it reloads it. Both judge by the **latest committed state**, not by the caller's snapshot (`read_only::read_one_latest`, a read-only `SPI_execute_snapshot` under `GetLatestSnapshot()`): a slot only ever holds a committed version (its creator reads it privately until it commits), so one the latest snapshot does not see was deleted and the deletion committed, and only the latest committed `current_version` supersedes. A snapshot older than a version's commit would otherwise evict it (any session, through `warren.evict`, whatever the graph), and a REPEATABLE READ reader spanning a promotion would evict the promoted version each time it reloaded the one its snapshot sees as current. In parallel mode, where the latest snapshot cannot be taken, neither evicts anything.
- *The cap.* `warren.shared_cache_memory` bounds the bytes of `loading`, `ready` and `draining` slots together. Admission evicts unpinned `ready` slots that no backend maps, least recently used first, until the new version fits; each is empty at once. A mapped slot makes no room (evicted, it would only drain, and a draining slot still counts), so admission leaves it, as it does a pinned one. Since mappings are kept across messages (item 28), a version refused for room also drains: when evicting unmapped slots is not enough, admission marks `draining` the least recently used mapped, unpinned `ready` slots until the version would fit beside what stays (on `NoSlot`, the least recently used one), and still refuses the version, which loads privately. The drained slots empty as their mappers release them, so a later load fits; without this, versions kept mapped by idle backends would hold the cap forever. Nothing is drained if even draining every such slot would not make room. A draining slot still counts against the cap until its last mapper releases it, and idle backends release only at their next read, so a drained version's next reader would be refused for the room its own draining copy holds, and drain the next version in turn: a cascade that ends with every version private and the cap held by draining copies. So a lookup that finds its key's slot draining *revives* it (from step 5's review): the reader pins its segment again through a mapping, its own or one it attaches under the registry's lock (which keeps some mapping, and so the segment, alive: backends detach under it, and an exiting one has given its mapping back first), and the slot is `ready` again, with nothing loaded or drained. A claim that finds the key draining says `Taken`, so the caller looks again. If what is pinned, mapped, loading or draining leaves no room, or the version is larger than the cap, the version is loaded privately, as in 0.4.0, under the backend's own limits, with a `NOTICE` once per statement naming the setting. Nothing waits for room, and no new error is raised.
- *Size checks.* A segment is about the version's bytes (§12: 1.2–6.0 GB at 10M nodes). Under `dynamic_shared_memory_type = posix` (Linux's default) it is a file in `/dev/shm`, which Docker limits to 64 MB unless the container sets `--shm-size`. Postgres reserves the space with `posix_fallocate`, so a short `/dev/shm` is an ERROR (`could not resize shared memory segment …: No space left on device`, 53100), not a later SIGBUS. That ERROR cannot be caught and turned into a fallback (§11 forbids catching around Postgres calls), so admission first checks the free space of `/dev/shm` (`statvfs`) and loads privately, with a `NOTICE` naming `--shm-size`, when the segment would not fit; only a race with another process filling `/dev/shm` between check and create can still fail a call, and the next call sees the space. `sysv` segments are not limited by `/dev/shm`; `mmap` segments are files in `pg_dynshmem`, which works but is backed by disk. With `min_dynamic_shared_memory` set (a restart setting), `dsm_create` takes segments from that area of the main shared memory first, which is backed by huge pages when `huge_pages` is on; `docs/limits.md` gains a section on these settings.

**What a shared version looks like (§14 item 29).** One contiguous region per version: a header (magic, format, key, byte length, and a table of contents) followed by every array of the loaded version at a 64-byte-aligned offset: the id map in both directions, the tombstones, each CSR's offsets, targets and weights (per relation, merged, forward and reverse), and the relation dictionary in its encoded form, which each backend decodes into its own small `RelationDictionary` on attach. The arrays are in native in-memory layout, so the shared cache is enabled only on little-endian targets (x86_64 and aarch64; others load privately), and the id map's pairs become a `#[repr(C)]` struct so that their layout is fixed. The size is exact before anything is read: each segment's stored length comes from one query over `warren.segments` (`octet_length` of an external `bytea` reads its TOAST pointer, not its data), and each array's size follows from it. `warren-core` reads the arrays through a new `graph::SegmentBuffer<T>`, which is either an owned `Vec<T>` (built CSRs, compaction, tests) or a shared view: an `Arc` of a `graph::SharedSegment<T>` trait object, which keeps its memory mapped and returns its elements as a slice. `warren-core` stays free of Postgres and of `unsafe`; `pg_warren` forms the slices from the mapping (an `unsafe` block whose `SAFETY:` proof is the session-pinned mapping, detached only after its last `Arc` drops, and the layout's alignment). Kernels, which take slices, do not change. A shared view's `bytes()` are never counted by the backend-local `VersionCache` limit or by `admit_transient`: the region is the registry's to account for, and counting it there too would count it twice. A backend-local version uses the same layout in one 64-byte-aligned Rust allocation, so there is one loader and one layout, the chunks are copied straight into place in both cases (except the pairs of an `int4` id map: an `IdPair<i32>` is 16 bytes in memory, `dense` at offset 8 after 4 bytes of padding, but 12 in the segment encoding, so the loader re-lays them out pair by pair; `int8` and `uuid` pairs match their encoding), and the 64-byte alignment that §5.5 notes as not yet enforced becomes true. The loader validates once; attaching backends check the header only, since the segment was written by pg_warren itself, never by a file another process can change.

**One code path behind a port (§14 item 30).** `cache::shared` (a new submodule of `cache`, so the feature graph is unchanged) owns the registry's state machine (claim, publish, abandon, wait, pin, unpin, evict, drain) and admission as pure functions over a slot table, and defines the port `SharedVersions`, whose operations are `lookup`, `claim`, `publish`, `abort`, `wait`, `unpin`, `release`, `evict`, `forget`, `keys`, `listing` and `exit` (`forget` and `keys` from step 6, `listing` from step 7), and which hands out regions to load into and views to read. Three adapters: `Dsm` (the registry and segments above, located per major), `Memory` (an in-process fake: a slot table and heap regions shared between threads, which stand in for backends in tests), and `Unavailable` (PG 15/16 without the preload, `warren.shared_cache_memory = 0`, a big-endian target). `cache::open::cached` and `cached_pair` ask the port first and fall back to the backend-local `VersionCache` on `Unavailable`, on a refused admission or on an uncommitted version, so every query function, `warren-core` and the `LoadedVersion` API stay as they are; only where a version's bytes live differs.

**The memory model, restated (§14 item 31; §5.5 and §14 item 20 describe it as built, step 6).**
- `warren.shared_cache_memory` (new; kB; `PGC_SIGHUP`, since it bounds a cluster-wide resource and only a superuser or the provider should set it; `0` disables the shared cache; default: §14 item 32) bounds the shared versions of all databases together.
- `warren.max_cache_memory` keeps its meaning and its `USERSET` context: what this backend's *private* cache holds between statements, now the versions it loaded privately (the fallback), the persisted vectors it caches, and transient vectors. Shared versions are not counted in it.
- `warren.max_compute_memory` is unchanged. A call's budget starts with what the *private* cache holds past its cap and cannot evict, exactly as §5.5 and §14 item 20 describe. Shared versions (and persisted vectors, step 7) a call reads are counted by neither per-backend setting: they are never charged to a call and never reclaimed by one, since evicting a shared version frees no memory of the caller's.
- Idle pins and `reclaim_for` work as before, over private entries only. A statement's shared pins are not idle pins in that sense: no budget can reclaim them, and no other backend can evict them, until the statement ends.
- So each backend's private memory stays within `warren.max_cache_memory` plus `warren.max_compute_memory` (with §14 item 20's exceptions; plus `warren.max_build_memory` while a `refresh` or `contract` runs in the statement, §14 item 20), and the cluster holds at most `warren.shared_cache_memory` of shared versions beside the backends' private memory. For the soak's b-merged case, a cap of 8 GB keeps the 6.0 GB version loaded once per cluster between statements, whatever each backend's `warren.max_cache_memory`. Linux reports shared pages in every mapping process's `RssShmem` (and VmHWM), not `RssAnon`, so measurements of shared memory use PSS (`smaps_rollup`), the cgroup's `memory.current` and `/dev/shm`.
- A shared version stays until it is evicted, its version is dropped, or the cluster restarts, also when no backend uses it; an idle cluster can therefore hold up to the cap. `warren.evict` (§14 item 34) and the cap are the levers.
- The cap is shared by every database and role that can read a graph: one database's loads evict another's unpinned versions. Per-database shares are §14 item 39.

**Parallel query (§14 item 33).** A parallel worker is a process with an empty backend-local cache, so §9.3 marks every graph read `PARALLEL RESTRICTED`. With the shared cache a worker finds the version in the registry by key and attaches by handle, exactly as another backend would (the leader's statement pin keeps it there for the query); if it is not shared, the worker loads it, into the shared cache if admitted, else privately within its own limits. What else a worker needs is already in place: `access::entry` sets `search_path` with `GUC_ACTION_SAVE`, which parallel mode allows; the catalog reads go through `read_only` and assign no transaction id; a worker has a `TopTransactionContext`, so its pins end with its transaction; and its `before_shmem_exit` callback releases its `pins` and `mappers` when it exits. Two reasons for `RESTRICTED` remain and are not the cache's: transient vectors (a worker cannot see the leader's, and would silently read the persisted vector of the same name instead), and `last_stats()` (a worker's record never reaches the leader). Candidates for `PARALLEL SAFE` are therefore the graph reads that use neither: `neighbours`, `degree`, `has_edge` and `id_map` record no stats and read no vector; `info` reads only the catalog; `reachable`, `shortest_path`, `dijkstra`, `bfs`, `reachable_set`, `components` and `walk` record `last_stats()`. The vector reads, `explain_map`, `diff`, `distance`, `prewarm`, `evict` and `last_stats` itself stay `RESTRICTED`. A label is fixed in the catalog, not chosen per cluster, so a function marked `SAFE` also runs in workers where the shared cache is unavailable (PG 15/16 without the preload, a small `/dev/shm`), and each worker then loads the version privately. Which functions flip is decided in §14 item 33: all of these candidates.

**Fallback (summary).** Every way the shared cache can be missing degrades to 0.4.0's backend-local cache, with its limits and semantics, and never to an error: PG 15/16 without the preload; `warren.shared_cache_memory = 0`; a big-endian target; a version larger than the cap, or one that does not fit beside what is pinned, loading or draining; DSM control slots exhausted; `/dev/shm` too small; a version the current transaction created. A `NOTICE`, at most once per statement, says why where the cause is configuration (the cap, `/dev/shm`). Where the shared cache is unavailable though `warren.shared_cache_memory` is set, a `NOTICE` says so once per backend: *"warren.shared_cache_memory is set, but on PostgreSQL 15 and 16 the shared cache needs shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_warren': this backend loads graph versions into its own cache"* (or *"… but the shared cache works only on little-endian targets: …"*, or, on PG 17/18 where a build with another registry format created the registry, *"warren.shared_cache_memory is set, but the shared cache was created by a build of pg_warren with registry format N, not M; restart PostgreSQL to share graph versions again: this backend loads graph versions into its own cache"*). The one remaining failure is the `/dev/shm` race above.

**Testing (CLAUDE.md tiers).**
- `warren-core` unit and property tests, under Miri on main: `SegmentBuffer` (owned and shared views give equal results through every kernel; a shared view outlives its creator's handle); the existing kernel proptests run over shared views of a heap region as well as over owned buffers. A view is a typed slice, whose alignment for its type Rust guarantees, so refusing misaligned or short regions belongs to the region layout's tests (step 3), below.
- Adapter logic against the `Memory` fake, plain `#[test]` and `proptest`: the registry's state machine against a model under random interleavings of backends (claim, publish, abandon, a dropped loader, wait and take over, pin, unpin, evict, drain, exit); admission and eviction never evict a pinned slot and keep the cap; exactly one loader per key; a waiter of an abandoned load loads; the region layout round-trips a version (every array, alignment, exact size).
- `#[pg_test]` for the DSM adapter in one backend, with `postgresql_conf_options` adding the preload so PG 15/16 run it too: load, attach, header check, pin and unpin at statement end, deferred detach, fallback at a cap of `0` and below the version, an uncommitted version loading privately, the `parallel_labels` and `access::guard` tables extended to new functions.
- Multi-backend tests in `test/sql` through `dblink` (as the race tests do), with `test/run.py` gaining a cluster configuration per mode (preload; no preload; `warren.shared_cache_memory = 0`): concurrent cold misses load once (the registry's view, item 37, shows one segment and pins from each session); a loader cancelled (`pg_cancel_backend`) or terminated (`pg_terminate_backend`) mid-load is taken over by a waiter; eviction under the cap spares pinned versions and falls back when it cannot make room; a `draining` version is freed after the statements using it end; promotion, retention and `drop_version` evict; a parallel plan's workers attach (`debug_parallel_query`); and, in a separate CI job, a backend killed with SIGKILL makes the cluster restart with no segment left in `/dev/shm`. `test/upgrade.py` and `test/dump.py` run in every mode.

**Measurement plan.** `bench/pooler.py`, results in `bench/results/pooler/`:
- *The pooler scenario.* PgBouncer in transaction mode in front of N = 1, 10 and 50 server connections, each reading one graph (cases a and b-unmerged of §12, at 1M/10M and at 10M/100M where the machine allows): `neighbours` and `bfs` depth 3 from random starts under `pgbench` with a custom script, then one `pagerank`. Record the cluster's memory (cgroup `memory.current`, the sum of the backends' PSS, `/dev/shm` usage) before and after, each backend's `RssAnon`, the first call's latency on each connection (one load per cluster against one per backend), and throughput, for 0.4.0 and for M9 with the shared cache on and off. Expectation: memory about one copy plus N backends' private memory instead of N copies; cold loads 1 instead of N.
- *Concurrent cold misses.* N sessions reading an unloaded version at once: one load, and every session's latency about one load's.
- *Attach cost per message* (informed item 28's detach rule): `neighbours` over a single connection, mapping kept against detached per message, and the page faults (`perf stat -e minor-faults`) of a `pagerank` iteration reading a freshly attached version; with and without `MADV_HUGEPAGE` (item 35).
- *Parallel query:* `SELECT count(*) FROM pairs WHERE warren.has_edge('g', src, dst)` over a table of 10M pairs with 0, 2 and 4 workers, shared cache on and off.
- *The soak:* `bench/soak.py` re-run with the shared cache, b-merged at a shared cap of 8 GB and the default per-backend limits: every statement after the first should find the version loaded (§12's "loaded by every statement" rows).

**Spike results (work breakdown step 1, 2026-10-21).** Measured with a throwaway C program (not in the repository) on a development VM (4 vCPUs, 15 GiB, Linux 6.18, `/dev/shm` a 16 GB tmpfs; transparent huge pages `madvise` for anonymous memory, `never` for shmem), over regions of 1, 2 and 6 GiB, a few runs each; the source reading is of PostgreSQL 15.19, 16.15, 17.11 and 18.6.
- *`posix` DSM against `memfd`: the same,* as expected. Creating, filling, attaching and faulting agree within run-to-run noise (about ±15%), so item 26 stands.
- *`posix_fallocate` takes 72–92 ms per GB.* The loader's first write of every page after it takes 330–390 ms per GB (4 kB write faults; writes get no fault-around), against 1.0–1.7 s per GB for first-touch writes into private anonymous memory on this VM, so a load into a shared segment costs no more than a private one, and a short `/dev/shm` fails in `posix_fallocate` before anything is read.
- *Attaching costs 2–4 µs, whatever the size* (`shm_open`, `fstat`, `mmap` and `close`, and `munmap` at detach: the system calls of `dsm_attach` and `dsm_detach`). The cost is in the faults. A read fault on a shared mapping maps 64 kB (the kernel's fault-around), so 16,385 faults per GB. Touching every page of a freshly attached GB takes 30–45 ms, in order or at random; a full sequential read of it takes 142–172 ms against 90 ms warm, about 50–80 ms per GB more; `MAP_POPULATE` takes 45–50 ms per GB up front and saves nothing. A statement that reads a whole version (a `pagerank` iteration, a deep `bfs`) therefore pays about 0.05 s per GB once per message, small beside the second per GB of the load it replaces.
- *Point lookups are the exception.* On a fresh mapping each random page costs 6–9 µs to fault and unmap, page tables included: 10 pages per attach 92 µs, 30 pages 240 µs (at 1 GiB and at 6 GiB), 100 pages 620 µs, against 1.7–1.9 µs for the same 30 pages with the mapping kept. A `neighbours` call at 10M nodes touches about 20–30 distinct pages (the binary search for the external id in 160 MB of pairs, a row's offsets and targets, each neighbour's external id), so detaching after every message would add an estimated 0.15–0.25 ms to a call that takes 0.16 ms (§12). This meets item 28's condition for revisiting the detach-per-message rule. The rule is not changed here: the choice between it and keeping a `ready` slot's mapping across messages (detaching at a message's end only once the slot is `draining`, so an idle backend keeps an evicted version's memory until its next message) went to the owner, who chose keeping mappings across messages (item 28, decided 2026-10-22); step 8's pooler measurement checks it in Postgres.
- *Huge pages (item 35).* `madvise(MADV_HUGEPAGE)` does nothing on a `posix` segment unless `/dev/shm` is mounted with `huge=advise` (or `within_size`, `always`): the `transparent_hugepage/shmem_enabled` setting covers only the kernel's internal mount, used by `memfd` and `sysv` segments. With `huge=advise` and the advice, 30 random pages per attach cost 40 µs instead of 174 µs, and the fill 313 ms per GB once huge pages were free (4.2 s per GB on the first run, which compacted memory to make them). Huge pages cut the per-message cost about fourfold, but do not remove it.
- *`dsm_detach` at the end of a client message is safe* on all four majors, whose `dsm_detach` is identical. It runs the segment's `on_dsm_detach` callbacks (pg_warren registers none on a version's segment), unmaps and, for the last reference, destroys through `dsm_impl_op` at `WARNING`, holds `DynamicSharedMemoryControlLock` briefly, skips `ResourceOwnerForgetDSM` for a session-pinned mapping (`dsm_pin_mapping` cleared its resource owner), and frees its own struct. The only other raise on its path is `FreePageManagerPut`'s `FATAL` on a corrupt free-page tree, for segments carved from `min_dynamic_shared_memory`: corruption, not normal operation. `MessageContext` is reset at the top of `PostgresMain`'s loop on all four (`MemoryContextResetAndDeleteChildren` on 15 and 16, `MemoryContextReset` on 17 and 18), after error recovery has released every LWLock and before `ReadyForQuery`, so a `WARNING` from the detach reaches the client before the next prompt. At exit, `shmem_exit` runs the `before_shmem_exit` callbacks before `dsm_backend_shutdown` detaches the remaining segments, so the exit callback can still reach the registry to decrement `pins` and `mappers`.
- *Bindings.* A throwaway crate referencing each item compiles against `pgrx-pg-sys` 0.19.3 on all four majors: `dsm_create`, `dsm_attach`, `dsm_detach`, `dsm_pin_segment`, `dsm_unpin_segment`, `dsm_pin_mapping`, `dsm_segment_address`, `dsm_segment_handle`, `dsm_segment_map_length`, `dsm_find_mapping`, `on_dsm_detach`; `ConditionVariableInit`, `ConditionVariableTimedSleep`, `ConditionVariableCancelSleep`, `ConditionVariableBroadcast`; `LWLockAcquire`, `LWLockRelease`, `LWLockInitialize`, `LWLockNewTrancheId`, `LWLockRegisterTranche`, `RequestNamedLWLockTranche`, `GetNamedLWLockTranche`; `RequestAddinShmemSpace`, `ShmemInitStruct`, `shmem_request_hook`, `shmem_startup_hook`, `process_shared_preload_libraries_in_progress`; `before_shmem_exit`, `RegisterXactCallback`, `MemoryContextRegisterResetCallback`, `MessageContext`, `TopTransactionContext`, `dynamic_shared_memory_type`, `min_dynamic_shared_memory` and `DSM_CREATE_NULL_IF_MAXSEGMENTS`. `GetNamedDSMSegment` is bound on 17 and 18 only, with the same signature on both (its init callback is an `extern "C-unwind"` function). Macros are not bound: `AddinShmemInitLock` is `&MainLWLockArray[21].lock` on all four (`MainLWLockArray` is bound), and `DSM_HANDLE_INVALID` is 0.

**Work breakdown.** Each step is a pull request that leaves `main` green and behaves as 0.4.0 until step 8 turns the shared cache on by default.
1. *Spike, recorded here, not merged as code (done 2026-10-21: the spike results above):* attach and fault costs per GB of a `posix` DSM segment against a `memfd` (expected equal), `posix_fallocate` time per GB, `dsm_detach` from a `MessageContext` reset callback, and the pgrx bindings needed (`GetNamedDSMSegment` and the DSM, condition variable, LWLock and hook functions are in pgrx 0.19.3's `pg_sys` for the majors listed above).
2. *`warren-core`: `SegmentBuffer<T>`* under `Csr`, `IdMap` (pairs as a `#[repr(C)]` struct), `Tombstones` and the vectors' storage, with owned and shared views; no behaviour change; property tests over both views; Miri. *Done:* `SegmentBuffer<T>` reads as a slice either way (a shared view through one virtual call, `SharedSegment::elements`, so the adapter's `unsafe` that forms the slice from the mapping stays inside its own implementation and no slice outlives the `Arc` that keeps it mapped); a shared view's `bytes()` are the bytes it views, the region's owner accounting for the region. `Csr::from_segments`, `IdMap::from_segments` (pairs as `IdPair { external, dense }`, `#[repr(C)]`, the same size as the tuple they replace; it checks the inverse map it is given rather than building one) and `Tombstones::from_segment` take segment buffers with the same checks as their owned constructors, and `Vector::shared` reads one in place, charging nothing. Changing a shared buffer copies it first (failing rather than aborting), so `Tombstones::insert` and `remove`, `Vector::values_mut` and `clear_tombstoned` became fallible; a copied vector is charged to its budget. The traversal, gather, compute-reference and version-history property tests draw owned or shared buffers.
3. *`pg_warren`: the region layout and the loader into a region.* Backend-local versions are loaded into one aligned region by exact size; `LoadedVersion` reads it through shared views of that region. No behaviour change beyond one allocation per version and the 64-byte alignment; the soak's cold-load row is re-measured. *Done:* `cache::region` holds the layout (a 64-byte header: magic `WRRG`, format, version id, byte length, entry count; then a 32-byte entry per array naming it, with its element width, offset and count; arrays at 64-byte-aligned offsets, the region ending with the last), heap memory (`alloc_zeroed`, byte-aligned and started at its first 64-byte-aligned byte, so zeroed lazily by `calloc` rather than by a `memset` of the whole region; 53200 on failure), and the only `unsafe` that forms typed slices of it: `in_place::typed` / `typed_mut` (alignment and whole elements checked; elements are `InPlace`, any bits valid, and `IdDatum` became an `unsafe trait` saying so of external ids) and the views' `SharedSegment::elements`. `Region::open` refuses misaligned or short memory, another version or format, and entries misaligned, outside the region or naming an array twice; it reads the header only, and indexes the entries by array, so each array is found in constant time (a version has entries per relation, and a scan for each would be quadratic in them). The loader (`cache::load`: `plan`, `fill`, `views`) sizes the region from each segment's stored length before reading anything but the relation dictionary, checks each segment's header as its first chunk arrives (`warren-core`'s `SegmentKind::check_header`) and copies its chunks straight into place (`int4` id-map pairs, and every pair on a big-endian target, re-laid-out pair by pair as they stream), builds the id map's second direction in place, and validates once through `IdMap::from_segments`, `Tombstones::from_segment`, `Csr::from_segments`, `GraphCsr::from_parts` and `GraphVersion::new`. `LoadedVersion` and its `bytes()` are unchanged (the region's header, table, padding and encoded relation dictionary go uncounted). Measured (case a at `--scale 10`, three alternating runs each, `bench/results/soak/README.md`): the cold load 0.19 → 0.16 s, its private peak 165 → 127 MiB for a 120 MB version, every other stage unchanged; zeroing the region lazily (*PR #41 review*: `calloc`, not a 64-byte-aligned `memset` of the whole region) then took the cold load from 0.155 to 0.136 s (medians of three alternating runs), its peak unchanged. Every access to a shared view is a virtual call, which made `pagerank` 25–30% slower until the product kernels borrowed each CSR's arrays once (`CsrSlices`); kernels that read rows through `Csr`'s or `CsrRows`' methods still pay a few calls a row (traversal and `components` measured within noise).
4. *`cache::shared`: the port, the registry's state machine and admission, and the `Memory` fake*, with the model tests. Not wired to Postgres. *Done:* `cache::shared` is compiled for tests only until step 5 wires it. `slot` (the key, the four states, a `Ticket` of slot index and claim generation, so that a transition on a slot since emptied and claimed again does nothing); `admission` (pure: `Disabled` at a cap of 0, `TooLarge`, `NoRoom`, `NoSlot` when no slot is empty or reclaimable, `StoreFull` when the region would not fit in the store's free bytes plus those its evictions free; every refusal is a private load, and a refusal changes nothing); `registry`, the state machine over any `SlotTable` (an array in shared memory, a `Vec` in the fake): `lookup`, `claim` (rechecks the key, admits, evicts, stamps the next generation and the loader's process: its pid and start time, a counter in the fake, which reuses pids as an operating system does), `publish` (ready, pinned once and mapped once by the loader, so nothing can evict it before the loader's statement pins it), `abandon` (by the loader's abort or exit callback, or by a waiter whose loader is gone, no live backend having its pid and start time: the take-over), `pin`, `unpin`, `handle` and `attach` (a backend reads a ready slot's handle, attaches, and only then counts itself a mapper, so an attach that raises an ERROR counts nothing and leaves nothing to give back), `release` (the last mapper of a draining slot empties it) and `evict` (unpinned `ready` slots by key: unmapped ones empty at once, mapped ones drain). Every transition runs under the registry's lock held exclusively except a pin's end, as above: the pins are a `PinTable` of atomic counters beside the slot table, which `pin` and `publish` increment under the lock, `registry::unpin` decrements without it (`Session::unpin` and the port's `unpin` take only the pin table; the fake's never touches its `Mutex`), and `admit` and `evict` read under it. `session` is a backend's side, kept equal to the registry's counts: the slots its statement pins, its mappings (each an `Arc`, so one a call holds is never released) and its claims; `release` releases the unheld mappings of draining slots (item 28's detach rule; until the owner decided it, step 4 also built `Release::Unheld`, every unheld mapping, removed once the rule was decided), and `exit` gives back everything. The port `SharedVersions` (`lookup`, `claim`, `publish`, `abort`, `wait`, `unpin`, `release`, `evict`, `exit`) and `acquire`, the protocol over it (look up; wait while loading; claim, load, publish; a refusal is `Private`). A failed load returns its error and drops its region, as an ERROR unwinds through `acquire` in a backend; nothing in `acquire` gives the claim up, since nothing after the ERROR runs there: the transaction's abort callback calls `abort`, which gives up every slot the backend is loading (`Session::abort`) and keeps its mappings, its pins ending with the statement as usual. There is no explicit abandon in the port. `Memory`, the fake: a registry in a `Mutex`, a `Condvar` broadcast when a slot leaves `loading`, heap regions freed when their last `Arc` drops (the registry's, standing in for the segment's pin, or a backend's mapping), each backend reading a region through its own `Mapping`, as each backend maps a segment itself, so that a session's count of a mapping's holders counts its own calls alone, and backends on threads. Tests: unit tests per module; threaded tests (concurrent misses load once; a waiter takes over an aborted load, and that of a loader that vanished; a statement's unpin completes while another backend holds the lock); and a `proptest` model test of random interleavings of three backends (read, wait and take over, publish, an ERROR in a load or in an attach followed by the abort callback, end a statement, drop what calls hold, release either way, evict, exit, and vanish without the exit callback, a new backend possibly reusing its pid) checking after every step that the cap holds, that no pinned slot is evicted, that a version has at most one loading or ready slot, that every loading slot has a loader, live or vanished, and that a wait takes over exactly the loads of vanished loaders, that each slot's pins and mappers are what the backends hold, that no slot drains with no mapper, that readers see their version's bytes, and that refusals match a simple model of admission; at the end of each case, once every live backend has ended its work and released what it holds (evicted mappings first), that no slot drains and every ready slot that no vanished backend holds is reclaimable; and, after every backend exits and everything is evicted, that every region is freed.
5. *The `Dsm` adapter*, behind `warren.shared_cache_memory` with a default of `0` (off): the registry on PG 17/18 through `GetNamedDSMSegment` and on PG 15/16 through the preload hooks; load, wait and take over; pins and the deferred detach; the abort and exit callbacks; the `/dev/shm` check; the `pg_test`s, and `test/run.py`'s modes with the multi-backend tests. CI runs the suite in each mode on PG 15–18. *Done:* `cache::shared` is compiled for every build. `shared::setting` defines `warren.shared_cache_memory` (kB, `PGC_SIGHUP`, default `0`); `shared::read` is `cache::open`'s entry (a miss in the private cache asks it, then loads privately if it answers `None`; shared versions are not counted in `VersionCache` or `admit_transient`); `shared::unavailable` is the reason the port cannot open (the PG 15/16 preload missing, a big-endian target), with its `NOTICE` (Fallback, above), so `Unavailable` is not an adapter of the port but the case where `cache::open` skips it; `shared::dsm` is the `Dsm` adapter: `placement` (the registry: `#[repr(C)]` header, lock, clocks, 128 slots, a pin counter and a condition variable per slot; a named DSM segment on PG 17/18, `ShmemInitStruct` under the preload hooks on PG 15/16, with `AddinShmemInitLock` taken from `MainLWLockArray`, as its C macro does), `segment` (one DSM segment per version: a 64-byte prefix, magic `WRSV`, the registry's format, the key, checked on every attach, then the region; `DSM_CREATE_NULL_IF_MAXSEGMENTS` as `Refusal::NoSegment`; the store's free bytes by `statvfs` of `/dev/shm`, or of `pg_dynshmem` for `mmap`, 0 when unreadable, so a private load) and `callbacks` (the transaction's and a subtransaction's abort call `abort`; `before_shmem_exit` calls `exit`). Statement pins end lock-free with the statement (`cache::statement`), and the message's end (its `MessageContext` reset callback) releases evicted mappings. Tests: `#[pg_test]`s with the preload, among them (off by default; an uncommitted version loads privately; loaded once and shared by later statements; larger than the cap is private; a failed load gives its slot up at the abort; an evicted version is released at the message's end), and `test/run.py --shared-cache off|preload|on` with four `dblink` tests (`shared_cache_concurrent_misses`, `shared_cache_eviction`, `shared_cache_loader_killed`, and from the review `shared_cache_exit_mid_kernel`) that count a version's segments in `/dev/shm` by their prefix; CI runs them in each mode on PG 15–18, and the whole suite preloaded on PG 16 (`cache_statement_pin`, which tests the private cache's pin, skipped itself where versions are shared until step 6). *Deviations:* item 28's detach rule is built as keep-across-messages (above; the owner decided it 2026-10-22), with admission draining mapped slots (The cap, above); a loader is live while a backend has its pid (`BackendPidGetProc`): its start time is recorded but not compared, since Postgres exports no reader for another backend's start time on every major; a backend validates a version fully once per mapping, not only the header, since `warren-core` has no unchecked constructors, and keeps the result with the mapping; at exit, mappings are left to Postgres's own detach of every segment (`dsm_backend_shutdown`, after `before_shmem_exit`); a cap of `0` skips the shared cache entirely, leaving shared versions until a restart (step 6's `warren.evict` frees them); `GetNamedDSMSegment` can raise an ERROR at a backend's first use when no DSM control slot is free (PG 17/18); a broken invariant of the registry (a ready slot's segment missing) is an internal ERROR. Still for step 6: `warren.evict`, prune and promotion against the registry. *Review fixes:* a FATAL during a kernel stops the compute pool before Postgres unmaps segments (§6.9); a mapping is in use while anything read through it is held, a piece cloned out of a loaded version included (the mapping keeps a weak reference to the region, and drops its own loaded version when no call holds it); mappings are released one at a time, each counted gone before its `dsm_detach`, so an ERROR there leaves the others held and counted; a mapping the registry refuses is detached before the internal ERROR; a draining version read again is revived (The cap, above); a read with `warren.shared_cache_memory` set to `0` by a reload, in a backend that has used the shared cache, evicts every unpinned shared version and releases its own evicted mappings, once per statement, so the memory comes back as backends next read; a version its statement already pins is read again without the registry (per-row calls take no lock), and the release at a message's start takes the lock only if a slot has started draining since the backend's last release (a drain count in the registry, read as an atomic) or that release left one held; the registry's format is 2; a registry of another format is its own unavailability, with its own `NOTICE` (Fallback, above). A `#[pg_test]` claims a slot for a process no live backend has and checks that a reader takes the load over (`BackendPidGetProc`). Measured on the development VM (4 vCPUs; PG 16, preloaded, a 1M-node ring, `warren.degree`): one statement of 200,000 calls takes 22–23 s with the shared cache and with it off, before and after the fast path, so the per-call cost is elsewhere (resolving the graph); `pgbench`, one call per transaction, gives 16,800 → 16,400 tps with 4 clients (19,300–20,000 with the shared cache off) and 3,060 → 3,070 tps with 1: within noise, the lock not being contended at this scale.
6. *The memory model:* the compute budget and reclaim over private entries only; the statement pin over shared entries; prune, promotion and `cached_pair` against the registry; `warren.evict` (item 34); §5.5 and §14 item 20 rewritten to describe the code as it is. *Done:* §5.5 and §14 item 20 now describe the code as it is. `warren.evict` (item 34) checks ownership as `access::is_owner` (membership of the owner role, as `require_owner`); a non-owner neither evicts nor opens the registry. It releases the caller's unheld mappings of the graph's versions even while its statement pins them (as it drops pinned private entries); its count is private entries plus shared versions evicted, not mappings. The graph's versions are those the statement's snapshot sees (the key has no graph id, so the registry's format is unchanged); others are the prune's. The prune (`cache::shared::eviction::prune`) runs at a private miss, at a shared miss before the claim (so its evictions make room for the load), and at `warren.evict`; it evicts the shared versions of this database that the latest committed state does not have, and every one of an earlier installation (another extension oid), which nothing could read again. *Fixed in review (PR #43):* the prune first judged existence under the caller's snapshot, so a snapshot older than a version's commit (a long REPEATABLE READ transaction, of any role that had used the shared cache, through `warren.evict` with no ownership needed) evicted every unpinned shared version committed since, across graphs; it now reads the latest committed state (`read_only::read_one_latest`; `shared_cache_snapshot_prune`, red before). Promotion: a shared miss of a graph's current version, current in the latest committed state (not the caller's snapshot), first evicts the graph's other unpinned shared versions and releases this backend's mappings of them (the loader's own mapping of the old version goes at once; others' drain). Nothing is stale after a promotion: the key is the version, resolved per call. *Fixed in review (PR #43):* "current" was the caller's snapshot's, so a REPEATABLE READ reader spanning a promotion, reloading the version its snapshot saw as current, evicted the promoted one (staged before its snapshot, so the snapshot saw it), and fresh and stale readers took turns reloading; a stale reader's load is now an ordinary admission (`shared_cache_stale_reader`, red before). `cached_pair` finds both versions first (`acquire::find`: a lookup that waits for a load but never claims), then loads the missing ones into the shared cache, then admits the rest together before loading them privately; before, the first version's claim could evict the second to make room and load it again (`shared_cache_pair`, red before). The compute budget and `IdlePins` already counted the private cache alone, since shared versions never enter it; a `#[pg_test]` pins that down. Found on the way: the key's `get_extension_oid` is an index scan of `pg_extension` per statement, now cached per backend until a `pg_namespace` change is processed (`cache::installation`); and the *private* caches served the old installation's versions after `DROP EXTENSION` and `CREATE EXTENSION`, since version ids restart (0.4.0 too): `resolve` and `warren.evict` now drop them when the installation changes. Tests: plain `#[test]`s for `Session::forget`, `eviction` (owner and reader, prune) and `find` against the `Memory` fake, and `forget` in the model test; `#[pg_test]`s for `warren.evict` by an owner and another role, the prune at a private and a shared miss, promotion (evicts, spares a pinned version, not for another version), the budget, and the installation cache (the shared cache's `#[pg_test]`s now hold each other off with an advisory lock: their versions are uncommitted, and another test's prune, whose snapshot does not see them, evicted them); `test/sql`: `shared_cache_pair`, `shared_cache_versions` (promotion, `drop_version`, `warren.evict` by owner and stranger across backends), `shared_cache_statement_pin` (the pin over shared versions: in a statement, a transaction block, an error, a `CALL` that commits; a library run reads its shared version without loading it) and `shared_cache_reinstall`, all in every mode; `cache_statement_pin` turns the shared cache off while it runs and so runs in every mode too, and CI runs it in each. The shared-cache tests that asked what a backend's own cache holds now ask as a role that owns nothing (`own_evictions`). Measured (development VM, 4 vCPUs; PG 16, preloaded, cap 1 GB, a 1M-node ring, `warren.degree`, release builds alternated): caching the extension oid saves about 2 µs a call (a 200,000-call statement 0.3–0.5 s faster, 1–3%), so it is not what made the shared path slower; several 200,000-call statements in one connection take 14.1–14.5 s shared and 14.4–14.5 s off; a backend's first read costs 19–27 ms shared (attach and validate) against 49–53 ms off (a private load); `pgbench`, one call per transaction, gives 6,350–6,540 tps shared against 6,300–6,600 off with 1 client, and 31,200–33,000 against 34,400–36,000 with 4 (5–10% lower, within reach of the noise; the registry's lock taken once a statement is the suspect). *Open:* some backends run the same 200,000-call statement in 17.4–18.7 s shared, whatever came before in the connection, and none off; unexplained (a mapping's page size or placement is the suspect), for step 8's measurements.
7. *Introspection (item 37) and persisted vectors (item 38)*: the same registry with a kind per slot. *Done:* the key names its kind (`slot::Kind`: `Version`, `Vector`) and id; the segment prefix records the kind at bytes 24..28; the slot layout changed, so the registry's format is 3 (`pg_warren.cache.3`; no release had a registry, so no release note needs to say to restart). *Persisted vectors:* a read that misses the backend's own vector cache (`compute_sql::Vectors::find`) asks the shared cache (`shared::read::shared_vector`) before loading privately; a miss there loads the vector into a region of its own (`cache::load::vector`: the region layout with one array, `Array::Values`, of the elements in native layout, the header's id the `vector_id`), filled from `warren.vector_chunks` (chunks numbered `0..n` and the exact byte count checked, as decoding checks them) and read in place as a `Vector::shared` view, charged to nothing. Admission, pins, mappings, draining, revival, the cap and the `NOTICE`s are the versions', through the same `acquire` and port; the mapping keeps the vector as it keeps a loaded version. A shared vector is charged to no call's budget, like a shared version; one the backend's private cache already holds is read from there first. Read privately, as before: a vector the current transaction wrote (its `warren.vectors` row's `xmin` is ours), transient vectors, and *`bool` vectors* (*deviation, conservative:* a region's arrays are read in place, where any bytes must be a valid element; a `bool` is valid only as 0 or 1, so sharing one would need a check of every byte at every mapping and a second kind of in-place element; left out). The prune also evicts shared vectors whose rows the latest committed state lacks (dropped, replaced, or cascaded with their version or graph; `eviction::prune` asks per kind); `warren.evict` by an owner evicts the graph's shared vectors with its versions (the key carries no graph, so the graph's vectors are read from `warren.vectors` under the statement's snapshot); `drop_vector` and a write that replaces a persisted vector evict the old one at once (`cache::forget_vector`; the caller is its creator or a member of the owner role, checked before the delete; a rollback costs a reload). Loading a graph's current version does not evict its other versions' vectors (two-version calls read them on purpose; the prune frees them once their version goes). *Introspection:* `warren.shared_cache()` (`shared::introspection`, `PARALLEL SAFE`, `VOLATILE`) returns `(kind, graph, version, vector, vector_id, state, bytes, pins, mappers, last_use, cap)`: a row per slot of this database's installation (`kind` `version` or `vector`; a vector's `version` is the one it is bound to; `last_use` is the registry's logical clock at the slot's last pin, not a time), then one row of `kind` `cluster`: the bytes, pins and mappers of every slot in the cluster (all databases), the clock now and the cap in bytes. The port gained `listing`, a copy of the slots in use and the clock, taken under the lock. *Privacy (conservative):* an entry is listed only if its version's or vector's row exists in the statement's snapshot and the caller may read its graph (a member of the owner role or of a reader role, `access::is_reader`); others' graphs, versions and vectors are not listed (a superuser may read every graph, so sees every existing one); dropped ones awaiting the prune and earlier installations' are listed to no one, superusers included. The totals row is every role's: it reveals only sizes and counts, which the cap and admission's `NOTICE`s already let any reader infer, and it is what an operator sizing the cap needs; per-database totals (item 39) are not shown, since they would reveal other databases' activity. With the cap at 0 the registry is read only by a backend that has used it (on PG 17 and 18 a look would create it), so the view then shows only the totals row, as where the shared cache is unavailable. Tests: unit tests of the vector region (every shared dtype round-trips; a missing chunk or wrong length; another vector's region; no region for `bool`), the registry (a version and a vector of the same id are held apart), the prune per kind and the view's rows (privacy, other databases and installations, totals) against the `Memory` fake; the model test's entries alternate versions and vectors that share ids; `#[pg_test]`s (a vector loaded once, shared by later statements and charged to no call; uncommitted and `bool` vectors private; drop, replace, `warren.evict` and the prune evict; the view's rows, pins and privacy across roles); `test/sql`: `shared_cache_vectors` and `shared_cache_view` across backends in every mode, and `shared_cache_concurrent_misses` reads the registry through the view besides counting segments in `/dev/shm`, which the other tests keep doing (the view does not list what the prune has yet to evict, which those tests check is freed).
8. *Measure and turn on:* `bench/pooler.py` and the soak re-run, results recorded in §12; the default of item 32; `docs/limits.md` (sizing, `/dev/shm`, `--shm-size`, `min_dynamic_shared_memory`, the preload on PG 15/16), README and CONTRIBUTING.
9. *Parallel labels* (item 33): the labels decided, the `parallel_labels` table and its tests under `debug_parallel_query`, and `last_stats()` recording only in the leader; the update script from the latest release.
10. *Release 0.5.0:* the install script, the update script from the previous release, `sql/released.sha256`, and `test/upgrade.py` from every release.

## 6. Compute layer

This is what turns `pg_warren` from a traversal index into a platform for whole-graph analytics. Its governing principle is **SQL all the way down**: there is no vertex-program language, no runner, and no control-flow DSL. Control flow is written in whatever procedural language the user already has (PL/pgSQL, PL/Python); the engine provides whole-graph primitives that each cost one SQL call per superstep; and the one place a per-row expression is needed accepts SQL expression syntax. The engine is a set of fast kernels behind ordinary functions, nothing more.

The justification: once every operation is a whole-vector primitive, a procedural loop costs microseconds per *superstep* (one C-level call), while the superstep itself costs tens to hundreds of milliseconds at 10M nodes. The loop is free. What is *not* free is per-row work that leaves the engine, and the compute model below is organised around how much of that each kind of program needs.

### 6.1 Three tiers of expressiveness

| Tier | Vertex state | Messages & combine | Apply | Speed at 10M nodes | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1 — numeric, in-engine** | named vectors (fixed-width numeric) | semiring SpMV | `vector_map` (SQL expression, compiled) | ms per superstep | v1 |
| **2 — gather in-engine, apply in SQL** | any table (text, arrays, jsonb, composites) | `gather`: engine fan-out over CSR, fixed set of combiners incl. `collect` | any SQL | seconds per superstep | v1 |
| **3 — WASM** | bytes | user-supplied combine and compute in a sandboxed module | user code in the engine loop | ~10–50× slower than tier 1 | v1.2+ |

Tier 1 covers the linear-algebra family: PageRank, components, label propagation, SSSP, scores, embedding propagation, spectral methods. Tier 2 is general Pregel with SQL as the vertex language: the expensive part of any Pregel program is the M edge operations, and those stay in CSR; the N per-vertex applies run as set-based SQL with the full type system. Tier 3 exists for state that must be per-vertex and bytes-shaped, and is expected to be rare once §6.6 exists.

Two engine-level decisions keep the tiers open and are **non-negotiable from M1**:

- `warren-core` message buffers are generic over a `Message` trait, with fixed-width numeric as the fast path and variable-width `(offsets, bytes)` as the general path. An `f64`-only core would force a rewrite to reach tiers 2 and 3. **Implemented (M4):** `compute::Message` is implemented by every vector element type (stored as a plain array) and by `Bytes` (packed as end offsets plus bytes); `MessageBuffer<M>` is append-only; before each reservation it charges the capacity it is about to reserve, so its charge is always its reserved capacity. **M5:** `Message::KIND` names the kind (an element dtype or bytes) for combiner type errors.
- The gather kernel accepts messages from, and returns results to, SQL relations as well as named vectors. Named vectors are an optimisation for tier 1, not the only interface.

### 6.2 Vectors and matrices

A `warren.vector` is a dense array of length `id_space`, indexed by dense ID, bound to a graph version. `width > 1` makes it a row-major N×d matrix — the natural home for a pgvector embedding column pulled into graph-aligned memory.

```sql
-- materialise from a query (via id_map); missing nodes get fill
warren.vector_from_query(graph text, name text,
                         query text,        -- SELECT <id>, <value or float[]> ...
                         dtype text DEFAULT 'f64', fill float8 DEFAULT 0,
                         version bigint DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;  -- vector_id

warren.vector_const(graph text, name text, value float8, dtype text DEFAULT 'f64') RETURNS bigint;
warren.vector_degree(graph text, name text, direction text DEFAULT 'out', rels text[] DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;
warren.vector_dense_id(graph text, name text) RETURNS bigint;   -- each node's own dense ID; seeds label propagation

-- read back
warren.vector_values(graph text, name text, version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, value float8);              -- width=1
warren.matrix_rows(graph text, name text, version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, row float8[]);              -- width>1; cast to vector for pgvector
warren.vector_topk(graph text, name text, k int, descending bool DEFAULT true)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, value float8);
warren.vector_stats(graph text, name text) RETURNS jsonb;      -- min/max/mean/sd/l2/nnz
warren.id_map(graph text, version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, dense_id bigint, live bool);

warren.drop_vector(graph text, name text, version bigint DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS void;
```

Vectors are persisted in `warren.vectors` / `vector_chunks` so results survive the session and can be materialised into user tables with ordinary `INSERT ... SELECT`. Storage cost: 8 bytes × id_space per f64 vector; 80 MB for 10M nodes.

Decisions made in M4 (`warren-core::compute`):
- `dtype` is `f32 | f64 | i64 | bool` (SQL `real`, `double precision`, `bigint`, `boolean`). A vector holds `id_space × width` elements, row-major, `width ≥ 1`.
- Every vector and kernel work buffer of graph size is charged to a `ComputeMemory` budget (the domain side of `warren.max_compute_memory`) before it is reserved with `try_reserve`; a charge past the limit is an error and charges nothing. The one exception is `Vector::from_values`, which adopts a buffer the caller has already allocated (a vector loaded from `warren.vector_chunks`) and charges its capacity afterwards. Per-task scratch bounded by the expression size or the vector width (at most a few thousand elements per thread) is not charged.

Decisions made in M4 (`pg_warren::vector`, `pg_warren::compute_sql`):
- **Signatures as implemented.** `vector_from_query(graph, name, query, dtype DEFAULT 'f64', fill DEFAULT 0, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint`; `vector_const(graph, name, value, dtype DEFAULT 'f64', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint`; `vector_degree(graph, name, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels DEFAULT NULL, dtype DEFAULT 'f64', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint`; `vector_dense_id(graph, name, dtype DEFAULT 'i64', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint`; `vector_values(graph, name, id_type anyelement, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, value float8)`; `matrix_rows(graph, name, id_type anyelement, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, row float8[])`; `vector_topk(graph, name, k int, id_type anyelement, descending DEFAULT true, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, value float8)`; `vector_stats(graph, name, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS jsonb`; `id_map(graph, id_type anyelement, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, dense_id bigint, live bool)`; `drop_vector(graph, name, version DEFAULT NULL, if_exists DEFAULT false) RETURNS void`. The functions returning external ids have no id argument to take the result type from, so, like `components` (§9.3), they take a type witness: `warren.vector_values('fraud', 'score', NULL::uuid)`. Every compute function takes a trailing `version DEFAULT NULL` (§7): vector names resolve among the vectors bound to that version, and vectors written are bound to it.
- **Persistence.** A vector's bytes are its elements in row-major order, little-endian (`bool` one byte, 0 or 1), with no header (dtype, width and length are `warren.vectors` columns), chunked like segments (`CHUNK_SIZE`); encoding streams chunk by chunk and decoding reserves the whole buffer up front (`compute::encode`, `compute::Decoder`), so persistence never holds a second copy. Every write is a new `warren.vectors` row; writing a name again replaces the old row, and the replaced vector's chunks cascade. A vector is written on every call that produces it: simple and transactional, at the cost of WAL per superstep (an unlogged or deferred write path is a later optimisation, to be measured).
- **Transient vectors (decided in M4 review, §14 item 18).** Every vector-writing function (`vector_from_query`, `vector_const`, `vector_degree`, `vector_dense_id`, `spmv`/`spmm`'s `y`, `vector_map`'s `y`) takes `persist bool DEFAULT true`, just before `version`. With `persist := false` the result is a *transient* vector: kept only in this backend's memory, keyed by (graph, version, **current role**, name), so roles sharing a backend (`SET ROLE`, a connection pooler, a `SECURITY DEFINER` function) can neither see, shadow, replace nor drop each other's (decided in M4 re-review; `warren.evict` frees only the current role's), never written to `warren.vectors` (no WAL, no TOAST, no vacuum), and it returns NULL instead of a vector id. Reads resolve a name to the current role's transient vector first, then the persisted one, in every function (`vector_values`, `vector_map`'s columns, `explain_map`, `spmv` operands, …). Writing a persisted name drops this session's transient vector of that name, and writing a transient name deletes the persisted vector of that name (under the §7 ownership rule); `drop_vector` drops the transient one if there is one. Transient vectors are **not transactional**: a ROLLBACK (or a failed subtransaction) neither removes one written in it nor restores what it replaced, and a persisted vector it deleted comes back while the transient one remains, which then shadows it. They do not pin their version against retention (§7), are invisible to other sessions, and are freed at session end, by `warren.evict` (which counts them), by `drop_vector`, and when a later cache load finds their version dropped. Between calls they count, with cached versions and vectors, against the backend's `warren.max_cache_memory` (*M7*, §5.5); unlike those they are never evicted, since they cannot be reloaded, and writing one that would take transient vectors alone past the cap is refused (53400). Roles sharing a backend share the cap: every role's transient vectors count against it, though each role sees and frees only its own. Each call that reads one is charged for it. Iterative programs keep scratch vectors transient and persist only results; the §6.7 library does so.
- **Tombstoned rows hold zero** (`false`) in every vector, whatever produced it (fill, constants, kernel results), and the rows read back, ranked or summarised are the live nodes only: `vector_values`, `matrix_rows`, `vector_topk`, `vector_stats` and `vector_reduce` skip tombstones (`compute::reduce_live`, `compute::summarise`, `compute::top_k`), so a deleted node is never a minimum. `vector_norm_diff` reads every row, so a node live on one side and tombstoned on the other contributes its value.
- **`vector_from_query`** runs the query as the caller, under the caller's `search_path`, as a read-only cursor in Postgres's read-only transaction state (*M5:* so a VOLATILE function it calls cannot write either, 25006; an integrity guard, not a sandbox, §7), wrapped as `SELECT q.id::<id_type>, q.value::<dtype's type>[[]] FROM (<query>) q(id, value)`. It is first prepared (`SPI_prepare`: parsed, analysed, rewritten; not planned or run) to read its column types, and must be one SELECT, VALUES or TABLE (`syntax_error` otherwise) of two columns whose first casts *implicitly* to the graph's id type (`datatype_mismatch` otherwise), so ids are never rounded: `int` ids work on a `bigint` graph, `double precision` ids do not. Values convert by Postgres's own `::` casts (`2.5` is 3 in an `i64` vector, as in SQL). An array value makes a matrix row; every row must have the same width (an empty query gives width 1). Nodes without a row get `fill` (cast as SQL casts `double precision`); NULL, unknown and tombstoned ids are skipped; two rows for one node are a `cardinality_violation`; a NULL value follows `vector_map`'s rule (§6.3: `NaN` in a float vector, 22004 otherwise). *M7:* rows are read a batch at a time (`source::for_each_tuple`), each batch in a memory context reset after it, and a matrix row's array is read in place (detoasted into that context only when it must be), so the read holds no Postgres memory per row beyond one batch; before, pgrx's typed getter copied every row's array into `TopTransactionContext` until the transaction ended (16 MiB for 20,000 rows of 64 `f64`).
- `vector_degree` counts distinct neighbours, as `warren.degree` does, and defaults to `f64` so `pr / NULLIF(deg, 0)` and `1.0 / deg` stay in `double precision`; `vector_dense_id` defaults to `i64`.
- `vector_stats` returns `{count, min, max, mean, sd, l2, nnz}` over the elements of live rows; `sd` is the sample standard deviation (as `stddev`), combined pairwise (Chan et al.), so it can differ from `stddev` in the last bit; undefined statistics are `null`, and non-finite ones the text `double precision` reads (`"NaN"`, `"Infinity"`), since JSON has no such numbers.
- **Access.** Functions that write vectors (`vector_*` constructors, `spmv`, `spmm`, `vector_map`) need reader access, like every compute function. Replacing or dropping a persisted vector is subject to the ownership rule of §7 (creator or owner). A write records `created_by`, the writing role (§5.1 `warren.vectors`).
- **Memory accounting.** `warren.max_compute_memory` (kB, default `4GB`, minimum `64kB`, `USERSET`) bounds what one call holds: each vector it reads into the cache is charged to the call's budget once (*M7, PR #16 review:* a vector the cache already holds, transient or cached, is memory the cache counts, so reading it is not charged again), and the call holds it, so a vector read twice in one call is loaded once even if the cache evicts it in between, as are the vectors and work buffers it allocates; going over is `configuration_limit_exceeded` (53400) before allocating, and allocator failure is `out_of_memory` (53200). Cached vectors between calls are not charged to any call's budget; the cache as a whole is bounded by `warren.max_cache_memory` (§5.5). *M7:* what the cache holds past that cap and cannot evict (transient vectors, the pinned versions and vectors calls are using, and evicted versions calls still hold) is charged to each call's budget from the start (`ComputeMemory::reclaiming`), as are the pinned versions and vectors no call is using, which give way, least recently used first, when a charge on the main thread needs their room (§5.5), and a version load that would take it past `warren.max_compute_memory` is refused with 53400, so the two settings together bound a backend's peak (§5.5, §14 item 20).

### 6.3 Tier 1 — semiring operations and `vector_map`

```sql
-- y = A ⊗ x over a semiring. A is the graph (optionally transposed / relation-masked / weighted).
warren.spmv(graph text, x text, y text,
            semiring text DEFAULT 'plus_times',   -- <add>_<mult>, GraphBLAS names; see below
            transpose bool DEFAULT false,
            rels text[] DEFAULT NULL,
            weighted bool DEFAULT NULL,           -- NULL = use weights if present
            mask text DEFAULT NULL, mask_complement bool DEFAULT false,
            replace bool DEFAULT false,           -- with a mask: false keeps y outside the mask; true sets it to the ⊕ identity
            accumulate text DEFAULT NULL)        -- NULL = overwrite y; else a binary op: y = y ⊙ result
                                                 --   plus | min | max | times | first | second
  RETURNS void;

-- Y = A ⊗ X for a width-d matrix X (neighbour aggregation of embeddings is one call)
warren.spmm(graph text, x text, y text, semiring text DEFAULT 'plus_times', ...) RETURNS void;

-- elementwise: y = <SQL expression over vector names and literals>, evaluated per node
warren.vector_map(graph text, y text, expr text) RETURNS void;
--   e.g. warren.vector_map('kg', 'pr', '0.15 + 0.85 * pr_next / NULLIF(deg, 0)')
--        warren.vector_map('kg', 'live', 'CASE WHEN is_account = 1 OR deg <= 50 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END')
warren.vector_reduce(graph text, x text, op text) RETURNS float8;   -- sum | max | min | l1 | l2 | nnz
warren.vector_norm_diff(graph text, a text, b text, norm text DEFAULT 'l2') RETURNS float8;
```

**`vector_map` accepts SQL expression syntax and nothing else.** The engine parses `expr` with Postgres's own parser against a synthetic row whose columns are the graph's vector names. If the expression lies in the *compiled subset* — arithmetic, comparisons, boolean operators, `CASE`, `NULLIF`, `COALESCE`, `LEAST`/`GREATEST`, and a whitelist of math functions (`abs`, `sqrt`, `exp`, `ln`, `log`, `power`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round`, `sign`) over numeric vectors and literals — it is compiled to a native closure and run across threads at tier-1 speed. Otherwise it is evaluated row-wise through the executor's expression machinery (`ExecEvalExpr`, JIT where available), roughly 100–500 ns per row: slower, but still correct and still SQL. The planner-visible contract is one language everywhere with graceful degradation, never "unsupported syntax". `EXPLAIN`-style introspection (`warren.explain_map(expr)`) reports which path an expression takes.

**Naming follows the GraphBLAS C API** (as exposed by OneSparse), so users moving between the two find the same vocabulary.
- A semiring is named `<add>_<mult>`.
- ⊗ is applied as `⊗(a_ij, x_j)`, so `first` takes the edge weight and `second` takes the input vector value.
- v1 semirings: `plus_times`, `min_plus`, `max_plus`, `max_times`, `min_times`, `lor_land` (alias `or_and`), `any_pair`, `plus_pair`, `plus_first`, `plus_second`, `min_first`, `min_second`, `max_second`.
- A mask is a value mask with GraphBLAS semantics: an entry is selected when it casts to boolean true, meaning any non-zero value. NaN is non-zero, so it is selected. pg_warren vectors are dense, so `replace` (the "no entry" state outside the mask) is represented as the ⊕ identity of the semiring: 0 for `plus`, +∞ for `min`, −∞ for `max`, false for `lor`. `mask_complement` and `replace` correspond to GraphBLAS descriptors `c` and `r`; `transpose` corresponds to `t0`.
- An unweighted graph acts as if every weight were 1.

Semirings are implemented generically in `warren-core` over `(⊕, ⊗, identity)` for f64, i64, and bool element types, and for f32 too, so `aggregate_neighbours` works on f32 embeddings (A.5). With `A[i][j]` the weight of the edge `i → j`, `transpose = false` computes `y = A ⊗ x` (`y_i` combines the out-neighbours' values), pulled row by row over the forward CSR; `transpose = true` computes `y = Aᵀ ⊗ x` (`y_i` gathers from the in-neighbours, as PageRank does), pulled over the reverse CSR. Without a reverse CSR, `transpose = true` scatters (pushes) over the forward CSR instead: one thread, plus an `id_space × width` scratch charged to the memory budget. The pull form parallelises by rows with no write conflicts, which is why `build_reverse` defaults to true. Both forms fold each row's entries in ascending dense-ID order, so they agree bit for bit.

Decisions made in M4 (`warren-core::compute`):
- A relation selection makes `A[i][j]` the sum of the edge's weights over the selected relations, in relation order (1 when unweighted), which is what the merged CSR holds for all relations; without a stored merged CSR, all relations are summed so as they are read (§5.3), with the same bits. `weighted := true` on an unweighted graph uses weight 1.
- Edge weights are cast to the element type as SQL casts `double precision`: `i64` rounds half to even and fails out of range, `f32` fails on overflow or underflow, `bool` is `weight ≠ 0`. A weight is cast only if `⊗` reads it (`second` and `pair` do not).
- `min` and `max` use SQL order (`NaN` above every number, `-0 = 0`, ties keep the first operand), like `LEAST`/`GREATEST`. `any` takes the row's first entry, so results are deterministic. Float kernels otherwise use IEEE arithmetic (infinities are values, not errors); `i64` overflow is an error (`bigint out of range`). The exception keeps dense "no entry" values stable across supersteps: in the semiring kernels, the `i64` identity of `min` (`i64::MAX`) and of `max` (`i64::MIN`) stands for ±∞ and absorbs `plus` (`MAX + w = MAX` under `min`, `MIN + w = MIN` under `max`), so repeated integer `min_plus`/`max_plus` (hop counts, SSSP) leave unreached nodes at the identity rather than failing. They also absorb `times` by a weight above zero (`MAX · w = MAX` under `min`, `MIN · w = MIN` under `max`, for `w > 0`), so `min_times`/`max_times` supersteps keep unreached nodes too; times by a weight of zero or less treats the identity as an ordinary `bigint` (`MAX · 0 = 0`, `MAX · −1 = −MAX`; `MIN · −1` and `|w| ≥ 2` overflow), since ±∞ · w has no `bigint` value there. Every other overflow still errors. Floats need no exception, since `∞ + w = ∞`.
- Accumulation follows GraphBLAS: a row of `A` with no entry leaves `y_i` unchanged under `accumulate`, and without it writes the `⊕` identity. `accumulate` accepts any GraphBLAS binary operator name (`first | second | pair | plus | times | min | max | lor | land | any`; `first` keeps `y`, `second` takes the result). The mask is a width-1 vector of any element type, also for `spmm`, where it selects whole rows.
- `vector_map`'s compiled subset is a typed IR (`compute::Expr<T>` for each element type, built by the adapter from Postgres's analysed tree with every implicit cast made explicit). It is evaluated in parallel chunks, column at a time, under a mask of the elements SQL would evaluate: `CASE`, `COALESCE`, `AND` and `OR` evaluate an argument only where Postgres's executor would, so `CASE WHEN x = 0 THEN 0 ELSE 1/x END` does not fail. Operators, functions and casts follow Postgres's `float.c` and `int8.c`, including their errors (division by zero, overflow, underflow, `sqrt`/`ln`/`power` domain errors), so the compiled path raises the executor fallback's error. When several elements fail, the error is the first failing element's in row-major order. *No-NULL path (M9):* vectors hold no NULL, so an expression built only from vectors, non-NULL literals, casts, arithmetic, comparisons, `NOT` and `CASE` with an `ELSE` over those is never NULL (`compute::map`'s `not_null`); such a chunk is evaluated without NULL flags, reading operands of the result's width in place, each node one loop with its operator fixed outside it. It runs the same `float.c`/`int8.c` arithmetic on the same values in the same order, so results are bit-identical; it only detects that an active element failed, and the chunk is then re-evaluated on the columnar path, which reports the first failing element's error as before (proptests compare the two paths over random expressions, chunks and masks). It made `pagerank`'s maps 2–3× faster and one whole iteration (maps and product) 1.3–1.4× faster on one thread and 1.3–2× on four (4M nodes, 40M edges, `benches/spmv_out_of_cache.rs`, a loaded 4-vCPU machine), so the product is now about half an iteration (§6.10). Postgres has no `boolean`↔`real`/`double precision` casts; the IR defines them as for `bigint`.
- Vectors hold no NULL. Where an expression is NULL, a float vector stores `NaN` (so `pr / NULLIF(deg, 0)` works), and an `i64` or `bool` vector raises `null value cannot be stored in a … vector` (22004); the executor fallback applies the same rule (`compute::Value::NULL`). `NaN` read back is `NaN`, never NULL.
- Broadcasting: the result's width is the widest operand's; literals and width-1 vectors repeat across each row (`emb / deg` normalises rows); any other width is an error.
- Throughput (`benches/compute.rs`, 100K nodes, 1M edges, `plus_times`, 4-vCPU Xeon): pull over the forward or reverse CSR 0.38–0.42 G edges/s on one thread and 1.2 G edges/s on four; push without a reverse CSR 0.22 G edges/s (single-threaded). The §12 PageRank row is measured in M5.
- `vector_reduce` reads elements as `double precision` (`bool` as 0/1); `max` and `min` of no elements are NULL; `nnz` counts `NaN`. Reductions and `vector_norm_diff` combine fixed-size chunks in order, so results do not depend on `warren.threads`.

Decisions made in M4 (`pg_warren::compute_sql`):
- **`spmv`, `spmm`** take exactly the arguments above plus a trailing `version DEFAULT NULL`. They read `y`'s old values only where the product keeps them — outside the mask without `replace`, or under `accumulate` — and then `y` must have `x`'s dtype; otherwise (and when there is no `y` yet) `y` starts as `x`'s dtype and width filled with the `⊕` identity, GraphBLAS's empty vector in dense form. Unknown relation names in `rels` are skipped, as in traversal (§9.3).
- **`vector_map(graph, y, expr, version DEFAULT NULL)`** replaces `y` with the expression's value. `expr` is first parsed alone as `SELECT <expr>` and must be one target with no other clause (`syntax_error` otherwise), then analysed under the caller's `search_path` as `SELECT (<expr>) FROM (VALUES (NULL::<type>, …)) AS v(<vector names>)`, whose columns are the vectors bound to the version (transient first, §6.2) that the expression's column references name (every field of each `ColumnRef` in the raw tree, so the row's width does not grow with the vectors bound), typed by dtype; aggregates, window functions, set-returning functions and subqueries are `feature_not_supported`. The result's dtype is the expression's type, with `integer`/`smallint` cast to `bigint` and `numeric` to `double precision` in the analysed tree (so both paths cast alike); other types are `datatype_mismatch`. A matrix column has its element type, and the result's width is the widest operand's (so `0.7 * emb + 0.3 * emb_nbr` over `f32` rows is `f64`; cast it, `(…)::real`, to keep `f32`).
- **The compiled subset** is decided by function OID (`OpExpr.opfuncid`, `FuncExpr.funcid`): Postgres's `float8`, `float4`, `int8`, mixed `float48`/`float84` and `int84`/`int48` arithmetic and comparisons, `bool` comparisons, unary minus and plus, `%` on `bigint`, `abs`, `sqrt`, `exp`, `ln`, `log`/`log10`, `power`/`pow`/`^`, `floor`, `ceil`/`ceiling`, `round(float8)`, `sign`, and the casts among `float8`, `float4`, `int8`, `int4`, `int2` and `bool`; plus `Const`, `Var`, `CASE` (searched and simple), `NULLIF`, `COALESCE`, `LEAST`/`GREATEST`, `AND`/`OR`/`NOT`. `integer` and `smallint` values compile as `bigint` (exact for values, comparisons and casts), but their arithmetic, which overflows at a smaller range, does not. A `numeric` constant compiles where it is cast to `double precision` or `real` (the usual `0.85 * x`), converted from its decimal text with correct rounding as `numeric_float8` does; a constant that cast would reject falls back. A user's own operator or function never compiles, whatever its name. Everything else is evaluated by the executor.
