- **The executor fallback** compiles the same analysed tree once with `ExecInitExpr` (parent: a standalone `ResultState` over a fresh `EState`), and evaluates it per element over a virtual tuple of the synthetic row (referenced columns set, others NULL; a width-1 vector repeats across each row), resetting the per-tuple memory and checking for interrupts every 4,096 elements. It JIT-compiles when the planner would for a plan of cost `elements × cpu_operator_cost` (`jit`, `jit_expressions`, `jit_above_cost`, with `jit_optimize_above_cost` and `jit_inline_above_cost` for the optimisation flags). It runs on the main thread only, as the caller (never as the catalog owner) and under the caller's `search_path`, so names a called function resolves when it runs (SQL bodies, PL/pgSQL statements, `regclass` casts) resolve as in the caller's own SQL; and (*0.3.0*) in Postgres's read-only transaction state, as every read of caller SQL is (§7), so a function it calls that writes fails with 25006. A NULL result is stored as on the compiled path.
- **Agreement.** The compiled path and the executor give the same bytes and the same errors (SQLSTATE and message) — tested on 300 random expressions over random vectors with zeros, `-0`, NaN, infinities and extremes, with the executor forced by a test-only hook, and on division by zero, float and bigint overflow, logarithm and square-root domain errors and NULL into `i64`.
- **`explain_map(graph, expr, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS jsonb`**: `{"path": "compiled" | "executor", "reason": …, "dtype": …}`; for the executor the reason names the first operation outside the subset (`"function numeric_add is not in the compiled subset"`). Every Appendix A expression takes the compiled path (tested).
- `vector_reduce(graph, x, op, version DEFAULT NULL)` reduces the live rows. `vector_norm_diff(graph, a, b, norm DEFAULT 'l2', a_version DEFAULT NULL, b_version DEFAULT NULL)` reads `a` at `a_version` and `b` at `b_version` (each the current version if NULL) and refuses (`invalid_parameter_value`) versions a compaction separates, and (*0.3.0*) versions whose shared dense ids name different nodes, such as sibling staged versions whose new nodes diverge: `version::shared_dense_ids`, the check `diff` and `distance` make (§8).

A tier-1 program is a PL/pgSQL function over these primitives. The canonical shape:

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION my_pagerank(graph text, damping float8 DEFAULT 0.85, tol float8 DEFAULT 1e-6)
RETURNS int LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE i int; delta float8;
BEGIN
  PERFORM warren.vector_degree(graph, 'deg');
  PERFORM warren.vector_const(graph, 'pr', 1.0);
  FOR i IN 1..100 LOOP
    PERFORM warren.vector_map(graph, 'msg', 'pr / NULLIF(deg, 0)');
    PERFORM warren.spmv(graph, 'msg', 'gathered', transpose := true);
    PERFORM warren.vector_map(graph, 'next', format('%s + %s * gathered', 1 - damping, damping));
    delta := warren.vector_norm_diff(graph, 'next', 'pr', 'l1');
    PERFORM warren.vector_map(graph, 'pr', 'next');
    EXIT WHEN delta < tol;
  END LOOP;
  RETURN i;
END $$;
```

`spmv` is Pregel's gather, `vector_map` is apply, keyword arguments (`semiring`, `mask`, `transpose`, `rels`) are the vocabulary of the model. The library in §6.7 is written exactly this way so its source doubles as templates.

### 6.4 Tier 2 — `gather`: messages from SQL, apply in SQL

```sql
-- Fan a per-node message out over the graph and combine at the receiver, in-engine.
warren.gather(graph text,
              messages text,                 -- a vector name, a relation name, or SQL: SELECT <id>, <msg of any type> ...
              id_type anyelement,            -- witness: NULL::<the graph's id type>
              value_type anycompatible,      -- witness: NULL::<the combiner's result type>, e.g. NULL::text[] for collect over text
              combine text DEFAULT 'collect',-- collect | count | sum | min | max | first | concat | bool_or | bool_and
              direction text DEFAULT 'in',   -- messages travel along edges; 'in' = receive from in-neighbours
              rels text[] DEFAULT NULL,
              mask text DEFAULT NULL, mask_complement boolean DEFAULT false,
              version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, value anycompatible);   -- value is <msg_type>[] for 'collect'
```

The engine reads the message relation once through SPI, maps IDs to dense IDs, fans out over CSR with the chosen combiner in-engine (variable-width messages via the `Message` trait), and returns one row per receiving node. `collect` returns the array of received messages and makes the operation fully general: any Pregel `compute()` is `collect` followed by SQL.

A tier-2 superstep is therefore a `gather` plus an `UPDATE`/`INSERT ... SELECT` over a state table:

```sql
-- entity resolution: each node accumulates the set of identifiers seen in its neighbourhood
CREATE TEMP TABLE state (node_id uuid PRIMARY KEY, idents text[]);
INSERT INTO state SELECT id, ARRAY[id::text] FROM fraud_nodes;
FOR i IN 1..10 LOOP
  UPDATE state s
     SET idents = (SELECT array_agg(DISTINCT x) FROM unnest(s.idents || g.value) x)
    FROM warren.gather('fraud', 'SELECT node_id, unnest(idents) FROM state', NULL::uuid, NULL::text[]) g
   WHERE s.node_id = g.node_id;
END LOOP;
```

Cost is dominated by N rows through the executor per superstep — seconds at 10M nodes — not by the M edge operations, which stay in-engine. Per-vertex set accumulation of this kind grows memory as O(N × neighbourhood); it is the right tool for local neighbourhoods and the wrong tool for community-wide state, which is what §6.6 is for.

Decisions made in M5 (`warren-core::compute::gather`):
- Senders. A receiver gets one copy of each message of each of its *senders*: its distinct neighbours in `direction` over the selected relations. `in` = in-neighbours `{s : s → r}` (the orientation of `spmv(transpose := true)`), `out` = out-neighbours (`transpose := false`), `both` = their union. An edge in several selected relations or in both directions makes its sender a sender once; edge weights are not read. The SQL oracle is therefore `SELECT DISTINCT` (sender, receiver) pairs joined with the messages, grouped by receiver.
- Messages are `(dense id, message)` pairs in any order; a node may send several messages or none. The adapter maps the message relation's ids to dense ids; a dense id outside the id space is an error. Fixed-width messages are any vector element type; anything else is serialised to bytes (`compute::Bytes`).
- Collect order: ascending sender dense id, then a sender's messages in the order they were read. Every combiner folds in this order, so results (float sums included) are bit-identical for any `warren.threads`. Receivers with no message, or outside the mask, return no row.
- Combiner types follow SQL: `collect`, `count` (→ `bigint`) and `first` take any message; `sum`, `min`, `max` take `f32 | f64 | i64` (`min`/`max` in SQL order, `NaN` greatest; `i64` sum overflow is `bigint out of range`, where SQL's `sum(bigint)` would widen to `numeric`); `bool_or`/`bool_and` take `bool`; `concat` takes bytes (the adapter sends `text`/`bytea` as bytes). Any other pairing is `combine := <c> does not accept <kind> messages`, raised before any work. `min`/`max` of text would need collation, so they are not done in-engine.
- Kernel: messages are counting-sorted by sender into an outbox (`id_space + 1` offsets plus a sender-ordered copy). Receivers are pulled in parallel tasks of 4,096 over the CSR rows holding their senders: forward rows for `out`, reverse rows for `in`. Without a reverse CSR, `in` scatters the senders' forward rows into a per-receiver inbox of `(receiver, sender)` pairs (one thread, cancellation checked every 65,536 rows), charged to the memory budget like the outbox. Every buffer is charged its reserved capacity (`Message::growth`, exact geometric reservations). Result parts reserve the budget ahead in blocks (4 KiB doubling to 1 MiB, exact near the limit) and settle to their reserved capacity at the end of each task: charging every message to the shared atomic made `collect` slower on more threads.
- Output: per receiver, ascending, a `count`, one message (fixed-width element or bytes), or for `collect` the receiver's messages with each message's boundaries kept (end offsets into packed storage), streamed to SQL row by row.
- Throughput (`benches/gather.rs`, 100K nodes, 1M edges, every node sending, 4-vCPU Xeon): pulled, `sum` of f64 14 ms on one thread and 8 ms on four, `collect` of f64 19 ms and 8 ms, `collect` of 8-byte messages 46 ms and 18 ms; pushed (no reverse CSR) 30–70 ms.

Decisions made in M5 (`pg_warren::compute_sql::gather`, the SQL binding):
- The result types come from two witnesses, as `components` takes one (§9.3): `id_type` (`anyelement`, the graph's id type) and `value_type` (`anycompatible`, a separate polymorphic family, the combiner's result type). One signature cannot return `T[]` for `collect` and `bigint` for `count` from a run-time argument; a wrong witness is an error naming the right one (42804).
- Message source (§14 item 13): text that is not an identifier or `schema.identifier` is a query, never a vector. An identifier is a vector bound to the version if one has exactly that name (every live node sends its value; width 1), else a relation (`TABLE <name>`); a name that is both a bound vector and a relation on the caller's `search_path` is an error (42725), since vector names are shared by the graph's writers and one could otherwise redirect another user's `gather`. `TABLE <name>` always means the relation. A relation or query must return two columns, `(id, message)`; the id must cast implicitly to the id type; it runs as the caller under the caller's `search_path` as a read-only cursor in Postgres's read-only transaction state, as `vector_from_query` and the build's source queries do (§7: an integrity guard, not a sandbox; what still works under it is listed there). A data-modifying CTE is refused (0A000), as is a write through a function the query calls (25006); a relation resolves on the caller's `search_path` and is read with the caller's privileges; the caller must be a reader of the graph. Rows with a NULL, unknown or tombstoned id, or a NULL message, send nothing; a node may send several messages (one row each). *M7:* the rows are read a batch at a time in a memory context reset after each batch, so what reading a message allocates (a detoasted copy, a send function's state) lasts one batch; before, it was held until the read ended (100,000 `int[]` messages: 27.4 MB, now 4.4 MB, one batch).
- Result types (`codec.rs`), with the message's base type: `count` → `bigint`; `first`, `min`, `max` → the message type; `sum` of `smallint`/`integer`/`bigint` → `bigint` (overflow is an error, where SQL's `sum(bigint)` widens to `numeric`), of `real` → `real`, of `double precision` → `double precision`; `bool_or`/`bool_and` → `boolean`; `concat` of `text`/`varchar`/`char` → `text`, of `bytea` → `bytea`; `collect` → `array_agg`'s type: `T[]`, or for array messages the same array type with one more dimension, built by `accumArrayResultAny`, so ragged arrays fail as `array_agg` fails (2202E). To collect sets of different sizes, send their elements as separate messages (`SELECT id, unnest(idents) ...`).
- `smallint`, `integer`, `bigint`, `real`, `double precision` and `boolean` messages take the fixed-width path; `concat` reads text or bytea payloads; any other type travels as its binary send form and is rebuilt by its receive function (a type without binary I/O is Postgres's error). `sum`/`min`/`max` of other types (`numeric`, `text`) and `bool_*` of non-booleans are refused before any work.
- `gather` reads (it needs read access to the graph, writes nothing) and runs within one `warren.max_compute_memory` budget: the messages, the kernel's buffers and the result are charged to it. The result streams: the SRF's iterator owns the kernel's result (charged until the last row) and builds each row's datums when the executor asks for it, so only the current row is palloc'd. `concat` reads text and bytea as varlena bytes, never re-encoding.

### 6.5 Spectral analysis

```sql
-- top-k eigenpairs by magnitude (or smallest, for Laplacian) via Lanczos with full reorthogonalisation
warren.eigen(graph text, k int,
             matrix text DEFAULT 'adjacency',   -- adjacency | laplacian | normalized_laplacian | random_walk
             which text DEFAULT 'largest',      -- largest | smallest
             rels text[] DEFAULT NULL, weighted bool DEFAULT NULL,
             symmetrize bool DEFAULT true,      -- (A + Aᵀ)/2 for directed graphs; false → Arnoldi (v1.1)
             tol float8 DEFAULT 1e-6, max_iter int DEFAULT NULL,
             vectors_prefix text DEFAULT NULL,  -- store eigenvectors as <prefix>_1..k
             version bigint DEFAULT NULL,
             dtype text DEFAULT 'f32',          -- f32 | f64: the Lanczos and eigenvector element type (§14 item 8)
             persist bool DEFAULT true)         -- false: the eigenvectors are transient vectors
  RETURNS TABLE (i int, eigenvalue float8, residual float8);
```

**Implemented (M6, `pg_warren::spectral_sql`)** with that signature. The precision argument is `dtype`, the stored vectors' element type, because `precision` is an SQL keyword. Eigenvectors are persisted by default (they are results, and `distance` reads them in later statements); `persist := false` keeps them as transient vectors of the session. *M7:* a transient solve checks up front, with the ownership checks and before solving, that its `k` eigenvectors fit `warren.max_cache_memory` (§5.5), counting the caller's transient vectors it replaces or drops (`<prefix>_<i>` for `i > k`, now dropped before the eigenvectors are written) as released, so a refusal (53400) writes none. Owners and readers may call it; replacing stored eigenvectors follows the vector rules of §7 (only their writer or the graph owner). `eigen` is `PARALLEL UNSAFE` (it persists by default); `distance` reads and stores nothing, a fresh solve for `spectral` included, so it is `STABLE PARALLEL RESTRICTED` and runs in parallel mode (§6.9). The call runs within `warren.max_compute_memory` and polls interrupts like the compute functions.

Only SpMV is needed from the graph; with full reorthogonalisation Lanczos keeps its whole basis, so its state is m vectors of length `id_space` (m the basis size below) plus an m² projected matrix. For N = 10⁷, k = 50 (m = 101) that is ~8 GB in f64 — use f32 Lanczos vectors by default with f64 accumulation, or cap k and document. Implement in `warren-core` directly (~300 lines with reorthogonalisation); use `faer` or `nalgebra` for the small dense tridiagonal eigenproblem. Do not pull in ARPACK bindings in v1. SVD of the (rectangular, directed) adjacency via Lanczos on AᵀA is a straightforward extension. Full spectra are out of scope; k ≤ a few hundred is the design envelope.

**Implemented (M6, `warren_core::spectral`).**

- *Operators* (`GraphMatrix`) are applied matrix-free with PlusTimes SpMV over the selected relations, weighted or not. `S = (A + Aᵀ)/2` from the forward and reverse CSR (pushed over the forward CSR without one); `symmetrize = false` skips the second SpMV and is refused (`NotSymmetric`) unless a Freivalds check (`A·r = Aᵀ·r` for a seeded random `r`, relative tolerance 10⁻⁹) passes, so an undirected graph halves the cost. `D` holds the row sums of `S` (a self-loop counts once). `laplacian = D − S`, `normalized_laplacian = I − D^-1/2 S D^-1/2`; `random_walk = D^-1 S` runs on the similar `D^-1/2 S D^-1/2` and returns eigenvectors `D^-1/2 φ`, renormalised (its residual is that of `φ`). The normalised operators refuse a node with an edge and a non-positive degree.
- *Inactive nodes.* The eigenproblem is over the **active** nodes, those with at least one edge of `S`. Tombstoned nodes have no edges, and isolated live nodes (including nodes with no edge in the selected relations) are left out too: each would add an exact 0 to both Laplacians, a whole eigenspace of them in a graph with many isolated nodes. Every eigenvector is exactly zero at inactive nodes, and `k` may not exceed the number of active nodes.
- *Lanczos* with full reorthogonalisation and thick restart: the basis holds m = `max(2k + 1, 20)` vectors (at most the dimension, ARPACK's default); when full, the Rayleigh–Ritz pairs of the dense `H = QᵀMQ` (nalgebra `SymmetricEigen`, `std` feature only) decide convergence, else the basis is compressed to the k wanted Ritz vectors plus one per converged pair, clamped to between (m − k)/4 and (m − k)/2 more (ARPACK's `nev + min(nconv, np/2)` adjustment; the lower clamp keeps clustered spectra, such as a bipartite graph's ±λ pairs at small k, from stalling), and extended from the last residual. Each step subtracts the three-term recurrence's `β·q_{j−1}` and `α·q_j`, then makes one classical Gram–Schmidt pass against the whole basis (the recurrence is the first pass of "twice is enough"); the first step after a restart or a random vector, whose product couples to every kept vector, makes two full passes. The norm and the `α` removal are fused into those passes, so a step sweeps the basis twice. The basis is stored in blocks of 4,096 rows, vector by vector within a block, so the passes stream contiguous segments. `H`'s entries are the reorthogonalisation coefficients, so it is the projection onto the basis as stored. A breakdown (invariant subspace) continues from a fresh seeded random vector orthogonal to the basis.
- *`which`*: `largest` is largest magnitude, `λ` before `−λ` on ties, where magnitudes within `rounding·max|θ|` of the first of a run tie (rounding 10⁻⁶ for f32 vectors, 10⁻¹² for f64; never `tol`, which would let a loose tolerance reorder distinct magnitudes) and each run puts its positive values first, so that which of `±λ` rounding made larger does not decide the order; `smallest` is smallest algebraic. No spectral shift is used for `smallest`: a Krylov space of `σI − M` is that of `M`, so the Ritz values are the same; shift-and-invert would need linear solves.
- *Convergence*: a pair has converged when `|β·s_last| ≤ tol·max|θ|`; `max_iter` counts operator applications (default 1,000·m, raised to k; *M7:* 100·m until the eigen scaling work, which stopped a 1M-node random graph short, below) and `tol` defaults to 10⁻⁶ (*M7:* 10⁻⁸ before; with the default `f32` vectors the explicit residual floors near 10⁻⁷·‖M‖, so a tighter default bought 5–16% more steps and no accuracy, §14 item 21) and `Eigen::converged` says whether the test passed in time. The `residual` returned is `‖Mx − θx‖₂`, computed explicitly for the eigenvector as stored. **Non-convergence is reported, not an error:** `warren.eigen` returns the pairs it has with a `WARNING` naming the steps taken and the largest residual (the rows' residuals say which pairs are unconverged; a column would change the signature for a fact the residuals already carry), and `distance('spectral')` with a fresh solve adds `converged` (both solves) to its `jsonb`.
- *Precision* (§14 item 8): `f32` basis and eigenvectors by default, every sum in `f64`; `f64` is the option. With `f32` vectors the explicit residual floors near 10⁻⁷·‖M‖, the rounding of the stored vectors.
- *Determinism* (Appendix A.2): start and restart vectors are counter-based functions of (seed, stream, dense id), every reduction sums fixed-size blocks in block order, SpMV folds rows in dense-id order, and eigenvectors are signed so that their largest-magnitude entry is positive. Results are bitwise identical across runs and thread counts.
- *Memory*: the basis, the operator's `f64` work vectors (three, four for the normalised operators) and degrees, `H` and its Rayleigh–Ritz decomposition, and the k returned vectors are charged to `ComputeMemory`; workers poll the cancellation flag per block and the main thread polls the interrupts before every step. *M7:* the solve is refused (53400, naming the bytes) **before its first step** when its working set cannot fit: once the operator is prepared (its degrees give the dimension), `warren_core::spectral::WorkingSet` sizes the peak, which comes at the end of a solve — operator, basis (m × the id space rounded up to whole 4,096-row blocks × the element size), `H` and three m × m matrices of the decomposition, the k eigenvectors in the target dtype and the components pass's 8 bytes a row — and that much is charged once, through the budget's reclaim (so idle pins may be evicted), and released; the solve then charges its buffers into the room found. A transient charge of a step (the push-form scratch of a transposed product) is smaller than what the end adds. Not counted: the row scratch of a product that merges relations on the fly (`store_merged := false`) and per-block partial sums. Before, the M7 soak's 6.0 GB merged graph (k = 20, f32, default limits) ran the load and all 200 steps, 141.5 s, before its eigenvectors were refused. A checked charge rather than a reservation held for the call, because every buffer is already charged where it is allocated; holding the peak as well would count it twice or need each allocation to draw on the reservation. `distance('spectral')` with a fresh solve checks the second version's working set (`spectral::admit_eigen`) before solving the first, so neither runs when either cannot fit.
- *Measured against ARPACK* (`bench/eigen.py`: SciPy 1.18 `eigsh`, seeded start vector, default `ncv`, on the harness's `random` fixture of 100K nodes and 1M undirected edges, 2M non-zeros; k = 20, tol = 10⁻⁶, f32 vectors; a shared 4-vCPU box, so seconds are noisy, steps are not; `results/eigen/`):

  | | ARPACK matvecs (s) | before: steps, 1 / 4 threads | now: steps, 1 / 4 threads |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | adjacency, largest | 1,020–1,313 (15–31 s) | 1,405, 31.0 / 13.9 s | 1,037, 9.9 / 3.7 s |
  | normalised Laplacian, smallest | 1,406–1,477 (10–65 s) | 1,702, 40.6 / 16.1 s | 1,342, 13.1 / 5.1 s |

  ARPACK's count varies by a quarter with its start vector; ours is fixed by the seed. Eigenvalues agree to 10⁻⁹. The step count fell with the restart retention, the time per step (now ~9.5 ms on one thread, ~3.7 ms on four) with the two-sweep orthogonalisation. A basis of 3k + 1 cuts the steps further (800 and 1,159) at 1.5× the memory; it is not the default. A uniform random graph's spectrum is a dense bulk, about the slowest case. Scaled linearly to 10M nodes and 100M edges, a step is ~0.4 s on four threads, so k = 20 projects to ~8–9 min on this box against the §12 target of 10 min on eight cores; M7 measures it.
- *At larger scales (M7 soak and `bench/eigen.py` at 1M nodes, 10M undirected edges, the same average degree 20; k = 20, tol 10⁻⁶, f32, 4 threads; `results/eigen/random-1000000-10000000-seed1.json`).* Adjacency, largest: 3,174 steps in 173 s against ARPACK's 3,325 matvecs in 499 s (0.95 of ARPACK's count; eigenvalues agree to 5·10⁻⁹). Normalised Laplacian, smallest: **not converged** in the default `max_iter` of 4,100 operator applications (residual 1.0·10⁻⁵, eigenvalues within 6·10⁻⁸ of ARPACK's), 226 s, where ARPACK converged at 4,343 matvecs in 712 s; so the ratio is at least 0.94, as at 100K (0.91–0.95). At 10M nodes and 100M directed edges (the soak's case a, symmetrised), the in-database solve also stopped at 4,100 steps (residual 1.0·10⁻⁴) after 65 min, 0.96 s a step on 4 shared threads; `warren-core` alone (`eigen_convergence` on the same edges, streamed through a FIFO, which symmetrises as A ∨ Aᵀ rather than (A + Aᵀ)/2, the same up to about a hundred reciprocal pairs) took the same 4,100 steps in 41 min, 0.60 s a step. ARPACK was not run at 10M (SciPy would hold a 3.3 GB f64 basis and a 2.4 GB matrix beside the generator's arrays on a 15 GB machine shared with the database). The step count of this spectrum grows about 3× per decade of N, so ~13,000 steps are projected at 10M: the default `max_iter` (100·m) is too low from about 1M nodes for the clustered end of a random graph's spectrum, and the §12 target of 10 min is missed by an order of magnitude on this machine. *Since (M7, below and §14 item 21):* the step count was measured against N, the cause found in the spectrum, and the cheap levers taken; the 1M solve now converges in 4,590 steps under the new default `max_iter`.
- *Why the steps grow with N (M7, `bench/eigen.py`, `results/eigen/`; k = 20, normalised Laplacian, smallest, tol 10⁻⁶, f32, 4 threads, average degree 20).* Lanczos needs about √(spread/gap) steps per digit of accuracy, where gap is the distance from the k-th eigenvalue to the next and spread the width of the spectrum (1.436 here). On a uniform random graph the k smallest eigenvalues are the lower edge of the bulk (0.564…0.566), where eigenvalues crowd ever closer as N grows: λ₂₁ − λ₂₀ was 9.1·10⁻⁵, 3.0·10⁻⁵ and 7.9·10⁻⁶ at 100K, 300K and 1M nodes (about 1/N; the mean spacing of λ₂…λ₂₀ 9.2·10⁻⁵, 4.3·10⁻⁵, 1.7·10⁻⁵), and the steps grew as about √N: 1,342, 2,941, 4,590 (ARPACK 1,406, 2,980, 4,343 matvecs). No polynomial method beats this by much, Lanczos being near-optimal among them: Chebyshev filtering, LOBPCG and larger bases were measured and rejected (§14 item 21). On a stochastic block model with 40 equal communities (16 of a node's 20 edges inside its community) the k smallest eigenvalues are community eigenvalues near 0.18, separated from the bulk at 0.55, and the steps stay flat: 300, 371, 356 at 100K, 300K, 1M (ARPACK 328, 373, 370). So `eigen` at 10⁷ nodes is fast exactly when the wanted eigenvalues are separated from the rest, which is also when its eigenvectors are well defined (subspace distance, A.2); the only lever for a random graph's bulk edge is a spectral transform (shift-invert, needing linear solves, or a polynomial preconditioner), a follow-up, not v1.
- *Basis passes (M7).* Each dot product of the basis passes (the recurrence's α, the Gram–Schmidt coefficients, the norms) sums in eight lanes, term i into lane i mod 8, the lanes then added in order: a fixed reassociation, so results changed in the last bits from M6's single chain of adds but are identical across runs and thread counts. A restart's rotation sums each row's combination in basis order over chunks of 256 rows (bit-identical to the row-at-a-time loop). When every selected relation's reverse CSR equals its forward CSR (an undirected graph built with reverse CSRs), `A = Aᵀ` exactly and the symmetrised operator multiplies once: the transposed product would read the same arrays in the same order, so `(A·x + Aᵀ·x)/2` is `A·x` to the bit. All three are pinned by property tests (the dense reference, exact rotation, the two-product operator).
- *Limitation*: from one start vector, Lanczos finds one direction per eigenspace, so the copies of a repeated eigenvalue appear only through rounding or a breakdown restart. A graph with c connected components has Laplacian eigenvalue 0 with multiplicity c; compute on the largest component when that matters. Block Lanczos would lift this. `Eigen::components` counts the connected components of the active nodes (union-find over the selected forward CSRs), and `warren.eigen` warns when there are several. Deflating against component indicators was considered for the Laplacians' 0 but not done: the eigenspace is known only for `smallest`, there can be more components than the basis holds (thousands of small components), and other repeated eigenvalues (symmetric components, a cycle's ±λ pairs) would still be missed; on 30 disjoint 40-cycles the 3 smallest Laplacian eigenvalues come back as [0, 0.025, 0.098] against a true [0, 0, 0], with the warning.
- *Follow-up: block Lanczos* (not now). Lanczos with a block of b vectors finds up to b copies of an eigenvalue and replaces b SpMVs per block step by one SpMM, which reads the CSR once per block (a bandwidth win at 10⁷ nodes). It needs more operator applications for simple spectra, a block QR per step, and block-aware restarts and convergence tests. Single-vector Lanczos already needs about as many operator applications as ARPACK, so block Lanczos is deferred until repeated eigenvalues matter in practice (components, symmetric structures) or the SpMV bandwidth binds at the M7 scale.

### 6.6 Community-stateful computation: contraction, project, lift

Per-vertex models — Pregel included — go wrong on **community-stateful** computations, where state belongs to a group of vertices rather than to each one: every member ends up carrying a copy, memory grows as O(N × community size), and the workaround is to elect leader vertices and route through them. The scalable form is two-level: state lives once per community, vertices carry only a membership label, and the computation alternates between levels. This design supports that in two stages.

**Stage 1 (v1, no engine support needed).** A community is a `GROUP BY` on a label vector produced by tier 1. The community table holds state once per community; broadcasting back to members is a join; member-to-member messaging uses `gather`. State is stored once, which is already better than Pregel.

**Stage 2 (0.4.0, done).** Three operations borrowed from multigrid methods make the two-level pattern native, fast and recursive:

```sql
-- Quotient graph: one node per distinct label, edges aggregated (weight = edge count or summed weight).
-- Creates (or adds a version to) a derived graph in the catalog whose node ids are the label values and
-- which stores the membership matrix P (N × K, one non-zero per row) linking it to its parent version.
warren.contract(graph text, label_vector text, out_graph text,
                weighted bool DEFAULT true, drop_self_loops bool DEFAULT false,
                promote bool DEFAULT true, version bigint DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;  -- quotient version_id

-- vertex → community aggregation (Pᵀ·x): sum | mean | max | min | count
warren.project(graph text, x text, out_graph text, y text, agg text DEFAULT 'sum',
               persist bool DEFAULT true, version bigint DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS void;

-- community → vertex broadcast (P·y)
warren.lift(out_graph text, y text, graph text, x text,
            persist bool DEFAULT true, version bigint DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS void;
```

With these, a community-stateful computation is a tier-1 or tier-2 program *on the quotient graph* with `project`/`lift` at the boundaries. Because `contract` produces a real graph version, it recurses: contracting the quotient gives hierarchical communities, which is exactly Louvain/Leiden and multilevel partitioning — library algorithms rather than special engines (§6.7 group 3).

Design obligations imposed in v1, both met in 0.4.0 (below):

1. The catalog's notion of a graph gains a second **source**: derived by contraction, referencing a parent version and a label vector, rather than only built from SQL queries. Versioning, staging, retention and vectors work identically; a `parent_version` column on `warren.versions` records the link.
2. Vectors are bindable to quotient graphs, and `project`/`lift` validate that the parent and quotient versions correspond.

Decisions made in 0.4.0 (`warren_core::community`, `warren_core::graph::Membership`, `pg_warren::community_sql`):
- **Signatures.** As above. `contract` gained `promote` (staging works as for `refresh`: `promote := false` leaves the version staged) before the trailing `version`, which is the *parent* version to contract (default its current one); `project` and `lift` gained `persist` (the library keeps its intermediates transient, §6.2) and a trailing `version`, which is the *quotient* version (default `out_graph`'s current one). Neither takes a parent version: a quotient version names the version it was contracted from, and `x` is read (`project`) or written (`lift`) at exactly that version, which is how the two always correspond. `contract` and `project`/`lift` are `PARALLEL UNSAFE` (they write).
- **The derived graph.** `graphs.source` is `'contraction'` (a `CHECK`ed column, `'query'` or `'contraction'`); its `node_query` and `edge_query` are NULL; its `id_type` is `bigint`; `directed`, `has_relations`, `build_reverse` and `store_merged` are its first parent graph's (a later contraction into it from a graph of another direction or relation shape is refused, 22023); it is `weighted` (weights are sums or counts); `retain_versions` is 2 and `node_order` `'id'`. The first `contract` into a name creates it, owned by the caller; later ones add versions. A graph built from queries cannot receive a contraction (42809), and `out_graph` cannot be `graph` (22023).
- **Ids.** The quotient's node ids are the distinct label values of the parent's live nodes, so `vector_values('q', …, NULL::bigint)` joins back to the labels. Its dense ids are assigned as a refresh's are (§5.2): a label already in its current version keeps its dense id, new labels are appended in label order, and labels gone are tombstoned. So `id_space` is the number of labels ever seen since the quotient's first version (the obligation's "label range" was a dense-id-equals-label reading; labels such as `components_vector`'s smallest dense ids are sparse, so it was not adopted). Vectors on two versions of a quotient compare by label (`vector_norm_diff`, `diff`, `distance`).
- **Labels.** A width-1 vector of `i64`, `f64` or `f32`. Each live node's label must be an integer within `bigint`'s range: NaN, an infinity, a fraction or a value out of range is refused with 22023 (`label vector: the label of dense id 3 is 0.5, which is not an integer`), and a `bool` vector with 42804, before anything is written. Tombstoned nodes are in no community. A transient label vector works; it is recorded as NULL.
- **Edges (`PᵀAP`).** For a directed parent, the edge `c → d` sums the weights (or, with `weighted := false`, counts) of the stored edges from members of `c` to members of `d`: exactly `PᵀAP`. For an undirected parent, stored both ways with a self-loop once, the quotient is undirected and is likewise `PᵀAP` of the stored matrix (decided in the PR #28 review): an edge between two communities adds its weight to both directions, an edge inside one adds it twice to the community's self-loop, and a parent's self-loop adds once. Under pg_warren's own degree convention (a self-loop counts once in its node's weighted degree, as in `spmv`, `vector_degree` and the spectral operators, §6.5) the quotient then keeps every community's weighted degree equal to the sum of its members' and keeps the total `2m`, so a quantity read off the quotient, such as modularity, is the parent's (tested: the quotient's weighted out-degree equals `project(sum)` of the parent's). The alternative first implemented, counting an internal edge once (NetworkX's `quotient_graph` with each block's internal edges as a self-loop), broke both: on the `docs/communities.md` example member degrees summed to 106 and quotient degrees to 87. `drop_self_loops` leaves out the edges inside communities. Relations are kept: each of the parent's relations is contracted on its own into the quotient relation of the same name, and the quotient's all-relations CSR sums them as any merged CSR does (so with `weighted := false` an edge present in two relations counts twice there). Parallel edges were folded by the parent's build, so a count is of distinct (source, target) pairs per relation. A parent's weight of NaN propagates into the sum.
- **The kernel.** `warren_core::community::contract` is Gustavson's row-by-row SpGEMM specialised to one non-zero per row of `P`: the parent's nodes are counting-sorted by community, then each community in turn accumulates its members' rows (ascending dense id, each row in order) into a dense row of the quotient, emitted into the build's `EdgeList`. Sums are therefore taken in one order whatever the thread count. It runs as one job on the compute pool (a single thread: the rows are not split; parallelising by community needs a dense row per thread, K per thread, or hashing), checks its cancellation flag every 65,536 row entries, and charges its scratch and its output to `warren.max_build_memory`: per node, at its peak, about 24 B (the labels as read, the membership, and the members grouped by community); per community about 33 B (the grouping's starts and its `next` cursors, 8 B each, and the dense row's sums, 8 B, touched flags, 1 B, and touched list, 8 B). The quotient's CSRs, reverse CSRs and merged CSR then come from the normal build path (`build::write_contents`: `BuildPlan` within the budget, refused before any write; the streamed segment writer; the version row, statistics and promotion with retention), with the membership written as one more segment. `deduplicated_edges` and `dropped_edges` are 0.
- **The membership.** `warren_core::graph::Membership` holds each parent node's community (a quotient dense id; `u64::MAX` for none), stored as the quotient version's `membership` segment (kind 7, `u64` elements, one per parent dense id), so `P` is frozen with the version whatever happens to the label vector later. Decoding checks every community lies in the quotient's id space; `project` and `lift` also check that exactly the parent version's live nodes have one (a mismatch is `data_corrupted`, XX001).
- **Lineage.** `versions.parent_version` (a self-reference, so dump order is unaffected, §5.1) is now `ON DELETE SET NULL`; `versions.label_vector` holds the persisted label vector's id, and a trigger on `warren.vectors` (`label_vector_is_forgotten`, `AFTER DELETE`, with a partial index on the column) clears it when that vector is deleted, so neither link dangles; the insert takes the label vector's row `FOR KEY SHARE` in the same statement, so a concurrent `drop_vector` waits for it and its trigger then clears the link (one already gone records NULL). `label_vector` cannot be a foreign key: with `vectors.version_id` it would close a cycle (§5.1). Both columns have partial indexes (`WHERE … IS NOT NULL`) for the trigger, retention's count of derived versions and the `SET NULL`. `warren.info` reports `source`, and per version `parent_version`, `parent_graph` and `label_vector`, the three NULL to a caller who cannot read the parent graph. An unknown `source` is `data_corrupted`.
- **Concurrency.** `contract` holds the parent graph's row `FOR SHARE` while it reads the parent version (conflicting with the `FOR UPDATE` of `refresh`, `promote`, `compact`, `drop_version` and `drop_graph`), and checks in a statement after the lock that the version still exists, so a concurrent drop either waits for the contraction or makes it fail as an unknown version (42704), never with a raw 23503 or a quotient unlinked from birth (`test/sql/contract_parent_race.sql`). It then locks the quotient's row `FOR UPDATE`, as `refresh` does.
- **Retention and drops.** A promoted version that a quotient version was contracted from is kept beyond `retain_versions`, as a version with bound vectors is, with a `NOTICE` (`… 1 contracted version derives from it`), so `project`/`lift` keep working on the quotient. `drop_version` of such a version, or `drop_graph` of the parent, is allowed: the quotient's versions keep their graph, CSRs and vectors but lose the link, and `project`/`lift` on them then fail with 55000 (`… was contracted from a version that has since been dropped`). Dropping the quotient graph frees the parent versions it held.
- **`refresh` and `compact` of a derived graph are refused** (42809, `cannot refresh graph "q": it is derived by contraction; warren.contract makes its versions`). Re-contracting from the parent's current version was the alternative, but a label vector is bound to one parent version (§6.2): on a newer version the named vector usually does not exist, or holds labels computed on older data, so a refresh would either fail or silently contract by stale labels. New labels come from running the labelling again, so `contract` is the one way to make a version. Compaction would have to renumber the membership's targets and carry the lineage; a quotient's tombstones are labels that disappeared, and contracting into a new graph gives dense ids without them.
- **Access (§7).** `contract` reads the parent: the caller must be a reader (or owner) of `graph`. A new quotient is owned by the caller, like `create_graph`, so a reader may derive a community graph of its own and grant it (exposing aggregated topology the reader could already read). Contracting into an existing quotient is a management action: the caller must be a member of its owner role (42501), and the parent must be a version of the quotient's parent graph (that of its latest version still linked; 22023 otherwise, and also when no version is linked any more, after the parent graph or its linked versions were dropped, or the quotient has no versions: such a quotient takes no new versions), so an owner cannot fold versions derived from another graph, perhaps one its readers cannot read, into a graph they read, nor mix two label spaces. `project`/`lift` name a quotient's actual parent in their mismatch error only to a caller who can read it. `project` and `lift` read one graph and write a vector on the other: the caller must be a reader of both, and the vector written follows the ownership rule of §7.
- **`project` and `lift`.** `project` reads `x` (any dtype, read as `double precision`, `bool` as 0/1; any width) at the parent version and writes `y` on the quotient version with `x`'s width: `f64` for `sum`, `mean`, `max`, `min` (SQL order, NaN greatest), `i64` for `count` (the member count, in every column). Members are folded in ascending dense id, so sums are deterministic; a community with no member (a tombstoned quotient node) holds 0. `mean` of a matrix is the centroid of each community's rows (pgvector embeddings to community centroids). `lift` writes `x` on the parent version with `y`'s dtype and width, each live node holding its community's row, tombstoned nodes 0. Both are single passes, one thread, charged to `warren.max_compute_memory` (the membership as read and decoded, and the result); both versions are opened through the cache (not as a pair: they have different id types).
- **Measured** (`bench/results/communities/`: release build, 4 shared vCPUs, through SQL, each call in a fresh session so including the parent version's load, 0.13 s at 1M nodes and 1.45 s at 10M; a block graph of N nodes in blocks of 1,000 with 5N undirected edges, 10N stored, labels from `components_vector` over the blocks' own edges): `contract` into N / 1,000 communities 0.35 s at 1M nodes and **4.2 s at 10M** (`refresh` of the graph itself: 3.1 s and 36.5 s); by each node's dense id (a quotient as large as the graph, the worst case) 1.7 s and 13.7 s; `project` (`count`, `mean`) and `lift` 0.14–0.16 s and 1.9–2.0 s, nearly all of it the load. A contraction is cheap next to the build it starts from: one pass over the stored edges, then a build of edges no more numerous than the parent's.
- **Tests.** Property tests of the kernel against dense `PᵀAP` and that a community's weighted degree is its members', of `project` and `lift` against their definitions, of labels and the membership segment; unit tests of the pipeline over the build's in-memory ports; pg_tests for the SQL surface, errors, access, lineage, retention and the trigger; `test/sql/oracle_contraction.sql` (undirected, weighted, self-loops, parallel edges, a tombstone) and `oracle_contraction_directed.sql` (directed, two relations, counts) against NetworkX's `quotient_graph` (whose diagonal, the undirected internal edges once, the oracle doubles), and `project`/`lift` against SQL `GROUP BY` and joins.

### 6.7 Algorithm library (v1)

Shipped as PL/pgSQL functions over the primitives (readable, copyable), each returning a named vector and parameterised by `rels`, `weighted`. Their intermediate vectors are transient (`persist := false`, §6.2); only the named result is persisted:

- `warren.pagerank(graph, out, damping, tol, max_iter, seed := NULL)` — seed gives personalised PageRank.
- `warren.components_vector(graph, out)` — min-label propagation (`min_second` SpMV); also available as the traversal SRF.
- `warren.label_propagation(graph, out, seed, max_iter)` — community / semi-supervised.
- `warren.sssp_vector(graph, out, source)` — min_plus SpMV from a source; all-nodes distances.
- `warren.degree_vector(graph, out, direction, rels)`.
- `warren.aggregate_neighbours(graph, x, out, agg DEFAULT 'mean', hops DEFAULT 1, rels)` — mean/sum/max over neighbour rows of an N×d matrix; the "one-layer GNN" primitive for smoothing or propagating pgvector embeddings.
- `warren.triangles(graph, out)` — per-node triangle count via masked SpMM (needs sorted targets).
- *0.4.0*, on top of §6.6: `warren.louvain(graph, out, …)` and `warren.leiden(graph, out, …)`, with `warren.modularity` and the two steps they are written over, `warren.local_moving` and `warren.leiden_refine` (group 3 below).

*Planned library (decided 2026-10-20, post-v0.3).* The library grows from a curated slice of NetworkX's catalogue, chosen for what the target users (knowledge graphs, fraud, entity resolution, recommendation) need and for near-linear cost at 10M nodes, not ported wholesale. Each addition gets a NetworkX oracle test on random graphs, a PL/pgSQL body wherever the primitives suffice (so it stays a copyable template, §6.1), and a §12-style measurement at scale; an algorithm that is awkward to write marks a missing primitive.
1. *Over the existing primitives (PL/pgSQL):* eigenvector centrality and Katz centrality (SpMV iterations); HITS hubs and authorities (alternating `spmv` and its transpose); majority-vote label propagation (`gather`; today's `label_propagation` is min-label); local clustering coefficient (`triangles` over degree pairs). **Done (0.4.0)**, decisions below.
2. *New `warren-core` kernels:* k-core decomposition and degeneracy (peeling; *done, 0.4.0*); strongly connected components (`components` is weak; *done, 0.4.0*); neighbourhood similarity — Jaccard, Adamic–Adar, common neighbours — for link prediction, on the sorted-row intersection `triangles` uses (*done, 0.4.0*); approximate betweenness (sampled sources, batched Brandes as LAGraph does) and harmonic/closeness centrality from sampled sources (exact forms are infeasible at 10M; *done, 0.4.0*, with the exact forms for small graphs); topological order and longest path on DAGs (citation, lineage; *done, 0.4.0*); minimum spanning tree/forest on weighted graphs (*done, 0.4.0*). Group 2 is done; decisions below.
3. *On contraction and SpGEMM (§6.6, §6.11):* Louvain and Leiden. **Done (0.4.0)**, decisions below.
4. *Not ported:* clique enumeration, isomorphism (VF2), exact betweenness and all-pairs shortest paths at scale, max-flow, matching — too costly at the target sizes or rarely needed; export for those.

Decisions made in M5 (`pg_warren::compute_sql::library`, one `.sql` file per function):
- Signatures: `pagerank(graph, out, damping DEFAULT 0.85, tol DEFAULT 1e-6, max_iter DEFAULT 100, seed DEFAULT NULL, rels, weighted, version) RETURNS int`; `components_vector(graph, out, rels, version) RETURNS int`; `label_propagation(graph, out, seed DEFAULT NULL, max_iter DEFAULT 100, mask DEFAULT NULL, rels, version) RETURNS int`; `sssp_vector(graph, out, source anyelement, rels, weighted, max_iter DEFAULT NULL, version) RETURNS int`; `degree_vector(graph, out, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels, version) RETURNS bigint`; `aggregate_neighbours(graph, x, out, agg DEFAULT 'mean', hops DEFAULT 1, rels, direction DEFAULT 'out', version) RETURNS void`. Iterative functions return the iterations they ran; `degree_vector` returns the vector id.
- Each pins its version once (`version`, else the current one) and passes it to every call, so a concurrent `refresh` cannot split a run across versions. Intermediates are transient vectors named `_warren_<function>_<part>`, dropped at the end, and also when the run fails or is cancelled (an `EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS OR query_canceled` handler drops them and re-raises: one subtransaction per call); only `out` is persisted. The `_warren_` prefix is reserved: no vector of such a name can be persisted (42939, `reserved_name`), so an intermediate can never replace, and so delete, a persisted vector, and the library refuses it for `out` and its input vectors. Constants (`1 / N`, `damping`, a seed's sum, the dangling mass) enter expressions as constant vectors, never as formatted text, so `extra_float_digits` cannot round them. Every function sets `search_path = pg_catalog, pg_temp`. Tests assert that no `vector_map` they run falls back to the executor.
- `pagerank` follows NetworkX: out-weight normalisation (`plus_first` row sums; edge count when unweighted), dangling rank and the teleport to the personalisation (uniform, or `seed / sum(seed)`), uniform start, stop when the L1 change < live nodes × `tol`.
- `components_vector` and `label_propagation` are min-label propagation (`min_second` both ways with `accumulate := 'min'`, Jacobi updates) until no label changes; `label_propagation` takes a seed vector and a mask (unselected nodes neither send nor take labels). It is deterministic min-propagation, not majority-vote LPA.
- `sssp_vector` is Bellman-Ford with `min_plus` along edges until no distance falls; by default at most the live node count of iterations, after which distances still falling mean a negative cycle reachable from the source, an error (22023), as NetworkX raises; an explicit `max_iter` just stops there. An unknown source is an error (22023); unreachable nodes are `Infinity`.
- `triangles(graph, out, rels DEFAULT NULL, persist DEFAULT true, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint` is a kernel, not PL/pgSQL: semiring products alone would need SpGEMM or an N×N dense matrix. It counts each node's triangles in the undirected simple graph under `rels` (directions and self-loops ignored, as NetworkX): a symmetric adjacency is built once (charged; no reverse CSR needed), then each node sums sorted-row intersections with its neighbours, in parallel: a merge for rows of similar length, else a binary search of the shorter row's values in the longer (more than 16× longer), so a hub costs about its degree × log, not its degree squared. Cost about `Σ_u Σ_{v∈N(u)} min(d_u, d_v) · log max(d_u, d_v)`; a task checks its cancellation flag every 65,536 row entries it visits, so a hub's row cannot outlast a cancel (§6.9).
- `aggregate_neighbours` iterates `hops` times over distinct neighbours (weights are not read); a node with no neighbours gets NaN for `mean` (SQL `AVG` of nothing), the `⊕` identity for `sum` (0) and `max` (−∞); `mean` needs a float matrix and keeps its dtype.

Decisions made in 0.4.0 (planned library group 1, `pg_warren::compute_sql::library`; the M5 rules above apply: one `.sql` file each, version pinned, transient `_warren_<function>_<part>` intermediates dropped on failure or cancel, constants as constant vectors, `search_path` pinned, no executor fallback, tested):
- Signatures: `eigenvector_centrality(graph, out, tol DEFAULT 1e-6, max_iter DEFAULT 100, rels, weighted, version) RETURNS int`; `katz_centrality(graph, out, alpha DEFAULT 0.1, beta DEFAULT 1.0, seed DEFAULT NULL, normalized DEFAULT true, tol DEFAULT 1e-6, max_iter DEFAULT 1000, rels, weighted, version) RETURNS int`; `hits(graph, hubs, authorities, tol DEFAULT 1e-8, max_iter DEFAULT 100, normalized DEFAULT true, rels, weighted, version) RETURNS int` (two output vectors, which must differ: 22023); `clustering(graph, out, rels, version) RETURNS void` (one pass, like `aggregate_neighbours`); `label_propagation_majority(graph, out, seed DEFAULT NULL, max_iter DEFAULT 100, rels, version) RETURNS int`. Defaults are NetworkX's, except that `weighted` follows `spmv` (NULL: the graph's weights if it has them), where NetworkX's eigenvector and Katz centrality default to `weight=None`.
- **NetworkX semantics, step for step.** Each centrality runs NetworkX's own iteration: eigenvector centrality `x ← (x + Aᵀx) / ‖x + Aᵀx‖₂` from `1/N`, stopping when the L1 change < N·tol; Katz `x ← α·Aᵀx + β` from 0, the same stop, then scaled to unit L2 norm if `normalized`; HITS (NetworkX's default `method='power_iteration'`) `a ← Aᵀh`, `h ← A·a / max(A·a)` from `1/N`, stopping when the L1 change of `h` < tol (not scaled by N), then each summed to 1 (or `a` scaled to a largest value of 1 unnormalised). So with the same `tol` and `max_iter` both stop at the same iteration and agree to rounding; the oracle tests (`test/sql/oracle_centrality.sql`, a weighted directed G(400, 1200)) hold every score within 10⁻⁹. N is the live node count (NetworkX's nodes), and centrality flows along edges (Aᵀ: in-edges), as NetworkX's left eigenvector does. Katz's per-node β (a NetworkX dict) is `beta × seed`, with `seed` a vector, as `pagerank`'s personalisation is.
- **Non-convergence warns.** NetworkX raises `PowerIterationFailedConvergence`; these write the last iterate, return `max_iter`, and raise a `WARNING` (`<function> did not converge in <n> iterations`; tested), as `eigen` reports non-convergence rather than failing (§6.5). `pagerank` (M5) stops silently at `max_iter` (§14 item 24).
- **Divergence is an error.** A run whose values stop being finite is refused with 22003 (`numeric_value_out_of_range`, `<function> diverged: …`, with a hint) and writes nothing, its intermediates dropped: Katz centrality with `alpha` at or above 1 / the largest eigenvalue (NetworkX raises, unable to converge; before, `out` was all NaN, or all Infinity unnormalised, with only the warning), and eigenvector centrality or HITS whose edge weights overflow `double precision`. Katz checks each iteration's L1 change, so a divergent run stops within a few iterations rather than running `max_iter`. One case refuses where NetworkX succeeds: `vector_reduce`'s L2 norm squares naively, so a norm past about 10¹⁵⁴ overflows, where NetworkX's `math.hypot` does not (eigenvector centrality with weights near 10¹⁵⁴, or a Katz result that large).
- **Arguments.** NULL `out` (or `hubs`, `authorities`), `alpha`, `beta`, `normalized`, `tol` or `max_iter` is refused up front with 22004 (`<argument> must not be NULL`), before any intermediate exists; `tol < 0` and `max_iter < 1` with 22023; an unknown `seed` with 42704, as `pagerank`. A graph with no live nodes is refused by the three centralities (22023). Only NetworkX's eigenvector centrality raises there (`NetworkXPointlessConcept`); its `katz_centrality` and `hits` return empty results. The three refuse alike because a centrality of no nodes is no result: an empty `out` would read as a successful run on a graph that was, say, emptied by a refresh. `label_propagation_majority` and `clustering` have nothing to normalise: on a graph with no live nodes they write an empty `out` (the former running no iterations, returning 0), as NetworkX finds no communities and no coefficients.
- **Never divide by a bare constant vector.** A constant vector is 0 at tombstoned rows (§6.2) and `vector_map` evaluates every row, so `x / c` raised division by zero on any version with a deleted node: `pagerank` with a seed did (M5, fixed in 0.4.0 by multiplying by the sum's reciprocal), and so did the first eigenvector centrality and HITS. Group 1 divides under a guard, `CASE WHEN c <> 0 THEN x / c ELSE 0 END`, which is NetworkX's division exactly; a reciprocal `1 / c` overflows (22003) when `c` is subnormal, as HITS's maximum is with edge weights of 10⁻¹⁵⁵, where NetworkX succeeds (tested). Whether `vector_map` should skip tombstoned rows is §14 item 23.
- **`label_propagation_majority`** (a separate function: `label_propagation`'s min-label semantics, mask and signature stay as they are). NetworkX has no deterministic majority vote: `asynchronous_label_propagation` updates in random order with random ties, `label_propagation_communities` is semi-synchronous over a graph colouring. This is synchronous (Jacobi): each node takes the most frequent label among its own and its distinct neighbours' (direction ignored, as NetworkX's undirected-only LPA; a self-loop counts the node's own label once more), the smallest label on a tie, until no label changes. Counting the node's own label keeps a two-node component from swapping labels forever; a synchronous update can still cycle between two labellings (six nodes suffice, tested), and then stops at `max_iter` with the warning. Its oracle is a direct Python reimplementation of the rule (`test/harness/oracle.py`), compared label for label. Tier 2 (§6.4): one `gather` (`collect`, `both`) per iteration inside `vector_from_query`, whose query counts each node's received labels plus its own with `GROUP BY` and keeps the winner with `DISTINCT ON`, N + M rows through the executor per iteration. Edge weights are not read.
- **`clustering`** is `2T / (d(d − 1))` from `triangles` (directions and self-loops ignored) and `vector_degree(…, 'both')`, 0 where T = 0: `nx.clustering` on the undirected unweighted graph, within 10⁻¹² on a random graph (`test/sql/oracle_community.sql`). Directed graphs get the undirected coefficient, not NetworkX's directed (Fagiolo) one.
- **Missing primitives** (where the primitives were awkward; not worked around): (a) `vector_degree` counts a self-loop among a node's own neighbours and nothing reports which nodes have one, so `clustering` gives a node with a self-loop in a triangle a lower coefficient than NetworkX (documented and tested): a degree that leaves self-loops out, or the diagonal of A as a vector, would fix it. (b) `triangles` does not read weights, so there is no weighted clustering (NetworkX's geometric mean of the triangle's normalised weights needs a weighted triangle kernel). (c) `gather` does not read edge weights, so there is no weighted majority vote (NetworkX's asynchronous LPA takes `weight`). (d) Majority vote has no in-engine combiner: a `mode` combiner for `gather` (most frequent message, smallest on a tie) would make each iteration one kernel call instead of N + M rows through the executor (at 1M nodes and 20M stored edges an iteration takes ~20 s, against 40–90 ms for an iteration of the SpMV centralities; docs/limits.md).

Decisions made for the structural kernels (0.4.0, planned library group 2; `warren_core::compute::{core_number, strong_components, dag, spanning_forest}`, `pg_warren::compute_sql::structure`):
- *Kernels, not PL/pgSQL.* Peeling, Tarjan, Kahn and Borůvka are sequential walks with per-node state that semiring products cannot express in fewer than O(diameter) or O(N) supersteps, so each is a native kernel like `triangles`. Each runs as one job on the compute pool (the main thread polls interrupts every millisecond, §6.9), checks its cancellation flag every 65,536 units of work, a node or a row entry each, in every loop over the nodes or edges (`interrupts::Work`, shared with `triangles`), charges every graph-sized buffer to the call's budget, and reclaims for it ahead on the main thread (`ComputeMemory::reclaim_for`), since nothing is reclaimed on the pool. The exception is the row scratch `Matrix` needs when it reads several relations' CSRs (a selection, or all relations without a stored merged CSR): 16 B per entry of the longest such row, charged as it grows on the pool and not reclaimed for, as for a multi-relation product (§5.5). They are single-threaded: the walks are inherently ordered, and at 10M nodes each is within a small multiple of `triangles`, which runs on every thread (measured in `docs/limits.md`). None needs a reverse CSR.
- *What is read.* `core_number` reads `triangles`' symmetric adjacency (`compute::symmetric`: both directions of every edge under `rels`, self-loops dropped, rows sorted), so it is NetworkX's `core_number` of the undirected simple graph. The other kernels read the selected relations' forward rows: `strong_components` each relation's CSR (an edge in several relations is followed more than once, harmlessly); `topological_order`, `dag_longest_path` and `minimum_spanning_forest` through the products' `Matrix`, so an edge in several selected relations is one entry with its weights summed, exactly `A[i][j]` of §6.3, in either layout.
- `core_number(graph, out, rels, persist, version) RETURNS bigint`: Batagelj–Zaversnik bucket peeling, O(N + M) after the adjacency's sort; returns the degeneracy, the largest core number (0 without edges), rather than the vector id, since the vector's id is rarely wanted and the degeneracy otherwise costs a reduction. Scratch: the adjacency (16 B per stored edge, 8 per node) and three 8-byte arrays per node.
- `strong_components_vector(graph, out, rels, persist, version) RETURNS bigint`: Tarjan's algorithm with an explicit call stack (a frame is a node and its position across its rows), so a path of any length needs no recursion; the visit index is kept in the output vector until a component is labelled, so scratch is one lowlink per node and the two stacks, charged as they grow and reclaimed for at their largest (at most 56 B per node: the lowlink, and each stack's 24 B entry per node in a buffer that grows by doubling). Labels are the smallest dense id in the component, as `components` and `components_vector`; the name pairs it with `components_vector`. Returns the number of strong components among live nodes.
- `topological_order(graph, out, rels, persist, version) RETURNS bigint` (vector id): Kahn's algorithm with a binary heap of ready nodes, smallest dense id first, so the order is unique, deterministic and NetworkX's `lexicographical_topological_sort` keyed by dense id (by node id on a first build in `'id'` order). Positions count from 0 over live nodes. Level order (Kahn by waves) was the alternative: cheaper and parallel, but not a NetworkX order and with ties left to break anyway. A cycle (a self-loop included) is `ComputeError::Cycle`, 22023 (`invalid_parameter_value`, as `sssp_vector`'s negative cycle), naming how many live nodes are on or after a cycle; nothing is written.
- `dag_longest_path(graph, out, rels, weighted, persist, version) RETURNS float8`: the same pass, relaxing each edge as its source is taken: each node's longest path *ending* there, at least 0 because, as `nx.dag_longest_path` does, a path restarts rather than carry a negative prefix; returns the longest, `nx.dag_longest_path_length`. `weighted` as `sssp_vector`'s (NULL: weights if the graph has them, else every edge 1). A NaN weight never lengthens a path. No set-returning form of the path itself: it is recovered by walking back from the node of the largest length along edges where `length[u] + w = length[v]`, which a caller can do in SQL; added if asked for.
- `minimum_spanning_forest(graph, id_type anyelement, rels, weighted, version) RETURNS TABLE (source, target, weight)`: a set of edges, because a forest is edges and external ids may not be numeric, so a parent vector could not hold them; `id_type` as `components`'. `PARALLEL RESTRICTED`, `STABLE`, persists nothing. Directions are ignored: `u → v` and `v → u` are parallel undirected edges and the lighter can be chosen, i.e. Kruskal's algorithm on the undirected multigraph. Edges are ordered by weight in SQL order (NaN heaviest, `-0 = 0`), then by smaller and larger endpoint dense id: a strict total order, so the minimum forest is unique and independent of the algorithm (property-tested: the same undirected edges and weights as Kruskal's, compared as unordered pairs). Borůvka's rounds were chosen over Kruskal because they need no M-sized sorted edge list (2.4 GB at 100M edges): each round scans every stored edge once and keeps each component's lightest edge out in a per-node candidate array, then joins along them in a union–find, at most log₂ N rounds. Scratch: a parent and a candidate per node (40 B), and the forest (24 B per edge, in a buffer that grows by doubling). Rows come ordered by source and target dense id.
- *Measured* (`docs/limits.md`, release, 4 shared vCPUs, 10M nodes and 100M random edges, through SQL): `core_number` 23 s (`triangles`, on four threads, 43–45 s), `strong_components_vector` 17 s, `topological_order` 4.7–4.9 s, `dag_longest_path` 7.6–7.7 s, `minimum_spanning_forest` 56–64 s weighted and 35–36 s unweighted. *Open:* the forest is the slowest, a random access per stored edge per round on one thread. With reverse CSRs each node's lightest edge in either direction could be found in parallel and reduced per component in node order (the tie order would then need the source as a last key, so both paths choose the same orientation); not done until a user needs it faster.

Decisions made for the shortest-path centralities and neighbourhood similarity (0.4.0, planned library group 2; `warren_core::compute::{centrality, similarity}`, `pg_warren::compute_sql::{centrality, similarity}`):
- *Centralities, signatures.* `betweenness_centrality(graph, out, k DEFAULT NULL, seed DEFAULT 0, normalized DEFAULT true, endpoints DEFAULT false, directed DEFAULT true, sources DEFAULT NULL, rels, weighted DEFAULT NULL, persist, version) RETURNS bigint`; `closeness_centrality(graph, out, k, seed, wf_improved DEFAULT true, directed, sources, rels, weighted, persist, version)`; `harmonic_centrality(graph, out, k, seed, directed, sources, rels, weighted, persist, version)`. Each writes an `f64` vector and returns its number of sources. NetworkX's defaults, except `weighted`, which follows `spmv` (NULL: the graph's weights if it has them) as group 1's does, and `seed`, which defaults to 0 rather than NetworkX's unseeded `None`, so a call is deterministic unless asked otherwise. NULL arguments other than `k`, `sources`, `rels`, `weighted` and `version` are refused with 22004; `k < 1` with 22023; `k` larger than the population takes it all (NetworkX raises).
- *Sources.* `k := NULL` takes every live node: the exact centrality, O(N·(N + M)), for the oracle and small graphs. Otherwise `k` sources are a bottom-k sample of the population (live nodes, or those a width-1 vector `sources` selects, non-zero values as a mask): each node's key is a SplitMix64 hash of the seed and its dense id, and the `k` smallest are taken, so every `k`-subset is equally likely and the sample is the same on any thread count. A node keeps its key while it keeps its dense id (§5.2), so a refresh keeps the sample only if no new node's key is smaller than a sampled one's (it displaces the largest) and no sampled node is deleted; `compact` renumbers, and so re-keys, every node. The `sources` vector is how a caller chooses the sample in SQL (a non-uniform one: the estimators' scaling is then unbiased only as far as the selected nodes are typical), and how the oracle gives NetworkX the *same* sources: it passes them to `nx.betweenness_centrality` through a `random.Random` whose `sample` returns them, so sampled betweenness is compared exactly, not as two random estimates. Reproducing Python's Mersenne Twister sampling in Rust was the alternative; rejected as brittle across Python versions.
- *Paths.* Along edges (`directed := true`) the rows are `A`'s (§6.3: an edge in several selected relations is one entry, weights summed, as for products and `dag_longest_path`); `directed := false` is the undirected simple graph: unweighted through `triangles`' symmetric adjacency, weighted through a symmetric copy of `A` in which a pair joined both ways keeps the lighter of `A[i][j]`, `A[j][i]` (as `minimum_spanning_forest` reads it: an undirected multigraph's shortest paths take the lighter edge, as NetworkX's weight function does on multigraphs). Unweighted searches are breadth-first; weighted ones Dijkstra's algorithm with an indexed binary heap (one entry per node, decrease-key in place, ordered by distance then dense id; allocated with the search's scratch, so nothing grows on the pool: *PR #29 review*, a lazy-deletion queue grew there to an entry per improving relaxation, outside every bound reclaimed for), which needs positive weights and finite distances: a followed edge whose weight is not above 0 (NaN included), or a distance that overflows to +∞ (which would otherwise tie with another +∞), is `ComputeError::NonPositiveWeight` (22023). Self-loops are skipped before the check: they are on no shortest path. NetworkX accepts zero weights but then counts paths through an already-settled node, which Brandes' order cannot; refused rather than reproduced. A path's distance is summed in the same order as NetworkX's (`d(v) + w`), so equal-length paths tie exactly as there.
- *Brandes, batched.* Each source's search records the reached nodes in order of distance, their path counts σ and distances; the dependency pass walks them farthest first and re-reads each row, taking the edges `v → w` with `d(v) + w = d(w)`, so no predecessor lists (M entries per source) are kept. Sources run in rounds of one per compute thread (each with its own scratch: distance, σ, δ and order, 32 bytes per node; 16 for closeness and harmonic), and after each round the contributions are folded into the totals node by node in ascending source order, in parallel over nodes. LAGraph batches sources as a matrix of frontiers (SpGEMM); one source per thread needs no SpGEMM and keeps each search's work proportional to what it reaches. The fixed fold order makes the result bit-identical for any `warren.threads` (tested); the cost is a dense fold of 8 bytes per node per source, and each round waits for its slowest source.
- *Betweenness rescaling* is NetworkX 3.7's `_rescale` exactly, with `n` the live node count: normalised by the pairs a path through `v` could join, unnormalised undirected halved, and a sample of `k` scaled up by `n / k`-style factors in which, without endpoints, a sampled source (never its own path's end) is scaled by one source fewer (NaN when `k = 1` and normalised, as NetworkX). A sample of every live node is the exact form, as NetworkX's `k == len(G)`.
- *Closeness and harmonic.* NetworkX uses the distances *to* each node on a digraph (closeness reverses the graph; harmonic sums `1/d(source, u)`), so a forward search from each source accumulates into the nodes it reaches: no reverse CSR is needed. Exact: closeness `(r − 1)/Σd · (r − 1)/(n − 1)` with `wf_improved`, harmonic `Σ 1/d`. From a sample `S`, with `k_u = |S \ {u}|` and `m_u` of those reaching `u`: closeness `m_u / Σ_{s∈S} d(s, u)`, times `m_u / k_u` with `wf_improved`; harmonic `(n − 1)/k_u · Σ_{s∈S} 1/d(s, u)`. These are the exact formulas with the sample mean standing for the population's (Eppstein–Wang): for a uniform sample conditionally unbiased for the sums, and exact when `S` is every live node. The error bound (`docs/compute.md`) is in `k_u`: within ε·Δ in mean distance with probability `1 − 2e^(−2 k_u ε²)` (Hoeffding, valid without replacement), only for a node every node reaches (else Δ is infinite and the estimate is of the mean over the nodes that reach it); harmonic's terms lie in [0, 1] only when every weight is at least 1; and a `sources` vector's sample is not uniform, so the `(n − 1)/k_u` and NetworkX's `n/k` scalings are then not unbiased. The oracle computes the estimator over NetworkX's own distances.
- *Similarity, surface.* Three set-returning functions of external ids, `STABLE PARALLEL RESTRICTED`, persisting nothing: `similarity(graph, pairs text, id_type anyelement, measure DEFAULT 'jaccard', rels, version) RETURNS TABLE (source, target, score)` scores the pairs a query returns (NetworkX's `ebunch`; run as the caller under their `search_path`, read-only, as `vector_from_query`'s query; rows in the query's order; NULL, unknown or tombstoned ids skipped, as external ids are elsewhere, where NetworkX raises); `most_similar(graph, node anyelement, k DEFAULT 10, measure, rels, version) RETURNS TABLE (node_id, score)` ranks one node's candidates; `similar_pairs(graph, id_type, k DEFAULT 10, measure, nodes DEFAULT NULL, rels, version)` ranks each node's (or each node a width-1 vector `nodes` selects as a mask), its rows charged as they are found (*PR #29 review*: the worst case, ranked × k × 24 bytes, was charged up front, so `k = 1000` at 10M nodes was refused). `measure` is `jaccard`, `adamic_adar` or `common_neighbours` (else 22023). All-pairs scoring is not offered: N² rows. A candidate is a non-neighbour other than the node sharing at least one neighbour (NetworkX's default `ebunch`, the non-edges, less the zero scores), ranked by score, then dense id.
- *Similarity, semantics.* The undirected simple graph under `rels`, as `triangles` (directions and self-loops ignored). Jaccard `|N(u)∩N(v)| / |N(u)∪N(v)|` with 0 for an empty union (NetworkX's rule); Adamic–Adar summed in ascending dense id of the common neighbour, `1/ln |N(w)|`. A common neighbour of two distinct nodes has degree ≥ 2, so `ln 1 = 0` arises only for a node paired with itself, where NetworkX raises `ZeroDivisionError` (`1 / log(1)` in Python); here the term is IEEE's `1/+0 = +∞`, the pair's score +∞, rather than an error for the whole call.
- *Similarity, kernels.* Pairs: two sorted rows intersected by `compute::symmetric::shared` (`triangles`' merge, or binary search for a row 16× longer, now reporting each common neighbour), in parallel tasks of pairs. Rankings: a two-hop expansion `u → w → v`, tallying shared neighbours and Adamic–Adar terms per candidate, `Σ_{w∈N(u)} |N(w)|` steps (so `similar_pairs` costs `Σ_w |N(w)|²` over the graph: quadratic in hub degree; a degree cutoff for intermediaries, as other systems offer, is not provided). One node's ranking runs on the calling thread after the adjacency (if built) returns from the pool, so its candidate list, rows and best-`k` heap (capped at the candidates found) reclaim held memory as they grow; its candidates are collected and sorted; each node's are tallied in dense per-thread arrays (20 bytes per node per thread, allocated and charged on the main thread) with threads taking the next task of nodes from a shared counter, so a hub's task holds back no one. Rows are read node by node from the forward and reverse CSRs (sorted, deduplicated per call) when every selected relation has a reverse CSR, for `similarity` and `most_similar`, on the calling thread (its interrupts polled through `interrupts::Work::polling`) so the row buffers reclaim as they grow; built rows are read in place on the pool, with no buffer: an interactive "most similar to this node" costs its two-hop neighbourhood, not the graph. Otherwise, and for `similar_pairs`, the symmetric adjacency is built once (16 bytes per stored edge, 8 per node for degrees), reclaimed for ahead.
- *Measured* (`docs/limits.md`, release, 4 shared vCPUs, G(n, m) with 10 edges per node): a source costs about 0.8 s on one thread at 1M nodes and 20–30 s at 10M (memory-bound random reads), so betweenness from 16 sources at 10M nodes and 100M edges takes 94–130 s unweighted and 216 s weighted, closeness 58 s; exact betweenness at 1M nodes would take days. `most_similar` takes milliseconds at 10M nodes (rows read node by node), 100,000 pairs 0.44 s, and `similar_pairs` over every node 12 s at 1M and 318 s (100M rows) at 10M. *Open:* a source's search is single-threaded and memory-bound; 4-byte distances, or a level-synchronous parallel search per source when `k` is below the thread count, would help, as would a degree cutoff for `similar_pairs`' intermediaries; not done until needed.

Decisions made for Louvain and Leiden (0.4.0, planned library group 3; `warren_core::community::{modularity, Undirected, local_moving, refine}`, `pg_warren::community_sql::{modularity, local_moving, library}`):
- *A library over two kernels.* `louvain` and `leiden` are PL/pgSQL (`community_sql/library/{louvain,leiden}.sql`, the M5 rules: version pinned, transient `_warren_<function>_<part>` intermediates dropped on failure or cancel, `search_path` pinned) over `local_moving`, `leiden_refine`, `contract`, `project`, `lift` and `modularity`. Memory: each level's `local_moving` and `leiden_refine` charge their scratch to `warren.max_compute_memory`, and its `contract` its build scratch to `warren.max_build_memory`, so a run's peak is bounded by the cache, compute and build limits together (measured, §14 item 20). A level's two phases are kernels because each node's move depends on the moves before it: no semiring product expresses a sweep. Aggregation is `contract` (§6.6), whose quotient is a graph version like any other. Every level's graph is a quotient of `graph` itself, contracted by the composed partition found so far (`PᵀAP` composes, so it is the graph contracting the level below would give): each level lifts its partition to `graph` once (`lift`), contracts `graph` by it, and drops the level before, so one level's graph exists at a time (rolled back with the run on an error). The first version contracted each level's graph into the next and kept the chain, lifting each level's partition down all of it: k lifts at level k and every level on disk at once, which at 10M nodes filled the disk during Leiden's fifteen-odd levels. Level `k > 1` labels by the quotient's external ids, the least dense id of each community's members in `graph` (a `keys` vector from `id_map`), so the labels of every level and of `out` are dense ids of `graph`; each level's partition is kept as `<levels_prefix>_<level>` when asked.
- *Level graphs and reserved names* (PR #30 review). The first version contracted into `_warren_<function>_<backend pid>_<level>`: a graph of that name the caller owned got a version and was then dropped (data loss), and a reader could create one to make the owner's runs fail. Graph names beginning with `_warren_` are now reserved, as vector names are (§6.2): `create_graph` and `contract` refuse them (42939, `reserved_name`). The library makes its levels with `contract(graph, labels, NULL)`, which never adds to an existing graph: it creates a new graph of the caller's, builds the version, and names the graph `_warren_level_<version id>` (version ids are never reused, so no graph can have had the name; an existing one is refused, 42710), and the library derives the name from the id `contract` returns. Users may call it too: it can only make a fresh graph of their own. A path users cannot call at all was the alternative, but in a trusted install the library runs as the caller (`SECURITY INVOKER`), so anything it can call the caller can, short of `SECURITY DEFINER` functions owned by the extension's owner, which would own the level graphs and run the caller's SQL elevated (§7). Graphs named `_warren_…` before 0.4.0 keep working; only new ones are refused.
- Signatures: `louvain(graph, out, resolution DEFAULT 1.0, seed DEFAULT 0, max_levels DEFAULT 100, tolerance DEFAULT 1e-7, weighted DEFAULT NULL, levels_prefix DEFAULT NULL, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS float8` (the partition's modularity; `leiden` adds `randomness DEFAULT 0.01` and `iterations DEFAULT 2` after `tolerance`); `modularity(graph, labels, resolution, weighted, version) RETURNS float8` (`STABLE`, `PARALLEL RESTRICTED`); `local_moving(graph, out, initial, keys, resolution, seed, max_sweeps DEFAULT 100, tolerance DEFAULT 1e-7, weighted, persist, version) RETURNS bigint` and `leiden_refine(graph, communities, out, keys, resolution, seed, randomness, weighted, persist, version) RETURNS bigint` (the number of communities). Labels are integer vectors, refused as `contract` refuses them; a negative or NaN weight is refused (22023), as is a resolution that is not a finite number of at least 0.
- *The undirected view* (`community::Undirected`): the symmetric matrix `B` of the merged relations, built on the pool within the call's budget (16 B per entry and per node, reclaimed for ahead). A directed graph is `B = A + Aᵀ` (an edge and its reverse add up; a self-loop counts twice), an undirected one `B = A`: a self-loop counts **once** in its node's degree, as in `vector_degree` and `contract`'s `PᵀAP`.
