Both are closed under contraction (`B` of the quotient is `PᵀBP`), so a partition's modularity on a graph equals that of the singletons on its quotient (a property test), which is what makes the levels consistent. NetworkX counts an undirected self-loop twice, so on an undirected graph `modularity` is NetworkX's with each self-loop's weight halved (`test/sql/oracle_modularity.sql` checks exactly that, on a random graph with self-loops); on a directed graph it is NetworkX's of `nx.MultiGraph(G)`, the undirected view, not NetworkX's of `G`, which is Leicht and Newman's directed modularity (A_ij − γ k_i^out k_j^in / m): Louvain and Leiden as written here maximise the undirected one, as NetworkX's own `louvain_communities` does not on a digraph (PR #30 review). Counting it twice was the alternative, but then `contract` and `modularity` would disagree, and a level's modularity would not be its parent's (PR #28 review).
- *Deterministic parallel local moving.* Sequential local moving is ordered: each node decides against the moves before it. The kernel colours the nodes greedily in a seeded order (Grappolo, Lu et al. 2015: no two neighbours share a colour) and sweeps colour by colour, in blocks of 65,536 nodes: a colour's nodes are not adjacent, so their weights to neighbouring communities do not change while they decide, and a block decides in parallel against the state at its start; its moves are then applied one by one in a fixed order, each re-checked against the communities' degree sums as they then are and kept only if it still gains. Modularity rises with every move applied, the run ends, and the result is the same for any thread count and fixed by the seed (SplitMix64 of seed and node, `community::random`). *Fast local moving* (Traag et al.'s queue): after the first sweep, a sweep decides only for the nodes woken since they last decided, the neighbours of a moved node outside the community it joined; the level's moving ends when a sweep moves nothing (no node is then woken), gains no more than `tolerance`, or after `max_sweeps`. A move also changes the gains of non-neighbours through their communities' degree sums, which no wake covers, so the end is not a strict local optimum. Sweeping every node until none moved (NetworkX's rule) was the first version: at 10M nodes its level 1 took 279 s, about ninety full sweeps for a tail of a few moves each; on a 1M-node block graph the queue takes 1.3 s against 10.9 s, for level-1 modularity 0.1259 against 0.1261 (a sweep of the woken followed by a full sweep whenever it moved nothing, to keep the optimum strict, still ran into `max_sweeps`). The alternatives were a sequential sweep (exact Louvain, one thread) and unsynchronised parallel moves (not deterministic, and modularity can fall: two neighbours swap communities). Scratch on the pool: 56 B per node at most (state 16; the start's modularity 40, before the colouring's 24 and the labelling's 16), reclaimed for ahead.
- *Leiden's refinement* is Traag et al.'s Algorithm A.2 for modularity: within each community nodes start alone; a well-connected node still alone merges into a well-connected refined community of its community with probability ∝ exp(ΔH/θ) over the non-negative gains (`θ = randomness`; 0 takes the best). ΔH = E(v, C) − γ k_v K_C / 2m is in units of edge weight, modularity's change times m, as Traag et al.'s and igraph's are, so θ = 0.01 nearly always takes the best merge; the first version passed modularity's change itself, about 1/m smaller, so θ = 0.01 drew almost uniformly, as θ = 1 does (PR #30 review; a test that θ = 0.01 beats θ = 1 on planted blocks), drawn from a counter-based function of seed and node, so communities refine in parallel and the result is the same for any thread count. Every refined community is connected (a property test; `oracle_louvain*.sql` check every Leiden community's connectedness through `components`). The next level contracts by the refined partition and starts from the unrefined one (`project(…, 'min')`: a refined community's members share one community). Every exit returns a connected partition: when a level's moving leaves every node alone, the partition is that level's nodes, the refined partition of the level below; when it stops at `max_levels` or because the refinement merged nothing, it returns that level's refined partition, as Traag et al.'s guarantee needs, not the moved one, in which a node that left could have split its old community (PR #30 review; a pg_test that `max_levels := 1` returns the refinement of that level's moving, and that every community is connected however it stops).
- *Termination.* Louvain stops when a level merges nothing, when a level raises modularity by no more than `tolerance` (NetworkX's `threshold`), or after `max_levels`; its levels move with tolerance 0, as NetworkX's do. Leiden stops as Traag et al.'s and igraph's do, when there is nothing to aggregate: when a level's moving leaves every node alone, when the refinement does (the next level's graph would be this one), or after `max_levels`; `tolerance` ends each level's moving. Louvain's rule stopped Leiden at the first level whose moving gained nothing although the next, coarser level could still gain, and left it below Louvain (0.770 against 0.798 on sparse blocks; a pg_test).
- *Iterations* (igraph's `n_iterations`, leidenalg's `optimise_partition` loop, Traag et al.'s iterated Leiden). `iterations DEFAULT 2`, igraph's default: that multilevel pass is one iteration, and each later one starts its first level's moving from the partition the iteration before returned instead of from singletons (`local_moving(…, initial)`); its refinement still starts from singletons within each community. Every iteration uses the same seed, so an iteration whose partition equals its start would repeat itself: the run stops after it (compared label for label over the live nodes, which is partition equality, since every label is the least dense id of its community's members). A negative value runs until then, as igraph's does, at most 100 iterations, after which it returns the last partition with a `WARNING` (`leiden did not stabilise in 100 iterations`), as the centralities warn (group 1); 0 is refused (22023), NULL (22004). `max_levels` and `tolerance` apply to each iteration. Each iteration ends with a connected partition (the exits above), so the run does, and it returns the last iteration's modularity. `levels_prefix` numbers every iteration's levels in turn (the first iteration's are as before; the last level is `out`), so the levels written show how many iterations ran (a pg_test: on a ring of cliques the second iteration changes nothing, and 3 and -1 write the same levels as 2). `iterations := 1` is the pass before this decision, to the bit (a pg_test pins its modularity on the smoke graph). A fresh seed per iteration, as igraph's running RNG gives, was the alternative; it would make stopping on an unchanged iteration a guess rather than exact.
- *Measured* (`bench/results/communities/`, release, 4 shared vCPUs, the §6.6 block graph: N nodes in blocks of 1,000, 5N undirected edges, unweighted, each call in a fresh session; a level's time is the difference of runs with `max_levels` 1, 2 and 3; after the PR #30 fixes): at 1M nodes `louvain` 5.8 s (3 levels: 2.4, 2.8, 0.3 s; modularity 0.837, 474 communities) and `leiden` 8.3 s (7 levels: 2.3, 3.2, 1.9 s, then 0.9 s; modularity 0.837, 482 communities); at 10M nodes `louvain` 93 s (5 levels: 31.6, 49.5, 11.8 s, then under 0.1 s; modularity 0.840, 1,425 communities) and `leiden` 119 s (8 levels: 29.4, 49.2, 22.3 s, then 18.2 s; modularity 0.840, 1,446 communities). Level 1, on the whole graph, is the undirected view, the starting modularity, the colouring and the sweeps; each later level adds a contraction of the whole graph. Before the θ fix Leiden ran 14 levels at 1M (13.4 s), its near-uniform draws keeping refined communities small. Those Leiden figures are one iteration (`iterations := 1` now). With the default two (`blocks-1m-iterations.json`, a later run on a machine slower that day: `louvain`, unchanged, 9.1 s, and one iteration 10.4 s for the same partition) `leiden` takes 20.9 s at 1M, 13 levels, about twice one iteration, for modularity 0.898 (852 communities) against one iteration's 0.837; until stable (`-1`) 0.898 in 40.7 s, 31 levels. The second iteration writes as much again: 516 MB of segments and 691 MB of WAL against 294 MB and 472 MB in the same session. The 10M figures above are one iteration's. With the default two (`blocks-10m-defaults.json`, the 0.4.0 soak's scratch cluster) `leiden` takes 246.5 s at 10M, 18 levels, for modularity 0.8996 (the planted blocks: 0.8995; 5,528 communities), peak private memory 5.28 GB, writing 7.8 GB of segments and 8.2 GB of WAL; `louvain` in the same run 115.4 s (the same partition as above), 4.31 GB, 2.5 GB and 2.6 GB. On the soak's 10M-node random graphs (§12) neither completes at the default limits: `leiden` is refused (53400) in its first refinement and `louvain` was stopped for disk after writing 7.5 GB of segments. *Disk.* A level's graph is a version (segments and WAL); one dropped during the run keeps its rows until `VACUUM` after the run's one transaction, so a run's disk grows with its levels: on the 1M-node graph (75 MB of segments) `louvain` wrote 166 MB of segments and about 190 MB of WAL, `leiden` 294 MB and 323 MB; at 10M nodes the database held 6.7 GB after the matrix's four `louvain` runs and 9.1 GB after its four `leiden` runs (graph included, before any `VACUUM`). *Deferred past 0.4.0 (maintainer's decision):* a native kernel holding Louvain's or Leiden's levels in memory (contract and move without writing versions, persisting only the result) would remove that cost and the per-level contraction of `graph`. It is not built because the PL/pgSQL library is the readable, copyable template users adapt into algorithms of their own, and because the per-level disk and WAL cost belongs to `contract`, not to these two: the general fix is to let `contract` keep a quotient in memory for the rest of the statement instead of persisting it, which serves every multilevel algorithm users write. *Open:* that in-memory `contract`. *Quality.* The planted blocks score 0.899 at 1M: on blocks this sparse (about ten edges a node) greedy moving merges across blocks early, and one pass of either algorithm stops at 0.837; Leiden's second iteration, which moves nodes out of those merges, reaches 0.898. On the bench's 20-block smoke graph (planted 0.855) NetworkX's `louvain_communities` scores 0.796 and ours 0.795; igraph's `community_leiden` (Traag's implementation, one iteration) 0.799 at β = 0.01, 0.805 at β = 1 and 0.811 at β = 100, and ours with `iterations := 1` 0.795, 0.804 and 0.815 (scored by igraph, which counts a self-loop twice): the same trend, as the θ fix should give. igraph with its default two iterations (a second pass from the first's partition) finds the planted 0.855, and so does ours with `iterations`' default of 2: 0.855 as igraph scores it, igraph's own figure, the planted blocks with one node moved (by `warren.modularity` 0.7943 for one iteration, and 0.85460666914 for two against the planted 0.85460666889); the third iteration changes nothing, so `iterations := -1` stops there with the same partition. A pg_test checks one iteration's figure and two's against the planted partition on this graph. Not in CI, which has no igraph.
- *Oracles* (`test/sql/oracle_louvain.sql`, `oracle_louvain_sbm20.sql`, weighted stochastic block models of 12 and 20 planted communities): each function's modularity is at least NetworkX's `louvain_communities`' less 0.01, the NMI against the planted partition at least 0.9 and 0.95, the modularity each returns equals that of its partition computed from the edges in SQL to 10⁻¹², `warren.modularity` scores NetworkX's partition as NetworkX does to 10⁻¹², the graph stored directed gives the same partition, and Leiden's communities are connected.

### 6.8 Tier 3 — WASM vertex programs (v1.2+)

For state that must be per-vertex and bytes-shaped with logic that neither SQL nor contraction expresses well, accept a WASM module exporting `init(vid)`, `compute(vid, messages_ptr, len)`, `combine(a, b)`; the engine runs it in a tight loop per superstep with `wasmtime`, messages marshalled through linear memory via the same `Message` trait as tier 2. Expect roughly 10–50× slower than tier 1. Deferred until tiers 1–2 and §6.6 have shown which programs users cannot express.

### 6.9 Parallelism

- A Postgres backend is single-threaded and **no thread other than the main one may call any Postgres function** (palloc, elog, SPI, catalog access, …). Pure compute over extension-owned buffers (CSR, vectors, message buffers) is safe on worker threads. `warren-core` therefore takes plain slices and a thread count, and uses `rayon` with a dedicated pool sized by the `warren.threads` GUC (default: half the cores, min 1). The binding layer does all Postgres interaction before and after each kernel call — including, for `gather`, reading the message relation through SPI on the main thread before fan-out and emitting result rows after.
- *Implemented (M4, `compute_sql::settings`):* `warren.threads` (`USERSET`, 1–1024, default half of `available_parallelism`, at least 1) sizes one compute pool per backend, built on first use and rebuilt when the setting changes. The pool's threads are started with every signal blocked, so Postgres's handlers (which touch backend state non-atomically) only ever run on the main thread. The main-thread poll is `pg_warren::interrupts::PostgresInterrupts`, shared with traversal; a cancel marked while a kernel runs stops it with `query_canceled` (tested). `Workers::run` catches the unwind of the ERROR that `CHECK_FOR_INTERRUPTS` raises in the poll (rayon's scope does), waits for the job to see its cancellation flag and return, then resumes the unwind. That is sound only because nothing calls into Postgres between the catch and the resume; no Postgres call may be added to that window. The test marks the cancel from the main-thread poll, so no worker thread ever touches Postgres state.
- Interrupt handling: the main thread polls `CHECK_FOR_INTERRUPTS()` between kernel calls and sets a cancellation flag that worker loops check every ~64K vertices. **Implemented (M4):** `compute::Workers::run` runs a kernel as a job on the pool while the calling (main) thread waits, polling the `interrupts::Interrupts` hook (shared with traversal) every millisecond. When a poll says stop, or unwinds (pgrx raising the ERROR), a drop guard raises the job's `Cancellation` flag and the rayon scope waits for the job to stop before the error propagates. The scope catches that unwind (`halt_unwinding`), waits for the job, then resumes it; this is the one place pg_warren holds a caught Postgres ERROR, and it is sound only because nothing between the catch and the resume calls into Postgres. A panicking job marks itself finished from a drop guard, so the scope returns at once and re-raises the panic, which pgrx turns into an ERROR. Worker tasks check the flag at the start of each task of at most 4,096 rows (4,096 elements for `vector_map`, 65,536 for reductions); the push scatter checks it every 65,536 rows, as do `gather`'s sequential sorts (outbox, inbox). Use `pgrx::check_for_interrupts!()`. pgrx's `pg_sys` FFI is `#[pg_guard]`ed, so an ERROR raised inside it unwinds the Rust frames (destructors run, `RefCell` borrows release). Never call unguarded FFI. *A FATAL during a kernel (M9 step 5 review):* `pg_terminate_backend` raises a FATAL from the main thread's poll, which exits the process without unwinding, so nothing joins the pool while its threads may still read a shared version's segment, which Postgres unmaps as it exits (a crash, and a restart of the cluster). `Workers` records its running job, and `Workers::stop` cancels it and waits for it; the first pool a backend builds registers a `before_shmem_exit` callback (`compute_sql::settings`), which runs before Postgres unmaps anything and stops every pool of the backend still in use. `test/sql/shared_cache_exit_mid_kernel.sql` terminates a backend mid-kernel and checks that the cluster did not restart.
- Errors are raised only through pgrx (`ErrorReport` with a standard SQLSTATE; detail and hint as needed), never by calling `pg_sys` error functions directly. Nothing in `pg_warren` uses `catch_unwind` around SPI; pgrx already installs the boundary at each `#[pg_extern]`.
- Memory for vectors, message buffers and work buffers is allocated with the Rust allocator, not `palloc`, and accounted against a `warren.max_compute_memory` GUC; exceeding it raises an error rather than OOMing the server (*M4:* per call, §6.2). Graph-sized buffers are reserved with `try_reserve`, so allocator failure is also an error (`53200`), not an abort.
- v1.1: a **compute background worker** that owns the DSM-resident graph and runs jobs submitted by backends via a shared queue, so compute does not compete with query backends and large jobs can outlive a client session. Results land in `warren.vectors`; `warren.job_status()` reports progress.
- GPU: the semiring/SpMV formulation is what cuSPARSE/GraphBLAST expect; a GPU backend for `warren-core` is a contained future addition behind the kernel seam of §6.10.

### 6.10 GraphBLAS: read, not imported

**Decided (M7, §14 item 9): pg_warren does not bind or import SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS, LAGraph or OneSparse.** Every kernel, today's and §6.11's, is written natively in `warren-core`. GraphBLAS is read as §4A reads all prior art: a source of lessons and algorithms, credited in `NOTICE` and in a comment where one is adopted. SuiteSparse is the mature implementation of the semiring model, and the decision is not a claim that native kernels are faster than it. It is that pg_warren's context differs from the one GraphBLAS was built for, and a binding would cost the properties that context needs.

**The different context, concretely.**
- *Trusted install and hosted providers (§7).* pg_warren needs nothing at run time beyond Postgres (§11), so a hosted provider ships one extension built from one Rust workspace. A binding adds libgraphblas, and LAGraph for its algorithms (OneSparse links both, prior-art notes §2.1), for every provider and packager to build, vet and update. SuiteSparse's fast paths for operators outside its precompiled kernels come from a JIT that runs a C compiler at run time (OneSparse exposes it as `onesparse.jit_control`, prior-art notes §2.1), which a hosted server would turn off.
- *One allocator and the main-thread rule (§6.9).* Every graph-sized buffer is Rust-allocated, charged to `ComputeMemory` before `try_reserve`, and runs on one `rayon` pool sized by `warren.threads`, whose threads block every signal. GraphBLAS brings a second thread pool (OpenMP: OneSparse runs it with no thread-count setting and no memory accounting, prior-art notes §2.1, §2.4) and its own allocations, so both limits and the signal rule would need to be re-established at a C boundary.
- *Cancellation.* Every native kernel checks a cancellation flag at least every 4,096 rows or 65,536 entries, and `Workers::run` polls the interrupts every millisecond (§6.9). A GraphBLAS operation cannot be stopped once called, so a long `mxm` could not be cancelled (OneSparse has no interrupt check at all, prior-art notes §2.1).
- *Semantics.* pg_warren's semirings follow SQL (§6.3): `min`/`max` in SQL order with NaN greatest, `bigint out of range` on `i64` overflow, the `i64` identities absorbing `plus` and `times`, and weights cast as SQL casts `double precision`. GraphBLAS operators are C functions with no error return, so overflow cannot be raised from one. Matching these semantics would mean user-defined operators, which run outside the precompiled kernels.
- *Versions and stable ids.* A product reads a version's CSRs in place: stable dense ids, `u32` or `u64` targets, per-relation CSRs and the stored or on-the-fly merged rows (§5.3), held in the `Arc`-owned cache (§5.5). A binding would import each version into GraphBLAS's own matrix form, a second copy per version that the cache would also have to count.
- *`gather`'s variable-width messages.* Tier 2 (§6.4) combines `Bytes` messages and `collect`s SQL values. GraphBLAS has no counterpart, so `gather` would stay native in any case, beside a second kernel stack.

**Evidence that native kernels are not what binds.** SpMV pulls 0.38–0.42 G edges/s on one thread and 1.2 G on four (`benches/compute.rs`, 100K nodes, 1M edges, `plus_times`). Re-run for this decision at f29818d on the same shared 4-vCPU Xeon (load average 1.7–2.3): pull forward / reverse 0.42 / 0.42 G edges/s on one thread and 1.19 / 1.22 G on four; push without a reverse CSR 0.20 and 0.21 G (median of criterion's estimate). At the M7 soak's scale (10M nodes, 100M edges, 4 shared vCPUs), `pagerank`'s 20 iterations take 15.2–16.7 s (`bench/results/soak/a.json`, `a-rerun.json`), inside §12's 30 s target, which assumes 8 cores. That is ~0.8 s per iteration for every call it makes, products and maps included. Out of a core's cache (4M nodes, 40M edges, `benches/spmv_out_of_cache.rs`), the product pulls 0.12–0.13 G edges/s on one thread (f64; 0.14–0.17 G for f32), about 2.2× below the in-cache rate under the same load, but before the no-NULL path (§6.3) `pagerank`'s four maps cost more than its product (340–390 vs ~310 ms of a ~800 ms iteration on one thread). Cache blocking the product (CSR segmenting) was therefore not pursued: at best it would save ~15–25% of an iteration, and per-segment row offsets would add ~1.6–3.2 GB at §12's scale. A product task is a fixed 4,096 rows, so hub-first node order is no faster at four threads (hubs make a few tasks heavy). `eigen` misses on time: ~14,500 steps are projected at 10M on a random graph (§12), and at the measured step cost even SpMV alone exceeds the target (§14 item 21), so a faster product kernel would not close it either. BFS-versus-CTE includes emitting the SRF's rows. `reachable_set` (11–12M edges/s) is a traversal whose frontier has no sparse form. That last one is §6.11 item 2, which the native work below targets. OneSparse's published 7 G edges/s BFS on GAP graphs (prior-art notes §2.1) comes from SuiteSparse's direction-optimising BFS on unstated hardware. It is the ceiling to learn from, not a measurement against this machine. Nothing was measured against SuiteSparse itself.

**What pg_warren adopts from GraphBLAS and LAGraph** (each credited where used):
- *Vocabulary and descriptors.* Already adopted (§6.3): `<add>_<mult>` semirings, value masks, `c` (`mask_complement`), `r` (`replace`), `t0` (`transpose`), accumulators as binary operators. §6.11's operations take the same names: `mxm` for SpGEMM, `eadd`/`emult` for elementwise matrix add and multiply, `extract` and `select` (OneSparse's `choose`). They add the descriptors they need: `t1` (transpose the second operand) and `s`, a structural mask (an entry is selected if it exists, whatever its value), which sparse masks make meaningful.
- *Masked SpGEMM* (§6.11 item 1). The mask is applied during the product, never after, so `C⟨M⟩ = A ⊕.⊗ B` costs about the work the mask selects rather than the full product. Following SuiteSparse, each row (or task of rows) chooses its method from a flop estimate. Gustavson's row-by-row saxpy uses a dense accumulator for dense rows and a hash accumulator for sparse ones. The masked dot product, `C⟨M⟩ = AᵀB` evaluated only at `M`'s entries, is the method when the mask is sparser than the product: a sorted-row intersection, as `triangles` already is (§6.7), and LAGraph's masked triangle-count and k-truss formulations (`C⟨L⟩ = L ⊕.⊗ Lᵀ`) show the shapes. Contraction's `PᵀAP` (§6.6), where `P` has one entry per row, is the first SpGEMM: Gustavson's row-by-row product specialised to that `P` (members grouped by community, a dense accumulator row per community), whose output then goes through the build pipeline (§5.4).
- *Sparse vectors and push/pull switching* (§6.11 item 2). A vector is held sparse (sorted ids and values), as a bitmap or dense, by its fill, as SuiteSparse switches its sparse, bitmap and full formats. A product over a sparse input pushes (saxpy over the forward CSR's rows for the frontier). It pulls (the dense rows over the reverse CSR, with the unvisited set as a complemented mask) once the frontier's edges pass a fraction of M. Beamer's direction-optimising rule, as LAGraph's BFS uses it, switches back when the frontier shrinks below a fraction of N. The thresholds are measured, starting from the ~N/20 of §10. This is what `reachable_set` and `components` need.
- *Cached properties.* LAGraph caches a graph's transpose and degrees. OneSparse loses them on every store (prior-art notes §2.2). pg_warren already persists the reverse CSR per version, and later per-version properties (degree vectors, a symmetric adjacency for `triangles`) follow that rule rather than being rebuilt per call.
- *Algorithms as library code over the primitives.* LAGraph writes BFS, connected components (FastSV), PageRank and betweenness (batched Brandes via SpMM) over GraphBLAS calls. That is how §6.7's PL/pgSQL library is written, and those formulations are the templates for its later functions.

The GPU route is unaffected (§6.11): a GPU backend is a second implementation of the kernel seam below, not a property of a binding. The trusted-install and cancellation reasons apply to it too, so it would be an optional build feature that hosted providers can leave out, cancellable only between launches or chunks, never the only implementation of an operation.

**The kernel seam** (defined in M7; not yet code). It is the boundary between `pg_warren`'s compute adapters and whatever runs a kernel: the native CPU kernels now, a GPU backend later. Today it exists as free functions with one shape, `spmv`/`spmm(workers, memory, interrupts, graph, product, x, y)`, `gather`, `triangles`, `map` and `reduce` in `warren_core::compute`, so callers already pass plain slices and a budget. The follow-up names it as a trait in a `compute::kernels` module, implemented by `Native` (which owns the `Workers` pool):

```rust
/// What a kernel call is given besides its operands.
pub struct Call<'a> {
    /// Every graph-sized buffer, on the host or a device, is charged here
    /// before it is reserved.
    pub memory: &'a ComputeMemory,
    /// The main thread's poll (DESIGN.md §6.9).
    pub interrupts: &'a mut dyn Interrupts,
}

/// Runs compute kernels over a version's CSRs.
pub trait Kernels: Send + Sync {
    /// Whether this backend runs `operation` over `dtype` operands; the
    /// adapter falls back to `Native` otherwise.
    fn serves(&self, operation: Operation, dtype: Dtype) -> bool;

    /// `y = A ⊗ x` or `Aᵀ ⊗ x` for width-1 vectors (§6.3).
    fn spmv(&self, call: &mut Call<'_>, graph: &GraphCsr, product: &Product<'_>,
            x: &AnyVector, y: &mut AnyVector) -> Result<(), ComputeError>;

    /// `Y = A ⊗ X` or `Aᵀ ⊗ X` for width-d matrices (§6.3).
    fn spmm(&self, call: &mut Call<'_>, graph: &GraphCsr, product: &Product<'_>,
            x: &AnyVector, y: &mut AnyVector) -> Result<(), ComputeError>;
}
```

`Operation` is an enum of the seam's operations (`Spmv`, `Spmm`, and those added below). §6.11's operations join the trait when they are built, each with a native implementation first. Masked SpGEMM is `fn mxm(&self, call, a: Operand<'_>, b: Operand<'_>, product: &MatrixProduct<'_>) -> Result<SparseMatrix, ComputeError>`, where an `Operand` is a selection of a version's CSRs (or a matrix value, §6.11 item 3) with its `t0`/`t1` orientation, and `MatrixProduct` is `Product`'s semiring, mask (a structural or value mask over a sparse matrix), `replace` and `accumulate`. The sparse-vector product is `spmspv`, over a `SparseVector` input and output. `gather` stays native and outside the trait until a backend can run variable-width messages. Every implementation keeps these rules:
- it is called on the main thread and never calls Postgres; any threads it starts (a pool, a device driver's) block every signal (§6.9);
- every graph-sized allocation, device memory included, is charged to `call.memory` before it is made, and a failed allocation is `ComputeError::OutOfMemory`, never an abort;
- it polls `call.interrupts` at least every millisecond while it runs and stops within one task of a cancel. An operation it cannot interrupt is one it must not serve;
- it reads a version's CSRs in place, or keeps a derived copy (a device copy) keyed by version and counted by the cache (§5.5);
- its results equal `Native`'s bit for bit, row order, errors and the §6.3 semantics included, except for floating-point reassociation where it is documented per operation (§6.11).

Follow-up work, none of it started: introduce `Kernels` and `Native` over the existing `spmv`/`spmm` with no change in behaviour, and move `compute_sql`'s product calls onto it. Then sparse vectors with push/pull switching for traversal and `spmspv`, then masked SpGEMM (`mxm`) when contraction or k-truss needs it, each benchmarked in `benches/` as it lands.

### 6.11 End state: Pregel and sparse matrix algebra, on CPU and GPU

*Direction (set 2026-10-19; not scheduled beyond the M7 decision).* Any graph computation can be written as operations on the adjacency matrix, and on accelerators that is usually the fastest form. Message passing is more expressive and easier to approach. pg_warren's end destination offers both, over one substrate:

- **Pregel-style is first-class and stays so.** `gather` with SQL-typed messages, the PL/pgSQL library and tier-1 loops are how most users write graph programs. Matrix algebra is added beside them, never in place of them.
- **Sparse matrix algebra for power users**, for when the vertex-centric form is not fast enough. Today's tier 1 covers adjacency × dense vector or dense matrix (`spmv`, `spmm`) under 13 fixed semirings, with masks, accumulation, transpose and relation selection, plus elementwise maps and reductions. What arbitrary sparse algebra still needs, in rough priority order:
  1. **SpGEMM**, including masked `C⟨M⟩ = A ⊕.⊗ B` (triangles, k-truss, two-hop neighbourhoods, and contraction's `PᵀAP`, §6.6).
  2. **Sparse vectors** and push/pull switching, so small frontiers do not cost O(N) per step (§10, direction-optimising BFS). *Push/pull switching exists for `reachable_set`'s traversal (M7, §9.3), with a bitmap frontier for pulled levels; tier-1 vectors are still dense.*
  3. **Matrices as values**: the result of a product stored and reused as a derived graph version, by the same catalog mechanism contraction needs (§6.6 obligation 1), plus selection, elementwise add/multiply of matrices and submatrix extraction.
  4. Possibly user-defined semirings or operators, if a need appears that the fixed set cannot express.
- **Hardware.** Kernels live behind one seam in `warren-core` (plain slices in, plain buffers out, already true; the `Kernels` trait of §6.10), so the native CPU implementation and, later, a GPU backend (cuSPARSE/GraphBLAST-style) can each serve the operations they do best, chosen per operation and per machine. No SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS binding (decided in M7, §6.10): its algorithms are adopted natively instead. Results must not depend on the backend beyond floating-point reassociation, which is documented where it applies. The §6.9 rule stands: only the main thread calls Postgres, whatever runs the kernel.
- **No rush on GPU.** The obligation now is only to keep the seam clean and the operation vocabulary GraphBLAS-shaped so a GPU backend stays a contained addition.

## 7. Versions, retention, and staging

- A graph has zero or more versions. Exactly one is `current` (promoted). Any call that takes `version bigint DEFAULT NULL` operates on `current` when NULL.
- `warren.refresh(name, promote := false)` builds a **staged** version: fully built, loadable, queryable by explicit `version`, but not visible to callers that use the default. `warren.promote(name, version)` makes it current; `warren.drop_version(name, version)` discards it.
- `retain_versions` controls how many promoted versions are kept; staged versions are never auto-deleted. A version with bound (persisted) vectors is not auto-deleted either (a `NOTICE` reports what was retained and why); transient vectors (§6.2) never pin a version, so scratch vectors should be transient or dropped with `drop_vector`. `compact` moves the source's vectors to the compacted version (§5.2), so the source no longer pins and retention deletes it in turn. *Implemented (M2):* every promotion (`refresh(promote := true)` and `promote`) keeps the current version plus the `retain_versions − 1` newest other promoted versions by `seq` (so the current version counts towards the limit and is kept even when it is an older one promoted back), and deletes the rest unless vectors are bound to them; deleting a version cascades to its segments. `drop_version` deletes any version but the current one (vectors included); `drop_graph` deletes the graph and everything built from it.
- Versions share the dense ID space (until `compact`), so any vector bound to version *n* can be read against version *m* for comparison (*M4:* `vector_norm_diff`'s `a_version` and `b_version`, §6.3) — `warren.vector_norm_diff` and `warren.distance` (§8) accept vectors from different versions of the same graph and align by dense ID, treating nodes absent from either side as zero. Both refuse versions whose shared dense ids name different nodes (sibling staged versions, §8).
- **Access.** A graph exposes topology computed with its owner's visibility, exactly like a materialised view; source-table RLS does not apply to what traversal reveals. Traversal and compute functions therefore require the caller to be a member of the owner or of a role in `graphs.readers`. `warren.grant(name, role)` and `warren.revoke(name, role)` maintain that list. Management functions (`refresh`, `promote`, `drop_*` except `drop_vector`, `compact`, `grant`, `revoke`) require membership in the owner role. **Vector ownership (decided in M4 review):** `warren.vectors.created_by` records the role that wrote each persisted vector. Replacing a persisted vector (writing its name again, from any vector-writing function) or dropping it (`drop_vector`) requires membership in its creator or in the graph's owner role (`insufficient_privilege` otherwise, naming both); a replacement is then the replacer's. So readers share one name space per version but may only replace or drop their own vectors, and owners may replace or drop any. A vector whose creator was dropped is the owner's alone. The check binds the write to the row it checked: the write deletes only that row, by `vector_id`, and any other row of that name (committed by another role since the writer opened the version, or in progress) makes the write fail with `serialization_failure` instead of being deleted unchecked; `drop_vector`, transient replacement and `compact`'s move delete by `vector_id` too (harness test `vector_replace_race`). Readers' vectors still occupy the catalog owner's storage, with no per-role quota (v1.1, with the `DROP ROLE` clean-up below). `owner` is set to `current_user` by `warren.create_graph` and no function changes it; ownership transfer is out of scope for v1.
- **Catalog writes and identity switches.** All `warren.*` functions are `SECURITY INVOKER`. Because the tables are revoked from `PUBLIC` (§5.1), each catalog, segment or vector SPI statement runs inside an internal switch to the **catalog owner**, the role that owns the `warren.*` tables: `GetUserIdAndSecContext`, then `SetUserIdAndSecContext(catalog owner, SECURITY_LOCAL_USERID_CHANGE | SECURITY_RESTRICTED_OPERATION)`, then restore. The catalog owner is read once per backend: it is the owner (`pg_class.relowner`) of `warren.graphs`, provided that table is a member of the extension, never `pg_extension.extowner` (see the trusted install below). An `ERROR` inside the switch is undone by (sub)transaction abort. This is preferred over `SECURITY DEFINER` functions because the elevation then covers only extension-written SQL. Schema-qualifying the tables is not enough to make that SQL fixed: operators (`=`, `||`), functions and catalog relations (`pg_roles`, `pg_has_role`) resolve through `search_path`, so a caller could put its own `=` ahead of `pg_catalog` and have it run as the catalog owner, or shadow `pg_roles`/`pg_has_role` to pass an access check (both reproduced in review, M2). **Every `warren.*` entry point therefore runs its whole body under `access::entry`, which pins `search_path = pg_catalog, pg_temp`** (`pg_temp` explicitly last) the way `fmgr` applies `SECURITY DEFINER … SET search_path`: `NewGUCNestLevel`, `set_config_option(…, PGC_S_SESSION, GUC_ACTION_SAVE)`, and `AtEOXact_GUC` on return, with (sub)transaction abort restoring it on error. This covers the access checks that run as the caller as well as the elevated statements; the checks also schema-qualify `pg_catalog` objects. The caller's own `search_path` is captured before pinning and used only for the caller's own SQL (a new graph's source queries, checked by `create_graph`). A test calls every function in schema `warren` with a hostile `search_path` and fails if the hostile operator runs or the access checks give way. With `SECURITY DEFINER`, everything the function evaluates would run elevated: user-supplied `node_query`/`edge_query`, `gather` message SQL and `vector_map` executor-fallback expressions. Avoiding that would need a switch back to the invoker at each of those points, which is easier to get wrong.
- **Trusted install (M7).** `pg_warren.control` sets `trusted = true` (with `superuser = true`), so a non-superuser with `CREATE` on the database can run `CREATE EXTENSION pg_warren`, and as the extension's owner `ALTER EXTENSION pg_warren UPDATE` and `DROP EXTENSION`. Postgres then runs the script as the bootstrap superuser. That role owns schema `warren` and every `warren.*` table and sequence, while `pg_extension.extowner` is the installer. In a superuser install the installing superuser is both.
  - *Elevation target.* The switch above elevates to the tables' owner, never to `extowner`. Elevating to `extowner` fails in a trusted install, since the installer has no privilege on the tables. The obvious fixes are privilege escalations: granting the tables to `@extowner@`, or making it their owner, would let the installer rewrite any graph's `node_query`, which `refresh` then runs as that graph's owner (a superuser included), or plant a graph, and read every graph's segments and vectors past the reader check. So the installer gets no privilege on the catalog. It reaches graphs only through `warren.*` functions, like any other role, and what it can do as the extension's owner is update the extension, or drop it and with it every graph. The elevation itself is unchanged: to a superuser, for extension-written SQL only, under the pinned `search_path` and `SECURITY_RESTRICTED_OPERATION`. The cached catalog owner fails closed if it goes stale: a role that no longer owns the tables has no privilege on them unless it is a superuser.
  - *Script audit* (every install and upgrade script, released ones included, so they install trusted as they are):
    - Objects are created only in schema `warren`. `CREATE SCHEMA IF NOT EXISTS warren` refuses to adopt a schema that exists outside the extension (CVE-2022-2625 hardening, which every supported major has), and no other statement uses `CREATE OR REPLACE` or `IF NOT EXISTS`.
    - What runs at install time (column defaults, `CHECK` constraints, the 0.1.0 → 0.2.0 backfill) uses built-in operators and functions with exact-match argument types, so it resolves in `pg_catalog`, which is searched first. Postgres puts `pg_temp` last in a script's `search_path`, so a temporary type cannot shadow a column type (checked on PG 16).
    - C functions, the `jsonb → warren.step[]` cast and `pg_extension_config_dump` need a superuser, which the script runs as.
    - Function bodies run later, under `access::entry`'s pinned `search_path`; the PL/pgSQL functions also set their own.
    - Later scripts keep to these rules: create only in `warren`, schema-qualify, and use only exact-match `pg_catalog` calls in anything evaluated at install time.
  - *Dumps.* The catalog is registered with `pg_extension_config_dump` (§5.1), so `pg_dump` reads it, which only the catalog owner may do. A non-superuser's `pg_dump` of a database with pg_warren fails (`permission denied for sequence graphs_graph_id_seq`). It can dump everything else with `--exclude-table-data='warren.*'`, which restores cleanly (as the installer, into a database it owns) to an extension with no graphs; each graph is then created again with `create_graph` and built with `refresh` from the restored tables (`test/dump.py` checks this). Restoring catalog rows needs a superuser as well. Granting the installer read access would make its dumps work but leak every graph to it, so that was not done.
  - *Superuser-only features stay out of the trusted path.* The v1.1 `DROP ROLE` clean-up needs an event trigger, which only a superuser can create. It will be a separate step a superuser opts into, never part of the extension script, and pg_warren keeps working without it. Nothing else needs a superuser: the GUCs are `USERSET`, and the only extra right a superuser has is `drop_graph` of a graph whose owner was dropped (below).
  - *What the installer can still do.* As the extension's owner it can `ALTER EXTENSION pg_warren ADD` an object it owns, which is harmless: the object keeps its owner and privileges, gains nothing on the catalog, and is dropped with the extension. It cannot add or detach anyone else's object (tested).
  - *Schemas the database owner can write.* In a trusted install the installer typically owns the database, and on PG 15+ that makes it the owner of schema `public`. A graph's source queries run as its owner under the `search_path` captured at `create_graph`. If that path includes `public`, or any schema another role can create in, that role can add a function or operator that captures an unqualified name in the queries (a better-matching overload), and every `refresh` then runs it as the graph's owner. The read-only state (below) limits what it can write, not what it can read. Schema-qualify the source queries' functions and operators, or create the graph under a `search_path` of schemas only its owner can write. `create_graph` raises a `NOTICE` naming such schemas, quoted: those on the effective path in which PUBLIC, or some role other than the creator that is not a superuser and not predefined, has `CREATE`. `pg_database_owner` counts through the database's owner. Membership is not excluded, since a superuser is a member of every role. Temporary schemas are left out: Postgres reports `CREATE` on the session's own for every role with `TEMP`, but no other session can use it. The check is advisory: it sees only the schemas and grants that exist at `create_graph`. In particular, a schema the path names but that does not exist yet is not checked, typically `"$user"`; if such a schema is created later, by a role that can, it lands ahead of `public` on the path (harness test `search_path_notice`).
  - *Tests.* `test/sql/trusted_install.sql` has a non-superuser database owner install pg_warren. That role and a second non-superuser are refused direct reads and writes of every catalog table and sequence, ownership changes, row security, triggers, `ALTER EXTENSION … DROP`, objects in `warren`, and each other's graphs. Source, message and value queries and executor-fallback expressions run as the graph owner or the caller, never elevated. `test/run.py --trusted` runs the whole SQL suite with each database's pg_warren installed by a non-superuser that owns it. Only `CREATE EXTENSION` runs as that role; the tests themselves run as the superuser, so non-superuser callers are covered by `trusted_install.sql` and the role tests of the pgrx suite. `test/upgrade.py --trusted` installs every release and updates it as that role. CI runs both, and `test/dump.py` checks the installer's dumps (§5.1).
- **Caller SQL runs read-only — an integrity guard, not a sandbox.** Every read of SQL a user wrote (a graph's `node_query`/`edge_query` during `refresh`, `vector_from_query`'s value query, `gather`'s message query or relation, and *0.3.0:* a `vector_map` expression on the executor path, §6.3) runs with the backend in Postgres's read-only *transaction* state (`XactReadOnly`, set around the read by `access::with_read_only` and restored on return or error; the `transaction_read_only` GUC cannot be set inside a function, so the flag is set directly); the queries also run as read-only SPI cursors. A read-only cursor alone checks only its top statement, so this is what stops a VOLATILE function the query or expression calls from writing: such a write fails with 25006, and a data-modifying CTE with 0A000. It is the same guarantee `SET TRANSACTION READ ONLY` gives, not isolation: still possible are INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on existing temporary tables, `pg_notify`, `ANALYZE`, `LOCK`, assigning a transaction id, `COPY … TO PROGRAM` and `lo_export` for roles privileged to use them, and writes over a separate connection (`dblink_exec`). None of these escalates privilege: caller SQL runs as the caller, and source queries as the graph owner (above), so it can do nothing its role could not do directly. What it guarantees is that a source, value or message query, or a `vector_map` expression, cannot change the tables a build or kernel is reading. Nothing else evaluates caller SQL: parse analysis (`vector_map`, `explain_map`) folds no constants and runs no SQL- or PL-language function (only the C input functions of literals' types), and the compiled subset is `warren-core`'s IR, which calls no Postgres function.
- `refresh` therefore makes two identity switches: to the **graph owner** while `node_query` and `edge_query` run (§5.4), and to the **catalog owner** for the catalog and segment writes. User SQL never runs as the catalog owner.
- **Dropped roles.** `owner` and `readers` are `regrole` values, which are bare OIDs with no `pg_shdepend` entries, so `DROP ROLE` does not see them. `refresh`, and every function that checks access, raise an error if the owner role no longer exists. Reader OIDs that no longer resolve to a role are ignored by access checks and pruned by `grant` and `revoke`. A graph whose owner was dropped can still be removed: `drop_graph` lets a superuser drop it. A `DROP ROLE` event trigger that cleans these up is a v1.1 item.
- `warren.diff(name, v1, v2)` returns structural deltas: nodes added/removed, edges added/removed per relation, degree-distribution shift — computed by merging the two CSRs row-wise, O(N + M). *Implemented (M2, `warren_core::version::diff`):* an edge is a (src, dst) pair, so a weight change alone is not counted; relations are aligned by name (later dictionary's order, then relations only in the earlier one); an all-relations delta comes from the all-relations rows (the merged CSR, stored or merged on the fly, §5.3). The degree shift is per direction (out, and in counted from the forward CSR, so no reverse is needed) over live nodes: a sparse histogram, the means, and the exact two-sample KS statistic (NULL when a side has no live nodes). `diff` refuses when the later id space is smaller or any shared dense ID names a different external ID, which catches comparisons across a compaction boundary. Benchmark: ~25 ms single-threaded at 100K nodes / 1M edges (`benches/version_diff.rs`). *SQL (M2):* `warren.diff(name, v1, v2) RETURNS jsonb`, chosen over rows because the degree histograms nest: `{from, to, nodes: {before, after, added, removed}, relations: [{relation, before, after, added, removed}], all_relations: {…}, out_degree: {mean_before, mean_after, ks_statistic, histogram: [{degree, before, after}]}, in_degree: {…}}`. Owners and readers may call it. It refuses (`invalid_parameter_value`) when a version with `compacted_from` set lies after the earlier and at or before the later of the two by `seq`, naming the compaction, before loading anything.

## 8. Change detection workflow

The motivating scenario: a large knowledge graph in Postgres receives a new corpus. Before committing it, the team wants to measure how much the *global structure* of the graph changes — not just "how many edges were added" but whether the new material reshapes the graph's dominant structure — and gate the commit on that.

Because builds are transactional and see uncommitted writes, this is expressible in one transaction:

```sql
BEGIN;

-- 1. ingest the new corpus into the ordinary nodes/edges tables (uncommitted)
INSERT INTO entities ...;  INSERT INTO relations ...;

-- 2. build a staged version from this transaction's view of the tables
SELECT warren.refresh('kg', promote := false, label := 'corpus-2026-10-17');
--    → returns new version_id, say 42; current is 41

-- 3. structural delta (cheap)
SELECT * FROM warren.diff('kg', 41, 42);

-- 4. spectral comparison (seconds to minutes depending on N, k)
SELECT warren.eigen('kg', 20, 'normalized_laplacian', 'smallest', vectors_prefix := 'lap', version := 41);
SELECT warren.eigen('kg', 20, 'normalized_laplacian', 'smallest', vectors_prefix := 'lap', version := 42);
SELECT warren.distance('kg', 41, 42, method := 'subspace', vectors_prefix := 'lap', k := 20);
--    → principal angles between the two dominant eigen-subspaces, summarised as a scalar

-- 5. decide
--    COMMIT  → corpus and version 42 persist; optionally SELECT warren.promote('kg', 42);
--    ROLLBACK → corpus, version 42 and its vectors all vanish; nothing to clean up.
COMMIT;
```

`warren.distance(graph, v1, v2, method, ...)` provides a small set of comparable scalars:

- `edit` — normalised symmetric difference of edge sets, per relation.
- `degree` — distance between degree distributions (Wasserstein / KS).
- `spectral` — distance between the top-k eigenvalue sequences (requires `eigen` on both versions, or computes it).
- `subspace` — principal angles / projection-Frobenius distance between the spans of the top-k eigenvectors; the most sensitive to structural reorganisation and the least sensitive to volume. Requires stable dense IDs (§5.2); refuses across a compaction boundary.
- `embedding` — mean/max row-wise distance between two N×d matrices (e.g. neighbour-aggregated embeddings before and after), so embedding drift induced by structural change is measurable too.

```sql
warren.distance(graph text, v1 bigint, v2 bigint, method text,  -- edit | degree | spectral | subspace | embedding
                vectors_prefix text DEFAULT NULL, k int DEFAULT NULL,  -- spectral, subspace: <prefix>_1..k
                x text DEFAULT NULL, y text DEFAULT NULL,      -- embedding: matrix x of v1, y (default x) of v2
                compared_rows text DEFAULT 'shared',           -- subspace, embedding: shared | all
                matrix text DEFAULT NULL,      -- spectral: required with vectors_prefix, else 'adjacency'
                which text DEFAULT 'largest', rels text[] DEFAULT NULL,
                weighted bool DEFAULT NULL, symmetrize bool DEFAULT true, tol float8 DEFAULT 1e-6,
                max_iter int DEFAULT NULL, dtype text DEFAULT 'f32',  -- spectral: the operator, and a fresh solve
                graph2 text DEFAULT NULL)                      -- v2 is a version of graph2, aligned by external id
  RETURNS jsonb;   -- {"method", "from", "to", ["graph2"], ...the method's scalars}
```

**Implemented (M6, `warren_core::spectral`, bound by `pg_warren::spectral_sql`).** Each method returns a small struct of scalars, which the binding returns as `jsonb` (§14 item 11); `edit` names each relation. Versions of one graph are aligned by dense id, which is sound only where both versions' shared dense ids name the same nodes: `version::shared_dense_ids` checks that, as `diff` does, and `distance` (and, *0.3.0*, `vector_norm_diff`) refuses otherwise (22023). It fails across a compaction and between sibling staged versions whose new nodes diverge (two `refresh(promote := false)` from one current version can hand the same new dense ids to different nodes). Relations never cause a refusal: they are matched by name, so siblings that differ only in their relations compare. Past a version's id space everything is absent.

- `edit_distance` → per relation (matched by name, through `version::relation_deltas`, the pairing `diff` uses) and overall over (relation, source, target) triples: `added`, `removed`, `union`, and the Jaccard distance `(added + removed)/union`. Weights are ignored. The row-wise merge of the two forward CSRs is `warren.diff`'s (`EdgeDelta::between`), O(N + M).
- `degree_distance` → `wasserstein` (W₁, in degrees) and `ks` between the distributions of distinct out-neighbour counts in the all-relations forward CSR, over live (not tombstoned) nodes; an empty version is a point mass at 0.
- `spectral_distance` → `l2`, `relative` (over the larger norm) and `max` of the in-order difference of two eigenvalue sequences, missing as 0.
- `subspace_distance` → `projection` (`‖P_X − P_Y‖_F = √(k₁ + k₂ − 2Σcos²θ)`), `max_angle` and the principal `angles`, from one block-ordered Gram pass over both vector sets, Cholesky and an SVD of `L_X⁻¹XᵀY L_Y⁻ᵀ` (inputs need only be independent; cosines within 8Kε of 1 count as 1, so identical inputs give exactly 0).
- `embedding_distance` → `mean_l2`, `max_l2`, `mean_cosine`, `max_cosine` (1 − cos, from normalised rows) and `rows` compared, skipping rows zero on both sides.
- `subspace` and `embedding` take a `ComparedRows`: `All` reads every row with absent rows as zero (§7), so added and removed nodes count as change; `Shared` reads only nodes present (non-zero) on both sides. A.2 needs `Shared`: with `All`, added volume moves every eigenvector's mass onto the new rows and outweighs restructuring.
- `spectral` with `vectors_prefix` compares the Rayleigh quotients of the stored `<prefix>_1..k` under `matrix`, which must then be named (a stored vector does not record its operator, and quotients under the wrong one look plausible) (their eigenvalues, without solving again); without it, it solves `k` eigenpairs on each version.
- `compared_rows` defaults to `shared`: A.2 compares structure, not volume, and over `all` the added nodes' rows dominate (§8 above).
- Cross-graph comparison (`graph2`, the shadow-schema pattern): `spectral::align_ids` maps the second version into the first's id space by external id (shared nodes keep the first graph's dense ids, the second's own nodes are appended in external-id order, relations match by name; O(N)), and `spectral::align` moves its vectors' rows by that map; the same functions then run. Only `edit` needs the second version's CSRs in the shared ids: `spectral::align_graph` rebuilds them for it alone, without a merged CSR, which `edit` does not read, charged to `warren.max_compute_memory` and polling interrupts every 64K rows. Both graphs need the same `id_type`. Versions of one graph are refused across a compaction (as `diff` is); across graphs the external ids align them, so a compaction is no boundary there.
- Small-instance A.2 check (unit test): 4 planted communities of 30, 48 nodes ingested; over shared nodes the subspace distance of the 4 smallest normalised-Laplacian eigenvectors is < 0.5 for *volume* and > 1 for *novel* and *bridge*, whose relative order depends on the sizes (it flips with communities of 40 and 64 ingested), so A.2's `novel < bridge` is checked at its own scale in the integration test. The harness test `test/sql/a2_spectral.sql` (6 communities of 40, 80 nodes and 1,600 edges ingested, k = 6, f64) measures volume 0.40, novel 1.27, bridge 1.43 and asserts volume < 0.5 and both others above twice volume; with 400 ingested edges the bridge barely merged its two communities (0.24), which shows the ordering's dependence on the generator's density. The shadow-schema comparison matches the same-graph one within 10⁻⁹ (k = 6, f64, tol 10⁻¹², so that both Lanczos runs converge past the comparison's resolution although their start vectors differ by dense id). After `aggregate_neighbours` (mean, one hop) of the fixture's 64-dim embeddings, `distance('embedding')` over shared nodes gives a mean L2 drift of 0.16 for *bridge*, 0.13 for *volume* and 0.02 for *novel* (whose entities barely link to the base graph), and the test asserts *bridge* highest; its margin over *volume* is only about a quarter at this scale.

Design consequences that this workflow imposes, all already reflected above: stable dense IDs; staged versions; transactional segment storage; eigenvectors stored as versioned vectors; alignment-by-dense-ID in all cross-version operations; retention that does not silently delete a version somebody is comparing against.

Operational note: a long transaction holding an uncommitted corpus plus a staged build holds back vacuum on the source tables. For very large corpora, the alternative is to ingest into a *shadow* schema or partition, point a second graph definition at it, build and compare across graphs with the same `id_type` (cross-graph `distance` aligns by external ID through both `id_map`s), and then swap. Both patterns should be documented.

## 9. Traversal functions

### 9.1 The query paradigm and the seam

`pg_warren` deliberately has no query language. The model is the PostGIS/pgvector one: functions that take node IDs in and return relations out, composed with ordinary SQL on either side. A graph query in a `pg_warren` system therefore has two forms, and the user chooses between them per query:

- **Shallow / small-frontier:** write a recursive CTE or explicit self-joins over the real `nodes`/`edges` tables. Fully transactional, planner-optimised, no staleness. This is the default and covers most queries.
- **Deep / large-frontier:** replace the CTE with a single `warren.*` call and join its result back to the real tables for filtering and projection. Snapshot semantics (§7).

The switching criterion is **frontier size, not hop count.** Three hops at average degree 10 is a thousand intermediate rows and belongs in SQL; three hops at degree 1,000 is a billion and belongs in the engine. A rule of thumb for users: if `EXPLAIN` of the CTE shows the recursive term producing more than ~10⁵–10⁶ rows, or if the query is iterative at all, move it above the seam.

What makes the seam cheap to cross is that both forms produce a relation keyed by external ID, so moving a query from one side to the other changes one clause, not the surrounding query. Keeping that property is a design constraint on every traversal function.

The one place the functions as originally scoped were too blunt is multi-relational graphs: real knowledge-graph queries are rarely "everything within k hops" but "follow *this* relation, then *that* one, with a predicate at each step". Chaining `warren.neighbours` per hop through lateral joins expresses that, but it re-enters the executor and materialises a set at every hop — exactly the cost model the engine exists to avoid. Hence `warren.walk` (§9.2), which keeps a typed chain inside the engine for its whole length. It is still a function; it is not a language.

### 9.2 `warren.walk` — typed path patterns

A walk is an ordered list of steps. Each step is a relation (or set of relations), a direction, an optional node filter applied to the nodes reached by that step, and a hop range for variable-length segments. The engine executes the whole chain over CSR without leaving the backend, returning the end frontier or full paths.

```sql
CREATE TYPE warren.step AS (
    rels      text[],      -- NULL = any relation
    direction text,        -- 'out' | 'in' | 'both'
    filter    anyarray,    -- allowed nodes after this step (external IDs); NULL = no filter
    min_hops  int,         -- default 1
    max_hops  int          -- default = min_hops; >min gives a variable-length segment
);

warren.walk(graph text, start anyarray, steps warren.step[],
            mode text DEFAULT 'ends',            -- 'ends' | 'paths' | 'count'
            distinct_ends bool DEFAULT true,
            max_results bigint DEFAULT NULL,
            reverse bool DEFAULT false,          -- execute the chain from the other end (see below)
            version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (end_id <id_type>, path <id_type>[], rels text[]);   -- path/rels NULL unless mode='paths'

-- convenience constructor so steps read well in SQL
warren.s(rels text[], direction text DEFAULT 'out', filter anyarray DEFAULT NULL,
         min_hops int DEFAULT 1, max_hops int DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS warren.step;
```

Example — papers cited by anything within 1–3 citation hops of a root, written by currently active people, at UK institutions:

```sql
SELECT i.*
FROM warren.walk('kg', ARRAY[:root], ARRAY[
        warren.s(ARRAY['cites'], 'out', NULL, 1, 3),
        warren.s(ARRAY['authored_by'], 'out', (SELECT array_agg(id) FROM people WHERE active)),
        warren.s(ARRAY['affiliated_with'], 'out', (SELECT array_agg(id) FROM institutions WHERE country = 'GB'))
     ]) w
JOIN institutions i ON i.id = w.end_id;
```

Semantics and implementation notes:

- Each step's filter array is converted to a dense-ID bitmap once per call; filters are tested before a node is admitted to the next frontier, so predicates on intermediate nodes prune the traversal rather than post-filter it.
- Within a variable-length segment the engine runs a level-synchronous BFS restricted to that step's relations, emitting nodes at every depth in `[min_hops, max_hops]` into the next step's start frontier. Visited sets are per segment (a node may legitimately be revisited by a later step).
- `mode = 'ends'` keeps only the end frontier and is the fast path (no predecessor storage). `'paths'` keeps per-step predecessor arrays and enumerates paths on return; it must be bounded by `max_results` and documented as potentially exponential. `'count'` returns the number of end nodes (or paths) without materialising them.
- `reverse` executes the chain from its last step backwards (directions flipped, filters applied in reverse order) and reports the nodes that would have been *starts*, with `start` then interpreted as the set of allowed ends. This is the manual version of the selectivity-aware start choice a graph planner would make automatically: when the far end of a pattern is rarer than the near end, start there.
- No variable binding and no joins within a pattern in v1: a walk is a chain. Cycles and "A and B share a C" are expressed in SQL by intersecting the results of two walks on `end_id`, which the planner handles well because both sides are already small. See §10 for what a pattern language would add beyond this.
- `warren.step[]` is a **stable intermediate representation**. Any future sugar — a parsed pattern string, say — must compile to it rather than bypass it, so that the engine has exactly one traversal entry point to optimise.

Decisions made in M3 (`warren-core::traversal::walk`, dense ids in and out):

- The code calls a step's `min_hops..max_hops` its **hop range**; a "variable-length segment" above is a step whose hop range spans several lengths. "Segment" keeps its storage meaning.
- **Hops are walks**, as in a recursive CTE with a depth column: nodes and edges may repeat. A step ends on every node some walk of `k ∈ [min_hops, max_hops]` hops reaches, not only on nodes whose shortest distance is in range (a `2..2` step from `a` with `a→b→c` and `a→c` ends on `c`; a start can be its own end through a cycle). Below `min_hops` each depth keeps its own node set; from `min_hops` on, one visited set per step is exact, because a node first admitted at depth `j ≥ min` already led everywhere a later visit would, within `max`.
- A step's filter holds every node a hop of the step enters and the node the step hands on. Starts are not filtered. `min_hops = 0` is allowed: the step may hand on the node it started from, if the filter allows it. `HopRange` requires `min ≤ max ≤ 1024`; the cap stops a cyclic graph doing work at absurd depths.
- **Walk identity** (for `count` without distinct ends, and for `paths`): a walk is a sequence of hops, each a `(relation, neighbour)` pair, with each step's hops its own. An edge in two selected relations is two walks; an edge read both ways by one relation under `both`, or a self-loop, is one hop; the same nodes split differently between steps are different walks (as with Cypher's per-pattern bindings). Repeated starts count once. A walk of no steps ends where it starts.
- `ends`: with `distinct_ends`, the fast path (merged rows, no predecessors), streamed as found (`walk_ends_each`); without, each end once per walk, streamed after the walks are counted.
- `count` is exact, never approximate: distinct ends, or walks counted level by level with checked `u64` arithmetic (an error past `u64::MAX`), without materialising them. `max_results` does not apply to it.
- `paths`: with `distinct_ends`, one path per end, read back from how the fast path first reached each node at each level, streamed as found (`walk_paths_each`). Without, every walk, enumerated depth-first over the walk counts' levels pruned backwards to nodes that can still finish, so every branch yields a path. Paths carry each hop's relation index. The number of walks can be exponential in the hops; the adapter always passes `max_results` (§14 item 15).
- `reverse` runs the steps last to first with directions flipped; reversed step `m` hops only *from* nodes in `filter_m` and hands on only nodes in `filter_{m-1}`, and the starts must be in the last filter, so every walk found is a forward walk read backwards. It reports would-be starts; its paths read forward, from the reported node to an allowed end. A property test checks every mode, forwards and reversed, against brute-force enumeration.
- `last_stats()` covers the whole walk: `nodes_visited` counts each node admitted to a level of a step (each step's starting nodes, then each node a hop admits), so a filter never raises it; `edges_traversed` counts row entries scanned; `truncated` means `max_results` stopped the walk.

Decisions made in M3 (`pg_warren::traversal_sql`, the SQL binding):

- `warren.step` is the composite `(rels text[], direction text, filter text[], min_hops int, max_hops int)`. A composite type cannot have a polymorphic attribute, so a step holds its filter's external ids as text in their id type's output format; `walk` reads them back with the graph's id type's input function (a malformed id raises `invalid_text_representation`), skipping ids that are not nodes of the version, as filters do everywhere (§9.3). A NULL `direction` is `out`, a NULL `min_hops` 1, a NULL `max_hops` equal to `min_hops`.
- `warren.s(rels text[], direction text DEFAULT 'out', filter "any" DEFAULT NULL, min_hops int DEFAULT 1, max_hops int DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS warren.step`: `filter` is `"any"` for the same reason as `bfs`'s `node_filter`. The `jsonb → warren.step[]` cast (`warren.steps(jsonb)`, §14 item 14) takes an array of objects with optional keys `rels`, `direction`, `filter`, `min_hops`, `max_hops` and the same defaults; anything else (not an array, a non-object element, an unknown key, a `rels` or `filter` that is neither an array nor JSON null) is `invalid_parameter_value`, since silently widening a malformed step would return more rows than asked for.
- `warren.walk(graph, start anyarray, steps warren.step[], mode DEFAULT 'ends', distinct_ends DEFAULT true, max_results DEFAULT NULL, reverse DEFAULT false, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (end_id anyelement, path anyarray, rels text[], count bigint)`. A fourth column, `count`, carries `mode = 'count'`'s answer in its single row (the other columns NULL); in the other modes it is NULL. A count above `bigint`'s range is `numeric_value_out_of_range`, never clamped. `path` and `rels` are set only in `paths` mode. With `reverse`, `end_id` is the reported would-be start, `path[1]`.
- A walk that reports a row per walk (`paths`, or `ends` without `distinct_ends`) is always bounded: `max_results` defaults to 100,000 for it, and a larger `max_results` than the `warren.max_walk_paths` GUC (default 10,000,000, `USERSET`) is refused with `program_limit_exceeded` (§14 item 15). Stopping at the default is truncation, with the `NOTICE`.
- The domain's walks are callback-driven (`walk_ends_each`, `walk_paths_each`) rather than cursors, so `warren.walk` collects its rows (within the row limit above) before returning the first; SQL `LIMIT` does not shorten a walk.
- `last_stats()` reports `function = 'walk'`; a step filter lowers `nodes_visited` (Appendix A.4, tested).
- Oracle: each mode, forwards and reversed, against a chain of recursive CTEs with a depth column (one per step, tracking each walk's origin), walk counts summed level by level in SQL, and, up to 100,000 walks, every walk enumerated in SQL with its path and relations: `paths` without distinct ends must equal that multiset, and each distinct-ends path must be one of its walks. Graphs: int8 with relations, uuid undirected, int4 with tombstones and a tombstoned start, and int8 without a reverse CSR (whose `in` steps and reverse walks are refused). The §9.3 oracles run on 10K-node random graphs; the whole pgrx suite takes about 50 s on PG 16.

`warren.bfs` is retained as the untyped special case (one step, any relation, `max_hops = depth`) with its own simpler signature.

### 9.3 Function reference

All take `graph text` first, external IDs for nodes, and `version bigint DEFAULT NULL`. All are `STABLE`. **Parallel labels (decided: M3, PR #4 review; M5 fix):** every function that writes or may persist what it computes (`create_graph`, `refresh`, `promote`, `compact`, `drop_*`, `grant`, `revoke`, the vector constructors and kernels — `vector_const`, `vector_from_query`, `vector_degree`, `vector_dense_id`, `vector_map`, `spmv`, `spmm`, `gather`, `triangles` — and the library functions) is `PARALLEL UNSAFE`. Every function that reads a graph or a vector is `PARALLEL RESTRICTED`: the traversal functions, `diff`, `info`, `prewarm`, `evict`, `last_stats`, and the vector reads `vector_values`, `matrix_rows`, `id_map`, `vector_reduce`, `vector_norm_diff`, `vector_stats`, `vector_topk`, `explain_map`, and the library's set-returning functions `minimum_spanning_forest`, `similarity`, `most_similar` and `similar_pairs`. A parallel worker has an empty backend-local cache (it would cold-load and hold the whole version), cannot see transient vectors (§6.2), and cannot hand `last_stats()` back to the leader; they return to `PARALLEL SAFE`, where that is true, with the shared cache (M9; which ones is §14 item 33, §5.6). Only `version`, `s`, `steps` and `shared_cache` (M9), which read no graph, are `PARALLEL SAFE`. A restricted function still runs in the leader while a parallel plan executes, which is parallel mode, so the read path assigns no transaction id and writes nothing: its single-row catalog reads go through `read_only::read_one` (a `Spi::connect` select, which never calls pgrx's `mark_mutable` and so never assigns a transaction id; it makes no promise about the snapshot, which is SPI's as for any read), never pgrx's `Spi::get_one*`, which runs the query through `SpiClient::update` and so calls `GetCurrentTransactionId()` ("cannot assign XIDs during a parallel operation"). `parallel_labels`'s tests check every function's label against a declared table and run every non-unsafe function (and reads of a transient vector) under `debug_parallel_query = on`, both in a subtransaction with no transaction id and in a transaction that holds one, and in the InitPlan of a cost-induced parallel scan. Clippy's `disallowed-methods` refuses pgrx's `Spi::get_*`, `run*` and `explain*` outside tests and the writer paths that allow them explicitly.

```sql
warren.bfs(graph text, start anyarray, max_depth int,
           direction text DEFAULT 'out',            -- out | in | both
           rels text[] DEFAULT NULL,
           node_filter anyarray DEFAULT NULL,       -- allowed nodes, external IDs
           max_results bigint DEFAULT NULL, version bigint DEFAULT NULL)
  RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, depth int, via <id_type>);

warren.neighbours(graph, node, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS SETOF <id_type>;
warren.reachable(graph, start, target, max_depth DEFAULT NULL, rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS boolean;
warren.reachable_set(graph, start anyarray, max_depth DEFAULT NULL, rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS SETOF <id_type>;
warren.shortest_path(graph, start, target, max_depth DEFAULT NULL, rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS <id_type>[];   -- bidirectional BFS
warren.dijkstra(graph, start, target, rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (path <id_type>[], cost float8);
warren.components(graph) RETURNS TABLE (node_id <id_type>, component_id bigint);
warren.degree(graph, node, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;
warren.has_edge(graph, src, dst, rel DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS boolean;
warren.walk(graph, start anyarray, steps warren.step[], mode DEFAULT 'ends', ...);   -- §9.2

-- management
warren.create_graph(name, node_query, edge_query, directed DEFAULT true, weighted DEFAULT false,
                    has_relations DEFAULT false, build_reverse DEFAULT true, retain_versions DEFAULT 2,
                    store_merged DEFAULT true,    -- M7: false merges relations on the fly (§5.3)
                    node_order DEFAULT 'id');     -- 0.4.0: 'locality' orders the first build's dense ids (§14 item 22)
                    -- owner := current_user; both queries are opened (not run) as read-only cursors to check
                    -- their columns; id_type is the node query's column type: int4, int8 or uuid
                    -- (edge endpoints must have the same type; M3)
warren.refresh(name, promote DEFAULT true, label DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;   -- the new version_id
warren.promote(name, version); warren.drop_version(name, version);
warren.compact(name, node_order DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS bigint;   -- NULL: the graph's node_order; one given becomes the graph's (0.4.0)
warren.diff(name, v1, v2) RETURNS jsonb;   -- §7
warren.drop_graph(name); warren.prewarm(name); warren.evict(name);
warren.shared_cache() RETURNS TABLE (kind text, graph text, version bigint, vector text, vector_id bigint, state text,
                                    bytes bigint, pins int, mappers int, last_use bigint, cap bigint);   -- §5.6 step 7, §14 item 37
warren.grant(name, role regrole); warren.revoke(name, role regrole);   -- §7 access; both RETURN void
-- promote, drop_version, drop_graph RETURN void; promote applies retention (§7)
warren.info(name) RETURNS jsonb;   -- current version, stats, staleness (now() - built_at), bytes, versions list;
                                   -- owners and readers may call it. Keys: name, owner, readers, id_type, search_path, the flags,
                                   -- retain_versions, store_merged, node_order, current_version, staleness, bytes, versions[{version, seq, staged,
                                   -- current, label, built_at, built_lsn, staleness, node_count, id_space, edge_count,
                                   -- dropped_edges, deduplicated_edges, target_width, bytes, compacted_from, vectors}]
warren.last_stats() RETURNS jsonb; -- nodes visited, edges traversed, path taken, for the session's last call (Appendix A.6)
warren.explain_map(graph, expr, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS jsonb;  -- 'compiled' | 'executor', and why (§6.3)
```

Typical use:

```sql
SELECT n.* FROM warren.bfs('kg', ARRAY[:root], 6, rels := ARRAY['cites','extends']) r
JOIN entities n ON n.id = r.node_id
WHERE n.kind = 'paper' ORDER BY r.depth;
```

Filtered traversal pushes predicates on intermediate nodes into the engine without a pattern-matching language: `node_filter` is converted to a dense-ID bitmap once per call. Accept a `roaringbitmap` type if that extension is present (v1.1); v1 takes an array.

Implementation notes:
- Traversal is level-synchronous BFS with frontier and next-frontier arrays (parallelisable later).
- Per-call scratch (visited set, depth and predecessor arrays) starts sparse (a hash set or map) and turns dense once it holds more than about `id_space/16` nodes, so a small traversal on a big graph does not allocate O(N) and no visit budget has to be guessed up front. Predecessors are stored only when the signature needs them.
- Truncation is never silent. `truncated = true` is set in `warren.last_stats()`, and a `NOTICE` is raised, exactly when the scan was stopped early by `max_results` or by a GUC ceiling (for example the `paths`-mode cap, §14 item 15). Reaching the caller's own `max_depth` is the requested result, not truncation.
- Interrupts are checked every ~64K units of work (nodes visited plus CSR row entries scanned), so edge scans that admit no node are bounded too. Traversals take an `Interrupts` poll hook: on the main thread (M3) the adapter implements it with `pgrx::check_for_interrupts!()`, which unwinds through the traversal; worker threads (M4) poll a shared `Cancellation` flag the main thread raises.
- There is no OFFSET-style pagination: SRFs stream rows (holding an `Arc` to the cached version) and SQL `LIMIT` stops them. Duplicate removal (`distinct_ends`, multi-start BFS) happens before any cap is applied.
- `shortest_path`'s bidirectional BFS finishes scanning the current level before choosing the meeting node with the minimum combined distance. Taking the first meeting node can return a non-shortest path, a bug pgGraph shipped and fixed. A differential proptest checks it against single-direction BFS.
- `components` labels each component with its minimum dense ID, so results are deterministic and comparable across versions.
- Defaults for caps may come from GUCs inside the SQL `DEFAULT` expression (`current_setting('warren.…', true)`).
- Decisions made in M1 (`warren-core::traversal`, dense ids in and out):
  - `direction` `in` and `both` read the reverse CSR and raise an error when the graph was built without one. `shortest_path` without a reverse CSR runs a forward-only BFS instead; `components` never needs one.
  - `bfs` and `reachable_set` also come in streaming forms (`bfs_each`, `reachable_set_each`) that hand each row to a visitor, so an SRF under SQL `LIMIT` stops the scan by breaking. A visitor stopping the scan is not truncation.
  - `neighbours` and `degree` count each neighbour once: `Adjacency::neighbours` returns the distinct dense IDs across the selected relations (and both directions for `both`) in ascending order, by a k-way merge of the sorted rows.
  - `node_filter` applies to the nodes a traversal reaches, never to its starts: starts are always admitted at depth 0, and repeated starts count once. `reachable_set` includes the starts.
  - `last_stats()` counts `nodes_visited` (nodes admitted or settled, starts included) and `edges_traversed` (CSR row entries scanned). The `Interrupts` hook is polled on the first unit of work (a visited node or a scanned row entry) and every 65,536th after.
  - `shortest_path` breaks ties between meeting nodes of equal combined distance by the smallest dense ID, so its result is deterministic.
  - `dijkstra` reads each selected relation's own CSR rather than the merged one, because the merged CSR sums the weights of an edge present in several relations and a cheapest path wants the minimum. Unweighted graphs cost 1 per edge. A negative or NaN weight raises an error when the search scans it.
