An edge whose cost would be infinite (an infinite weight, or an overflowing sum) is never followed, so a target reachable only at infinite cost returns no path.
  - `components` is union–find over the forward all-relations CSR. The domain labels every dense ID; a tombstoned node has no edges and so is its own component, and the adapter leaves tombstoned nodes out of the result.
- Decisions made in M3 (`pg_warren::traversal_sql`, external ids in and out):
  - **Signatures as implemented.** `bfs(graph, start anyarray, max_depth int, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels DEFAULT NULL, node_filter "any" DEFAULT NULL, max_results DEFAULT NULL, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, depth int, via anyelement)`; `reachable_set(graph, start anyarray, max_depth DEFAULT NULL, rels DEFAULT NULL, direction DEFAULT 'out', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS SETOF anyelement`; `neighbours(graph, node anyelement, direction DEFAULT 'out', rels DEFAULT NULL, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS SETOF anyelement`; `degree(…same…) RETURNS bigint`; `has_edge(graph, src anyelement, dst anyelement, rel text DEFAULT NULL, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS boolean`; `reachable(graph, start anyelement, target anyelement, max_depth DEFAULT NULL, rels DEFAULT NULL, direction DEFAULT 'out', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS boolean`; `shortest_path(…same…) RETURNS anyarray`; `dijkstra(graph, start, target, rels DEFAULT NULL, direction DEFAULT 'out', version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (path anyarray, cost float8)`; `components(graph, id_type anyelement, version DEFAULT NULL) RETURNS TABLE (node_id anyelement, component_id bigint)`; `last_stats() RETURNS jsonb`. `direction` was added (after `rels`) to `reachable`, `reachable_set`, `shortest_path` and `dijkstra`, since the core supports it and `in` is otherwise inexpressible.
  - **Polymorphism (§14 item 1): `anyelement`/`anyarray`, one C function each, dispatching at run time on `graphs.id_type`.** An id argument must have exactly the graph's id type (`datatype_mismatch` otherwise, with the cast to use): Postgres resolves the result type from the argument, so an `int4` literal against an `int8` graph cannot return `int8` ids. Integer literals therefore need a cast on `int8` graphs (`ARRAY[1::bigint]`, `42::bigint`). Lookups whose result holds no ids (`degree`, `has_edge`, `reachable`) widen an `int2`/`int4` argument losslessly to the graph's integer id type, so `warren.degree('g', 42)` works on a `bigint` graph. An array argument that is not an array (`node_filter`, `warren.s`'s `filter`, including an untyped literal like `'{1,2}'`) is `datatype_mismatch` naming the array cast to write.
  - Postgres coerces a polymorphic parameter's default to the pseudo-type itself, so an omitted `anyarray DEFAULT NULL` breaks type resolution. `node_filter` is therefore declared `"any"` (C functions only), defaults to `NULL`, and is checked at run time to be an array of the id type. For the same reason `components`, which has no id argument, takes a type witness, as `json_populate_record` does: `warren.components('g', NULL::bigint)`.
  - **Unknown ids.** An external id the version does not have, or has only as tombstoned, is not a node of that version. As a start or `node_filter` member it is skipped (so is NULL); as the node of a lookup it finds nothing: `neighbours` is empty, `degree` 0, `has_edge` and `reachable` false (an unknown node does not even reach itself), `shortest_path` NULL, `dijkstra` no row. A NULL node argument gives NULL (no rows). Relation names a version does not have are skipped likewise (a relation absent from a version has no edges in it); `rels => '{}'` selects nothing. Unknown ids are not errors because a version is a snapshot: ids come and go between versions, and the SQL side of the seam (§9.1) treats a missing join partner the same way.
  - **Streaming.** `bfs` and `reachable_set` run over `warren_core::traversal::BfsCursor`, which owns an `Arc` of the cached version and runs one BFS level per call; the SRF returns a level's rows before running the next. Postgres fetches value-per-call SRFs one row at a time only in the select list (`SELECT warren.reachable_set(…) LIMIT 10`); in `FROM` it runs the function to completion into a tuplestore, so there `LIMIT` does not shorten the scan and `max_results` is the bound to use. `last_stats()` is updated after every level, so after a `LIMIT` it counts the work actually done.
  - `last_stats()` is a session's `jsonb` `{function, graph, version, nodes_visited, edges_traversed, truncated}` (NULL before the first recording call); it is `VOLATILE PARALLEL RESTRICTED`. The `NOTICE` on truncation is raised once per call. `components` reports the union–find work.
  - Errors: a missing reverse CSR is `object_not_in_prerequisite_state`; a negative or NaN weight is `data_exception`; a negative `max_depth`/`max_results` or an unknown direction is `invalid_parameter_value`; a NULL `graph` or `direction` is `null_value_not_allowed`.
  - Cancellation: the `Interrupts` poll is `pgrx::check_for_interrupts!()` on the main thread, so `statement_timeout` and `pg_cancel_backend` stop a long traversal with `query_canceled` within 64K units of work.
- Decisions made in M7 (`warren-core::traversal::level`, `BfsCursor::reachable_set`):
  - **Where the time goes.** Measured at 1M nodes / 10M edges (G(n, m) from a seeded `random()`, one start, `bigint` ids, release build, shared 4-vCPU machine, load average 0.3–1.7; `examples/bfs_cost.rs` for the kernel, `bench_seconds` medians of 5–9 runs for SQL). There is no `count` mode, so `count(*)` over the call was the cheapest SQL form:

    | | kernel alone | SQL in `FROM` | SQL in the select list | recursive CTE |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | `bfs` depth 5 (92.6K rows) | 7.7 ms | 25.7 ms | 21.7 ms | 160 ms (6.2×) |
    | `bfs` depth 6 (604K rows) | 30.7 ms | 199 ms | 141 ms | 1.64 s (8.2×) |
    | `reachable_set`, 1M rows, 10M edges, before | 243 ms (304 ms in the database, through `reachable` to an unreachable target) | 494 ms | 393 ms | |
    | `reachable_set`, after (pull by Beamer's switch; load average 1.0) | 60–65 ms | 210 ms | 160 ms | |

    Postgres's own floor for a million one-column rows (`count(*)` over `generate_series`) is 80 ms in `FROM` and 24 ms in the select list. At 10M / 100M (in-process G(n, m)), the kernels take 0.114 s for `bfs` depth 6 (835K rows) and 5.96 s for `reachable_set` (100M row entries), against the soak's 0.69–0.76 s and 8.3–9.2 s through SQL. So `bfs` is about 85% row emission at every scale, and `reachable_set` was about two thirds kernel at full scale. Stack samples of the backend split the emission among the tuplestore (`heap_form_minimal_tuple`), building each level's visits and pgrx's per-row call. Writing the rows straight into the tuplestore in `FROM` (materialise mode, which Postgres offers there with `SFRM_Materialize_Preferred` and never in the select list, so select-list streaming would have been kept) was tried and measured no faster at 1M, so it was not kept.
  - **`reachable_set` is direction-optimising (Beamer; §10).** When the adjacency can be read backwards (`out` needs the reverse CSR; `in` reads the forward one; `both` is its own reverse), each level is decided afresh by Beamer's switch: pull if the frontier holds more than 1/24 of the id space (β = 24) and the unvisited ids' reverse rows hold fewer entries than the frontier's rows (m_u < α·m_f, α = 1), with row lengths read from the CSR offsets in O(1) a node and m_u summed over the unvisited ids only until it reaches α·m_f. A pulled level visits the unvisited ids in ascending dense id order, scanning each one's reverse row only until it meets the frontier. Push-only is kept for anything that reports `via` or promises the order within a depth: `bfs` (its `via` is the first frontier node, in frontier order, that reaches the node), `reachable`, `shortest_path` and walks. `reachable_set` reports the same nodes at the same depths, by depth; within a pulled depth they come in dense id order (property-tested against the naive reference and the cursor).
    - *What `edges_traversed` promises.* A pull scans at most the m_u entries and a push scans exactly m_f, so with α = 1 a pulled level scans fewer row entries than pushing it would, and `reachable_set`'s `last_stats().edges_traversed` is at most a push-only search's (property-tested). The exception is a graph of several relations without the stored merged CSR, read with every relation: its rows are merged on the fly, a row's length is then an upper bound (a target in several relations counts once per relation), and a pulled level can scan more than its push would. Beamer's α = 14 bounds a pull only by 14 times the push, and a first version that compared node counts (frontier against unvisited ids) could pull a level whose unvisited ids, in parts the start never reaches, had more in-edges than the frontier had out-edges (review of #21). At 10M / 100M, α = 14 and α = 1 took the same time (1.03 and 1.15 s, medians of 3; 14 pulls one level earlier, depth 7, and scans 73.4M entries instead of 16.9M), so α = 1 was kept for its bound. Deciding costs one pass over the frontier and, for a frontier over 1/24 of the id space, over the unvisited ids until their rows outweigh it; a pulled level and that pass count each unvisited id examined as a unit of work, so they poll every 65,536 even over empty rows.
    - *Before and after.* `edges_traversed` counts the row entries actually scanned: 17.9M rather than 100M at full scale through SQL (`last_stats()` in the soak, from external id 1; 16.9M in the `bfs_cost` kernel bench, from dense id 0), 1.65M rather than 10M at 1M. Throughput targets are therefore stated in the graph's edges per second, and `bench/soak.py` divides the version's edge count by the time. At 10M / 100M the kernel takes 1.24 s instead of 5.96 s (4.8×, about 81M graph edges/s on one thread); at 1M, 0.060–0.065 s instead of 0.243 s. Through SQL at full scale (the M7 soak re-run, §12) it takes 3.86 s instead of 8.3–9.2 s, 26M graph edges/s (6.15 s without the merged CSR). Through SQL at 1M, `FROM` takes 0.210 s instead of 0.494 s and the select list 0.160 s instead of 0.393 s (load average 1.0); `bfs` depth 6 is unchanged at 0.193 / 0.134 s.

## 10. Future directions (not v1)

- **Incremental maintenance.** Triggers or a logical-replication consumer write deltas to `warren.deltas`; traversal consults CSR + a small overlay (added edges in a hash map, removed in a tombstone set); a background worker compacts into a new version when the overlay grows. Seconds-level staleness without full rebuilds. Note that the stable-ID design already supports this: a delta-compacted version is just another version in the same ID space.
- **DSM shared cache and compute worker** (§6.9). The shared cache is M9 (designed in §5.6: one DSM segment per version rather than `dsa` or pgGraph's sealed `memfd`, §14 item 26); the compute worker stays in 1.x and would run on the shared copy.
- **Direction-optimising BFS** (push/pull switch when the frontier exceeds ~N/20 and the reverse CSR exists) for `reachable_set` and `components`. This is how GraphBLAS reaches multi-billion edges/s. *Implemented for `reachable_set` (M7, §9.3), by Beamer's edge-count switch (α = 1, β = 24).* `components` is union–find and does not use it; `bfs` keeps push for its `via`.
- **Parallel traversal** via the same thread pool as compute.
- **WASM vertex programs** (§6.8).
- **Text IDs**; **edge properties** beyond one weight (parallel arrays); **node property vectors** auto-synced from table columns.
- **Arnoldi** for non-symmetric spectra; **randomised SVD**; **spectral clustering** as a library algorithm (eigen → k-means over rows).
- **Pattern sugar, if ever.** `warren.walk` covers typed chains. What a pattern *language* would add, in ascending order of value: joins within a pattern (cycles, shared neighbours) and variable binding of intermediate nodes — both expressible today by intersecting walks in SQL — and **selectivity-aware start selection**, which is the real reason graph query planners exist and which `reverse` only approximates. If a language is ever added it should be SQL/PGQ (`GRAPH_TABLE`, SQL:2023) rather than Cypher: it is the standard, composes with SQL rather than sitting beside it, and Postgres has no implementation yet. The shape would be `warren.match('MATCH (a:paper)-[:cites*1..3]->(b)-[:authored_by]->(p) ...')` parsing to `warren.step[]` and a small join plan over walks — sugar over the existing IR, not a second engine. Not before v1 has demonstrated that users hit the limits of `walk`.
- **Custom scan node** so joins with traversals get cardinality estimates from version statistics.
- **Sparse matrix algebra beyond SpMV** (SpGEMM, sparse vectors, matrices as values), native and following GraphBLAS's algorithms (§6.10), and a **GPU backend** for `warren-core` kernels, both behind the kernel seam (§6.10, §6.11).

## 11. Implementation language and tooling

**Rust via [pgrx](https://github.com/pgcentralfoundation/pgrx).** The hot code is array manipulation and numerical kernels; pgrx gives SPI, SRFs, memory-context integration, GUCs, and a test harness that runs against real Postgres instances across versions. Target Postgres 16+ as baseline; test on 15–18. No runtime dependencies beyond Postgres.

Toolchain discipline (adopted from pgGraph's working setup):
- `pgrx` and `cargo-pgrx` are pinned to the same exact version (`=0.19.3`) and upgraded together in their own change. The Rust toolchain is pinned in `rust-toolchain.toml`.
- Per-major features are `pg15`…`pg18`, built with `--no-default-features --features pgNN`. `panic = "unwind"` is set in every profile.
- Lints: `unsafe_op_in_unsafe_fn = "deny"` and `clippy::undocumented_unsafe_blocks = "deny"`. `unwrap`/`expect`/`panic` are denied outside `cfg(test)`. Every `unsafe` block carries a `// SAFETY:` proof, and `warren-core`'s tests run under Miri in CI on every push to main (not on pull requests: `warren-core` has no `unsafe` today, so Miri checks only its dependencies' unsafe code along the tested paths, at 20+ minutes a run).

Workspace split, non-negotiable:

- `warren-core` — CSR, traversal, semiring kernels, gather, Lanczos. **No Postgres dependency.** Takes slices and a thread count; returns results. Unit-, property- and bench-tested standalone. `rayon` for parallelism; `faer` or `nalgebra` for small dense problems; `proptest` and `criterion` in dev-deps.
- `pg_warren` — the pgrx extension: catalog, SPI build pipeline, chunked persistence, cache, SQL bindings, GUCs. Thin.

## 12. Testing and benchmarking

### Correctness

- `warren-core` unit and property tests: CSR construction (duplicates, self-loops, dangling edges, tombstones, stable-ID merge across builds), BFS, shortest paths, components, every semiring kernel against a naive dense reference on small random graphs, `gather` with fixed- and variable-width messages against a naive implementation, Lanczos against a dense eigensolver on graphs with N ≤ 500.
- `vector_map`: every expression in Appendix A and the library takes the compiled path (asserted via `warren.explain_map`); compiled and executor paths agree bit-for-bit on random expressions over random vectors.
- **Use-case tests (Appendix A):** each worked example is an integration test with a generated fixture and the stated expected outcome. *Implemented (M2):* `bench/generate.py` writes seeded, deterministic fixtures (CSV + `load.sql` + ground truth) for every Appendix A fixture and the benchmark graphs; `test/run.py` runs `test/sql/*.sql` in fresh databases of a scratch cluster, with `-- fixture:` / `-- oracle:` directives and `assert.*` functions that raise on failure (format in `test/README.md`).
- Integration tests (`cargo pgrx test`): every SQL function; every `id_type`; directed/undirected; weighted/unweighted; relations present/absent; reverse present/absent; filters; refresh with a prior version loaded; staged → promote → retention → drop; vectors bound to retained versions; `compact` and the refusal to compare across it; rollback of a transaction containing a staged build leaves no rows; cancellation of long traversals and compute; memory cap enforcement.
- **Oracle tests:** every traversal function against an equivalent recursive CTE; PageRank and components against NetworkX on exported fixtures; `gather(... 'collect')` against a plain SQL join + `array_agg`; `diff` against SQL `EXCEPT`. Exact match on random graphs of ~10K nodes. This suite is the most valuable thing in the repository.
- Robustness: a `cargo fuzz` target for the segment decoder (§5.3); a differential proptest of bidirectional against single-direction shortest path (§9.3).
- Extension lifecycle: `cargo pgrx schema` output diffed against the committed `sql/pg_warren--X.Y.Z.sql` (*M2:* `sql/schema-drift.sh`, in CI on PG 16; both sides are normalised — comments dropped, entity blocks sorted — because pgrx's order is unstable and its comments carry source lines). From the first release on, install the previous release, run `ALTER EXTENSION pg_warren UPDATE`, and assert the resulting `pg_proc`/`pg_class` signatures equal a fresh install's (*implemented after 0.1.0:* `test/upgrade.py`, in CI on PG 15–18, compares functions with their signatures, defaults and attributes, relations, columns, storage, constraints, indexes, sequences, types, casts, grants, comments and extension configuration, and checks backfilled rows and a retention pass over versions written before the update; released install scripts are frozen by `sql/released.sha256`). *M7:* the state written before the update is written by each release's own build, from the commit `sql/released.commits` records for it, built with the cargo-pgrx of the pgrx its `Cargo.lock` pins and the toolchain its `rust-toolchain.toml` pins (one target directory per release and major, cached in CI with the release cargo-pgrx; the check requires `warren.version()` to be the release's after its install and the development one's after the restore), not by the development library over the old catalog; the check also reads a weighted multi-relation version built by the release and compares its `bfs`, `components` and `spmv` results with the release's, and runs again with a trusted install (`--trusted`). A `pg_dump`/restore round trip preserves `warren.*` rows (§5.1; *implemented in M7:* `test/dump.py`, plain and custom formats, in CI on PG 15–18).
- Cross-version tests: build v1, mutate tables (add/remove nodes and edges), build v2, assert dense IDs of surviving nodes unchanged, assert `distance` is zero when nothing changed and monotone in perturbation size for `edit`.

### Performance

Targets on a commodity 8-core machine, graph in cache, 10M nodes / 100M edges, avg degree 10:

| Workload | Target |
|---|---|
| BFS to depth 6 from one node | ≥ 20× faster than recursive CTE with btree on `(src)` |
| `reachable_set`, full component | > 50M edges/s traversed |
| `components()` | < 10 s |
| `pagerank`, 20 iterations, 8 threads | < 30 s (≥ 5 GE/s SpMV per thread-group) |
| `aggregate_neighbours`, d = 768, f32 | < 5 min |
| `eigen`, k = 20, normalised Laplacian | < 10 min |
| `refresh()` incl. reverse CSR | < 8 min, < 4 GB RAM |
| `diff` between two versions | < 30 s |
| Cold load into backend | < 15 s |
| Memory | the whole graph fits within the default 4 GB per-backend limits (`warren.max_cache_memory`, `max_build_memory`, `max_compute_memory`), with a library run's scratch vectors alongside |

The memory row is a positioning goal as much as a benchmark. Graphs of 10M nodes and 100M edges are beyond what most teams ever reach. Serving that scale from one ordinary backend, at default settings, removes the usual reason to export to a native graph database. The estimates (u32 targets, reverse CSR included, an `int8` id map, from the `bytes()` the cache counts; decimal GB, while the limits' `4GB` is Postgres's binary unit, 4 GiB ≈ 4.29 GB):
- one relation, unweighted: ~1.2 GB;
- one relation, weighted: ~2.8 GB;
- five relations, unweighted: ~2.8 GB with the merged CSR (per-relation targets 0.8 and offsets 0.8; merged CSR 0.8 + 0.16; id map 0.24), ~1.84 GB without it (`store_merged := false`, M7, §5.3);
- five relations, weighted: ~6.0 GB with the merged CSR (per-relation targets 0.8, weights 1.6 and offsets 0.8; merged CSR 0.8 + 1.6 + 0.16; id map 0.24), so it misses at the default `store_merged`; ~3.44 GB without it. The graph alone then fits, but with `pagerank`'s scratch (0.88 GB at 10M nodes) a run needs ~4.32 GB, about 25–30 MB over the 4 GiB cache cap, so the row is not quite met even then (and all-relations reads are slower); since the statement pin (§14 item 20) the excess is charged to `warren.max_compute_memory` instead of costing a reload per call. The M7 soak measured what a run holds (below): the cached bytes match these estimates to within 0.1%.

PageRank's transient scratch adds ~0.88 GB at 10M nodes. Workloads whose data is itself larger, such as d = 768 embeddings (~31 GB per f32 matrix, A.5), need the limits raised.

**Measured (M7 soak, `bench/soak.py`, `bench/results/soak/`).** *Measured on* a 4-vCPU Intel Xeon VM (no SMT), 15 GiB RAM, no swap, PostgreSQL 16.15, a release build, `warren.threads = 4` (set in each query session), `shared_buffers = 1GB`, `work_mem = 64MB`, `maintenance_work_mem = 1GB`, `max_wal_size = 1GB`, `wal_level = minimal`, `fsync`, `synchronous_commit` and `full_page_writes` off, `jit = on` and `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2` (the defaults, which the recursive CTE baseline may use), every `warren.max_*_memory` at its 4GB default. That is half the cores §12 assumes: CPU-bound rows would run up to about 2× faster on 8 cores. Graphs: G(n, m) with replacement, 10M nodes, 100M directed edges (99,999,953 after deduplication), from a seeded `random()` inside Postgres (case a through an unlogged `(src int4, dst int4)` table of 3.6 GB; the b cases read the same edges from the generator query, whose build is 8–14% slower than from the table); relations and weights derived from the endpoints; ids `bigint`. Peaks are each backend's `/proc` RssAnon peak (VmHWM, which adds the ~1.1 GB of shared buffers a backend touches, in parentheses). *The table is the full-scale re-run after all of M7* (10f68ab: the statement pin and idle-pin reclaim, the charged and streamed build, eigen's new defaults and steps, batched reads, direction-optimising `reachable_set`; `bench/results/soak/*-m7*.json`), on an idle machine (load average 0.4–1.6 at each case's start and end). "Was" gives the first soak's figure where it changed: case a at 6e20074 and 495646e, b-unmerged at f1987a1 and 495646e, b-merged at 495646e (`bench/results/soak/README.md` lists each file's build), measured with another agent's builds alongside (load average 2–6), where times of one workload varied by up to 2× between runs.

| Workload | Target | a: 1 relation, unweighted | b: 5 weighted, merged CSR | b: 5 weighted, `store_merged := false` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Segment bytes / cached bytes | | 1.12 / 1.20 GB | 5.92 / 6.00 GB | 3.36 / 3.44 GB |
| BFS depth 6 (1.47M nodes) vs recursive CTE | ≥ 20× | **11.0×**: 0.67 s vs 7.32 s (was 10.5–11×); about 85% of it row emission (below) | **23.4 s**: the version is over the cap, so each statement loads it (was 21.9 s); 0.71 s with the cap at 8GB | 0.71 s (was 0.80–0.85 s) |
| `neighbours`, one node | | 0.16 ms | **22.6 s** (the load); 0.18 ms at 8GB | 0.17 ms |
| `reachable_set`, full component (100M edges) | > 50M edges/s | **26M/s** (3.86 s; was 11–12M/s, 8.3–9.2 s; the projection from 1M was 27–34M/s) | **3.8M/s** (26.2 s, the load); 25M/s at 8GB (4.03 s; was 11.5M/s) | **16M/s** (6.15 s; was 7.5–7.7M/s) |
| `components()` | < 10 s | 6.2 s (was 4.5–5.4 s) | **29.2 s** (the load); 5.8 s at 8GB | **10.1 s**, borderline (10.6 s at 8GB; was 9.3–10.4 s) |
| `pagerank`, 20 iterations | < 30 s, 8 threads | 15.6 s, no load (17.3 s from cold, one load), peak 2.27 GB | **40.1 s**, one load (37.9 s from cold), peak 7.31 GB (was ≥ 900 s, stopped, ~170 reloads); 17.4 s at 8GB | **35.6 s**, no load (42.2 s from cold, one load), peak 4.51 GB (was ≥ 900 s, stopped); 32.7 s at 8GB |
| `eigen`, k = 20, normalised Laplacian, smallest, tol 10⁻⁶, f32 | < 10 min | **misses**: 0.59–0.61 s a step (200 steps in 117.6–122.2 s, ~26 s of it fixed cost; 0.46 s a step from 100 to 200), peak 4.31 GB (was 0.96 s a step, and 65 min unconverged at the then default 4,100 steps); ~14,500 steps for this class (below): ~2 h | **refused (53400)** at the default limits (at 10f68ab after its 200 steps, 141.5 s; since PR #22 before its first step): the run needs ~3 GB beside the 6.0 GB version, over the two limits together; at an 8GB cap 0.77 s a step (0.82 s from 100 to 200), peak 9.03 GB (was 0.83 s a step, with the load) | not run (was 2.6 s a step, with the load) |
| `refresh()` incl. reverse CSR | < 8 min, < 4 GB | 153 s, peak 1.55 GB (2.70) (was 146–167 s, 2.0 GB) | 260 s, peak 3.26 GB (4.44) (was 347 s, **9.0 GB**) | 228 s, peak 3.26 GB (4.44) (was 221–234 s, **5.5 GB**) |
| `diff` between two versions (0.1% of edges removed) | < 30 s | 17.8 s, peak 2.89 GB (was 13.5–22.3 s) | **refused (53400)** before loading, as designed: two 6.0 GB versions (12.0 GB) are over the two limits together (8.59 GB); before the pin, killed for memory at 12.8 GB in an uncommitted run | **58.4 s**, peak 7.21 GB, within the two limits (was 47.7–55.2 s, also 7.2 GB) |
| `distance`, `edit` / `degree` | | 0.58 / 0.16 s (was 11.7–15.9 / 2.7–4.4 s) | refused (53400), as `diff` | 11.6 / 23.4 s (was 17.5–36.4 / 28.5–30.6 s) |
| Cold load into backend | < 15 s | 1.9–2.2 s (was 2.2–2.9 s) | **23.0 s** (22.6–27.1 s over the run's loads; unchanged) | 10.3 s (7.4–13.3 s over the run's three loads; was 6.2 s) |
| Memory: graph and `pagerank` scratch within 4 GiB | fits | **fits**: 1.20 + 0.88 GB; the build and `diff` too | **misses**: the graph alone is 6.00 GB; the build fits (3.26 GB) and `pagerank` runs within the cache and compute limits together (one load), but every statement reloads, and `diff`, `distance` and `eigen` are refused | **misses by 30 MB**: 3.44 + 0.88 = 4.32 GB > 4.29 GB; the build fits, and the excess is charged to compute memory, so `pagerank` loads once and `diff` runs |

Not measured: `aggregate_neighbours` at d = 768 (two 31 GB matrices do not fit this machine).

What meets: on case a, every row but BFS-vs-CTE, `reachable_set` and `eigen`; on all three cases, `refresh()` (time, and private memory under 4 GB: 1.55, 3.26 and 3.26 GB, as §5.4 estimated) and, for the b cases, `pagerank` now loads its version once at the default limits. What misses, and why:
- *Traversal through SQL* (all cases). BFS and `reachable_set` are timed as `SELECT count(*) FROM warren.bfs(…)`, so emitting 1.5M and 10M rows from a set-returning function in `FROM` (which Postgres materialises) is in the time; the recursive CTE is fast on this graph (depth 6 reaches only 15% of it). *The split (M7, §9.3):* `bfs` depth 6 is about 85% row emission at every scale (kernel 0.114 s of 0.67 s at 10M; 31 of 199 ms at 1M), so the kernel cannot close its gap to 20× (11.0×); the costs are Postgres's tuplestore, pgrx's per-row call and building each level's rows, and writing straight into the tuplestore was measured no faster. `reachable_set` now pulls levels by Beamer's switch (kernel 1.24 s, from 5.96 s): through SQL 3.86 s, 26M graph edges/s, 2.2× the first soak but half the target, now more than half of it row emission (3.86 s against 1.55–1.75 s projected for the kernel in the database; the projection from 1M, 2.9–3.7 s, was slightly optimistic). Without the merged CSR, the pulled levels merge the relations' rows on the fly: 6.15 s, 16M/s.
- *A version larger than `warren.max_cache_memory` is loaded by every statement that reads it* (b-merged, §5.5): the 6.0 GB version costs 23 s a statement, so single calls are slow at the default cap (`bfs` 23.4 s, `neighbours` 22.6 s, `reachable_set` 26.2 s, `components` 29.2 s; 0.71 s, 0.18 ms, 4.03 s and 5.8 s at 8GB). *Fixed by the statement pin (§14 item 20), measured at full scale:* a library run is one statement and loads its version once, so `pagerank` takes 40.1 s at the default limits (23 s of it the load; 17.4 s at 8GB), where the first soak reloaded the version about 170 times and was stopped at 15 min. On b-unmerged, 30 MB over the cap with `pagerank`'s scratch, the run loads once (35.6 s at the default cap, 32.7 s at 8GB), where the first soak reloaded it on every call (≥ 900 s, stopped). Raise the cap, or use `store_merged := false`, to keep a graph between statements.
- *`pagerank` on the five-relation graphs* takes 35.6–40.1 s on these 4 vCPUs against < 30 s for 8 threads: b-merged's run is mostly its load (17.4 s with the graph cached), and b-unmerged's products merge five relations' rows (32.7 s even cached). Case a meets it at 15.6 s.
- *Two-version calls on b-merged are refused* with 53400 before either version is loaded (12.0 GB against 8.59 GB for the two limits together), as §5.5 designs: before the pin, `diff` loaded both versions outside every limit and the backend was killed at 12.8 GB. b-unmerged's `diff` runs within the limits (peak 7.21 GB, both versions pinned, the excess over the cap charged to compute memory) but misses the time at 58.4 s; case a's takes 17.8 s.
- *`eigen` does not converge in 10 min at 10M nodes on a uniform random graph* (every soak case): the smallest eigenvalues of its normalised Laplacian crowd at the edge of the bulk, and the step count grows as √N (below; ~14,500 steps projected at 10M). A step costs 0.59–0.61 s in the database on case a (0.46 s marginal; was 0.96 s before the M7 levers), so a solve would take about 2 h. On b-merged at the default limits, `eigen` is refused with 53400 because the 6.0 GB version's 1.71 GB over the cap and the run's ~3 GB of basis and vectors exceed `warren.max_compute_memory`; at 10f68ab the refusal came after 141.5 s, about the 23 s load plus 200 steps at case a's cost, when the first 40 MB eigenvector was charged (`b-merged-m7.json`); since PR #22 the solve's peak is checked before its first step, so the same call is refused at once (§6.5). At an 8GB cap a step costs 0.77 s (0.82 s marginal; was 0.83 s with the load).
- *`eigen` per graph class (M7; `bench/eigen.py`, `results/eigen/scaling/` and `step-cost/`).* The target row stands; what decides whether `eigen` meets it is the graph's spectrum (§6.5, "Why the steps grow"). Steps are for k = 20, normalised Laplacian, smallest, tol 10⁻⁶, f32, at 100K / 300K / 1M / 3M nodes of average degree 20, measured at 5611b0d (the cheap levers change the step cost, not the steps; the eight-lane dot products reassociate sums, so a count can move by up to a restart, not re-measured). The step cost at 10M is now measured in the database (the M7 re-run, case a, a directed graph of 100M edges symmetrised, so about the 200M stored entries of an undirected graph of average degree 20): 0.46 s marginal, 0.59 s over a 200-step call with its fixed cost. Step counts at 10M remain projected.

  | Graph class | Steps, 100K / 300K / 1M / 3M (ARPACK) | 10M: steps projected, step cost measured | Target (< 10 min) |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Community structure (block model, 40 equal communities, 16 of 20 edges inside) | 300 / 371 / 356 / 386 (328 / 373 / 370 / 429): flat | ~400 steps × 0.46–0.59 s: ~3–4 min | **met** (step count projected) |
  | Uniform random G(n, m) | 1,342 / 2,941 / 4,590 / not run (1,406 / 2,980 / 4,343): ∝ √N | ~14,500 steps (4,590·√10) × 0.46–0.59 s: ~1.9–2.4 h (the soak's case) | **missed** by an order of magnitude |

  Full solves were not run at 10M; 3M random was stopped to free the machine. A spectral transform (shift-invert) is the follow-up for random graphs (§14 item 21).
- *`components()` without the merged CSR* is borderline at 10.1 s (10.6 s cached at 8GB; 11.8 s and 10.2 s in the 0.4.0 re-run, below): it merges the relations' rows on the fly (5.8 s with the merged CSR cached).
- *Cold load of the merged graph* takes 23.0 s for 6.0 GB, about 0.26 GB/s (case a: 1.20 GB in 1.9 s; b-unmerged: 3.44 GB in 7.4–13.3 s, slower than the first soak's 6.2 s, not investigated).
- *Build memory* (fixed after the first soak, §5.4; now measured at full scale). `warren.max_build_memory` bounded only the offsets (§14 item 17), and the build held the edge list beside a copy of every row, then every forward and reverse CSR at once, while the edge reader kept ~17 B of Postgres memory per row of a graph with relations: 2.0, 5.5 and 9.0 GB. Every buffer is now charged to the setting and CSRs are streamed to their segments: the re-run's `refresh` peaks at 1.55, 3.26 and 3.26 GB (RssAnon; the build charges 1.56, 3.24 and 3.24 GB, run in `warren-core`), within 4 GB. VmHWM (2.70 and 4.44 GB) adds the shared buffers the backend touched, which `bench/soak.py`'s verdict counts.

**Re-measured for 0.4.0 (`bench/results/soak/*-040*.json`, `a-ab-*.json`; release build of 5b0bf81, which is adf0fff's code: 0.4.0 with Leiden's iterations).** The same machine, settings and command lines as the M7 re-run, plus 0.4.0's library, `louvain` and `leiden` at the default limits, and case a again with `node_order := 'locality'`; `bench/results/soak/README.md` has every figure. *No §12 row moved beyond the machine's run-to-run spread, and no target is newly met.* Every peak is unchanged to 0.01 GB. Case a's times were 4–15% slower than M7's, and `eigen` 33% (0.81 s a step), but an A/B of case a, three runs of each build alternately, finds no stage reproducibly slower: median ratios 0.87–1.05, `eigen` 1.05 (0.61–0.71 s a step against 0.56–0.61 s at 10f68ab), within the 10–20% that one build varies between runs on this VM. b-unmerged's `components` crossed its target (11.8 s against 10.1 s; 10.2 s at 8GB in the same run), as borderline as before. b-merged's `eigen` is now refused before its first step, after only its 21 s load (§6.5), and its second version was skipped for disk (5.92 GB with 8.8 GB free), so `diff` was not re-run (refused at M7, 53400). In locality order case a's first build peaks at 2.51 GB (1.55 GB in id order; §14 item 22's ordering scratch) in 163 s; its queries are within the spread of the id-order runs, as expected of a uniform random graph, which has no locality to recover. *The 0.4.0 library at this scale* (case a; seconds, private peak with the 1.2 GB version cached): eigenvector, Katz and HITS for 20 iterations 15.0, 9.8 and 17.3 s (1.8–2.0 GB); `clustering` 47.5 s (3.0 GB); `core_number` 22.8 s (3.2 GB); `strong_components_vector` 15.8 s; `minimum_spanning_forest` 54.4 s; betweenness, closeness and harmonic from 16 sources 61.1, 29.4 and 30.9 s; `most_similar` 2 ms; `similarity` of 100,000 pairs 0.55 s; `similar_pairs` over every 100th node 10.2 s (3.6 GB). b-unmerged takes 1.4–3.5× as long (weighted searches, merged rows); on b-merged each call also pays the 22 s load, and `similar_pairs` is refused (53400). *Louvain and Leiden do not complete at 10M nodes and 100M random edges on this machine:* `leiden` is refused (53400) at the default limits in its first level's refinement on cases a and b-unmerged (88 B a node beyond the 3.2 GB undirected view and the moving state), and both are refused on b-merged (the undirected view beside the version's 1.71 GB over the cap); `louvain` was cancelled by the harness's disk guard after 302–383 s, having written 7.5 GB of segments on case a (2.7 GB on b-unmerged, with less disk free), because on a graph without communities every level's quotient of the whole graph stays large and stays on disk until the run's transaction ends (§6.7 *Disk*). At 1M nodes the same graph takes 22–33 s for `louvain` (5–6 levels, 0.61 GB of segments, modularity 0.14) and 52–66 s for `leiden` (16–17 levels, 2.0 GB, modularity 0.20). A statement that runs `contract` is bounded by cache, compute and build memory together, not cache and compute alone: b-unmerged's `louvain` peaked at 9.50 GB private against 8.59 GB for the first two, the rest `contract`'s build scratch, charged to `warren.max_build_memory` (verified at 1M nodes: §14 item 20). So the memory row's "a library run's scratch alongside" does not hold for `leiden` (nor `louvain` on the merged graph) at this scale and default limits, and the disk a run needs is the binding limit for both: the in-memory `contract` of §6.7 (*Open*) is the fix.

*The statement pin at a tenth of the scale* (PR #16 review; `bench/results/soak/b-unmerged-scale10-*.json`, case b-unmerged at 1M nodes and 10M edges, the same machine under load): with the cap 3.1 MB under the version and `pagerank`'s scratch (429.0 MB against 344.1 + 88 MB), as at full scale, `pagerank` from a cold cache loads the version once and takes 6.14 s (5.77 s at a 1GB cap); the build before the pin (71e7272) reloaded it 156 times and took 60.7 s (6.0 s at 1GB). The full-scale re-run confirms it (one load, above).

Two defects the soak found were fixed (§5.5): a cold load peaked at 2.7× the version (3.2 GB for 1.2 GB; now 1.69 GB, the version plus one CSR's raw segments), because each segment's chunks were held in SPI memory and again in Rust before assembly, and vector reads kept every detoasted chunk until SPI finished; and a call that opened one version twice (`vector_norm_diff` of two vectors at the same version, every `pagerank` iteration) loaded a second copy when the cap had evicted the first (8.1 GB peak for a 3.44 GB graph; 4.7 GB after).

*Node order (0.4.0):* ordering dense ids for locality makes SpMV, BFS and `components` 1.25–1.66× faster at 10M nodes on a community-structured graph whose ids carry no structure, and 1.04–1.25× on a power-law graph, for 10–12 s of ordering at the first build or `compact` (§14 item 22; `examples/locality.rs`). On the soak's uniform random graph (case a through SQL, above) it gains nothing measurable, and its first build peaks at 2.51 GB against 1.55 GB.

Benchmark harness: generators for random, power-law (preferential attachment), grid, and *evolving* graphs (a base graph plus a parameterised perturbation, so `distance` methods can be evaluated for sensitivity and monotonicity); loads into `nodes`/`edges`; builds; runs the matrix against baselines (recursive CTE, NetworkX, igraph). Commit results per version to `bench/results/`. *Implemented (M2):* `bench/run.sh` runs `bench/matrix.json`; a workload names the functions it `requires` and is recorded as skipped until they exist. SNAP datasets (LiveJournal, Orkut, road networks) as secondary benchmarks.

## 13. Repository layout and milestones

```
pg_warren/
├── README.md               ← user-facing: what it is, install, 10-line example, semantics
├── DESIGN.md               ← this document, kept current
├── Cargo.toml              ← workspace
├── crates/
│   ├── warren-core/        ← CSR, traversal, kernels, gather, eigen; no Postgres dependency
│   └── pg_warren/          ← pgrx extension
├── docs/                   ← prior-art-notes.md (§4A), semantics, limits
├── sql/                    ← hand-written SQL, upgrade scripts
├── bench/                  ← generate.py, run.sh, results/
├── test/                   ← integration tests not expressible in cargo pgrx test
├── docker/                 ← one Dockerfile, parameterised by a PG_MAJOR build arg
└── .github/workflows/      ← fmt, clippy, unit, Miri (warren-core, on main), pgrx tests PG 15–18 on PGDG packages
                               (cargo pgrx init --pgNN=<pg_config>), schema-drift check (added in M2); upgrade-path check from the first release (v0.1.0) on (`test/upgrade.py`, added for 0.2.0); bench targets compile+run once in core tests
```

Each milestone leaves `main` green and demonstrable.

**M0 — Skeleton and prior-art reading.** Layout, pgrx builds, `CREATE EXTENSION pg_warren`, `warren.version()`, CI. Licence: Apache-2.0. The §4A reading is completed, `docs/prior-art-notes.md` is written, and §14 item 16 is recorded before M1 begins.

**M1 — warren-core: structure and traversal.** CSR with per-relation segments and tombstones; stable-ID merge; BFS, reachable set, shortest path, components. Property tests. `criterion` micro-benchmarks.

**M2 — Catalog, versions, build pipeline.** `create_graph`, `refresh` (promote/staged), `promote`, `drop_version`, retention, `compact`, `info`, `diff`. Chunked persistence. `int8` IDs. Tests for rollback-cleans-up and stable IDs across builds.

**M3 — Cache and traversal SQL.** Loader, all §9.3 traversal functions, `prewarm`/`evict`, oracle tests vs recursive CTE. `int4`, `uuid`. Relations and filters. `warren.walk` with `ends`/`count` modes, variable-length segments and `reverse`; oracle tests vs an equivalent chain of CTEs. `paths` mode may slip to M7 if predecessor bookkeeping proves intricate. Tag `v0.1.0` — a usable traversal index.

**M4 — Vectors and kernels.** `warren.vectors` persistence; `vector_from_query`, `matrix_rows`, `id_map`, etc.; `Message`-generic buffers; semiring SpMV/SpMM, `vector_map` with SQL-expression parsing (compiled subset + executor fallback + `explain_map`), `vector_reduce` in `warren-core` with `rayon`; GUCs for threads and memory cap; cancellation. *Done:* kernels in `warren-core::compute`; persistence and cache in `pg_warren::vector`; the SQL surface in `pg_warren::compute_sql` (§6.2, §6.3), with oracle tests against SQL joins and aggregates. `compact` carries persisted vectors into the new id space (§5.2).

**M5 — Gather and algorithm library.** `warren.gather` with all combiners; the PL/pgSQL library (PageRank, components-via-LPA, label propagation, SSSP vector, degree, `aggregate_neighbours`, triangles). Oracle tests vs NetworkX and vs SQL joins. Appendix A.1 (fraud ring) and A.3 (entity resolution) as integration tests. Benchmarks for the compute rows of §12.

**M6 — Spectral and distance.** Lanczos `eigen` with f32/f64 modes; `distance` with all five methods; evolving-graph benchmark; the §8 workflow and Appendix A.2 as integration tests end-to-end. Tag `v0.2.0`.

**M7 — Hardening.** A size-bounded LRU for the backend-local cache (`warren.max_cache_memory`, §5.5); 100M-edge soak; memory-context and allocator hygiene; docs for semantics, staleness, retention, the two change-detection patterns, and limits. Decide §6.10 from benchmarks, against §6.11's operation set (masked SpGEMM and sparse vectors included), and define the kernel seam it implies. **Install without a superuser:** make pg_warren a *trusted* extension (`trusted = true` in the control file), so a role with `CREATE` on the database can run `CREATE EXTENSION pg_warren`. Hosted Postgres providers generally require this before they offer an extension to customers. The work has three parts:
- Check every statement in the install and upgrade scripts against what a trusted extension script may run.
- Re-check §7's extension-owner identity switch. In a trusted install, the script runs as the bootstrap superuser while `pg_extension.extowner` is the calling role, so the role that owns the `warren.*` tables and the one the switch elevates to may differ.
- Keep later features within those rules: the v1.1 `DROP ROLE` event trigger needs a superuser, so it must be optional or done another way.

Test it by installing as a non-superuser in CI and running the suite. **Make the stored merged CSR optional:**
- Today a graph with several relations also stores the all-relations CSR, which is about 40% of a weighted multi-relation graph's bytes (§5.3).
- Add a per-graph choice. Without the merged CSR, all-relations reads (traversal over any relation, `spmv` without `rels`, `components`, `gather`, `diff`'s `all_relations`) merge the per-relation rows on the fly.
- Benchmark the cost, using M1's `relation_layout` bench for 1–5 relations, and set the default from the result.
- Aim: a five-relation weighted 10M/100M graph within the §12 memory row.

**The 100M-edge soak** covers two cases:
- an unweighted graph at default limits;
- a weighted multi-relation graph, with and without the merged CSR, recording the measured peak of build, cache and compute against each limit.

`docs/limits.md` gains a sizing table from those measurements. Tag `v0.3.0`. **Done (0.3.0, 2026-10-20):**
- the size-bounded LRU and the statement pin, which make `warren.max_cache_memory` plus `warren.max_compute_memory` an enforced bound on a backend (§5.5, §14 items 18 and 20; plus `warren.max_build_memory` during a `refresh`, and since 0.4.0 a `contract`);
- the build bounded as a whole by `warren.max_build_memory` and streamed to its segments (§5.4, §14 item 17);
- the trusted install, with the switch elevating to the catalog's owner rather than the extension's, and dumps that restore (§5.1, §7);
- the optional merged CSR (`create_graph(…, store_merged)`, stored by default; benchmarked for 1–5 and 10 relations, §5.3, §14 item 19);
- the 100M-edge soak (`bench/soak.py`), re-run at full scale after M7: every case builds within 4 GB of private memory (VmHWM, with shared buffers, 4.44 GB for the five-relation cases, §12); case a meets every §12 row but BFS-vs-CTE, `reachable_set` and `eigen`; the five-relation weighted cases miss the memory row, by 30 MB without the merged CSR; `docs/limits.md`'s sizing table is from it (§12);
- what the soak led to: its memory defects fixed (§5.5), `eigen`'s cheaper steps and new defaults and its refusal before solving (§6.5, §14 item 21), and direction-optimising `reachable_set` (§9.3);
- the §6.10 decision: native kernels, with GraphBLAS read rather than bound (§14 item 9). The kernel seam is defined there; making it code is follow-up work;
- the user documentation (README, `docs/`).

**M8 — 0.4.0: locality and the library.** Locality ordering of dense ids (§14 item 22); the planned library's groups 1 and 2 (§6.7); contraction, project and lift (§6.6, whose `PᵀAP` is the first SpGEMM) and Louvain and Leiden over them (§6.7 group 3). The native in-memory Louvain/Leiden kernel is deferred past 0.4.0 (§6.7).
**Done (0.4.0, 2026-10-22):**
- locality ordering of dense ids, opt-in per graph and on `compact` (§14 item 22): 1.25–1.66× on SpMV, BFS and `components` at 10M nodes on a community-structured graph, little on power-law graphs, nothing on uniform random ones;
- the planned library's group 1 in PL/pgSQL and group 2 as kernels, each against a NetworkX oracle and measured at 10M nodes (§6.7);
- contraction, projection and lifting (§6.6), and Louvain and Leiden in PL/pgSQL over them, with Leiden's iterations (default 2) (§6.7 group 3);
- the 100M-edge soak re-run for 0.4.0 (§12): no row moved beyond the machine's run-to-run spread; at 10M nodes `leiden` is refused (53400) at the default limits and `louvain` stopped for disk on a graph with little community structure, while both complete on the 10M block graph; a statement that runs `contract` is bounded by the cache, compute and build limits together (§14 item 20). The in-memory `contract` is the candidate fix (below).

**The road to 1.0** (decided 2026-10-21, §14 item 25). By the end of M8, everything §3 put in v1's scope has shipped, and more. What stands between it and 1.0 is what a 1.0 promises: a SQL surface that will not change, behaviour proven on real data and in real deployments, and working well where Postgres actually runs, behind poolers with many backends. Four milestones:

**M9 — 0.5.0: one copy per cluster.** The shared cache, **designed in §5.6** (decisions §14 items 26–31, the owner's decisions items 32–39, and the PR-sized work breakdown there): a loaded version lives once in its own dynamic shared memory segment, mapped by every backend that reads it, instead of once per backend. Today 50 pooled connections reading a 1.2 GB version hold 60 GB. pgGraph's sealed-`memfd` sharing was read and weighed against DSM rather than measured: both are kernel shmem pages, so the difference is in lifecycle, rendezvous and the `/dev/shm` limit, not speed (§5.6). With it:
- reads become `PARALLEL SAFE` where §9.3 holds them back only because of the per-backend cache;
- cold loads happen once per cluster, not once per connection;
- a graph larger than one backend's `warren.max_cache_memory` no longer reloads per statement.

`warren.max_cache_memory` keeps its per-backend meaning over private memory, and a new `warren.shared_cache_memory` bounds the shared versions cluster-wide; the accounting of §5.5 and §14 item 20 is restated for it (§5.6, §14 item 31). PG 17 and 18 share with no configuration; PG 15 and 16 need `shared_preload_libraries`, and without it keep the backend-local cache (§14 item 27). Versions are immutable, so sharing needs no invalidation beyond eviction. The background compute worker (§6.9) is not part of M9.

*Candidate for a 0.5.x release (after the shared cache, not part of §5.6's design):* the in-memory `contract` (§6.7, *Open*): within one statement, `contract` keeps the quotient it builds in memory, charged to the statement's budgets, instead of writing it as a graph version, and later calls in the statement read it like a cached version. The 0.4.0 soak (§12) showed why it is needed: on a 10M-node graph with little community structure `louvain` wrote 7.5 GB of level segments before it was stopped for disk, and `leiden` with its two iterations writes 7.8 GB even on the 10M block graph, all of it kept until the run's transaction ends. It removes that disk and WAL cost for Louvain, Leiden and any multilevel algorithm users write over `contract`. It does not address `leiden`'s refusal (53400) at the default `warren.max_compute_memory` on such graphs, which is its refinement's memory, not disk.

**M10 — 0.6.0: identity, lifecycle and visibility.**
- `text` node ids. Knowledge graphs key on strings (IRIs, slugs), and today they must be mapped to integers or `uuid` first.
- Ownership transfer of a graph.
- Cleanup of graphs and vectors whose owner role is dropped, as an optional superuser-installed event trigger, so a trusted install keeps working without it (§7).
- Per-role quotas on readers' vectors.
- Observability: what each backend, or the shared cache, holds and its bytes; cache hits, misses and evictions; and the progress of a long `refresh`, `eigen` or `louvain`, through a view or function a monitoring query can poll.

**M11 — 0.7.0: proven on real graphs and real deployments.**
- Benchmarks on real data, not only synthetic graphs:
  - SNAP's LiveJournal, Orkut and road networks (§12);
  - Appendix A's scale targets at 10M, which have not been run;
  - a head-to-head with igraph and Neo4j (with and without GDS) on the same machine and graph, published with its caveats.
- Robustness:
  - a soak of several days behind a connection pooler, watching for memory growth;
  - many backends refreshing and querying one graph at once;
  - physical streaming replicas, which §5.1 claims and nothing tests;
  - crash recovery during `refresh`;
  - SQL-surface fuzzing (sqlsmith-style), beside the segment decoder fuzzing that exists.
- Distribution: CI builds and tests on arm64 as well as x86_64 (most hosted Postgres runs on arm64), prebuilt packages for PG 15–18 on both, and a PGXN release.

**M12 — 1.0.0: the stable surface.** A review of the whole SQL surface before it is frozen:
- argument order, names and defaults (`persist` and `version` placement, the type witnesses such as `NULL::bigint`);
- SQLSTATEs used consistently;
- the GUCs.

Renames are cheap before 1.0 and costly after. Also a written stability and deprecation policy: within 1.x, functions and their behaviour change only additively, and removals go through a deprecation release. Release candidates first.

1.0.0 is signed off by the project owner, who first installs a release candidate outside CI and uses it in anger on a real corpus. What that turns up is fixed in further release candidates before 1.0.0 is tagged.

**Left to 1.x**, documented as limits rather than blocking 1.0:
- incremental maintenance (§10; a full rebuild per `refresh` is an honest model when staleness is documented);
- the background compute worker (§6.9);
- sparse matrix algebra beyond SpMV (§6.11);
- a GPU backend (§6.11) and WASM programs (§6.8);
- the §12 rows still missed:
  - BFS-vs-CTE and `reachable_set` are bounded by row emission; count, summary or vector output modes would close them;
  - `eigen` on uniform random graphs, where shift-invert is the lever;
  - parallel traversal.

## 14. Open decisions (make a call, record it in DESIGN.md, move on)

1. **Polymorphism over `id_type`** in SQL: `anyelement` runtime dispatch vs. generated overloads. Lean: `anyelement`. **Decided (M3): `anyelement`/`anyarray`** with run-time dispatch on `graphs.id_type` and an exact type match for id arguments; `"any"` for `node_filter` and a type witness for `components` (§9.3).
2. **Persistent store**: chunked `bytea` only, or additionally a regenerable `$PGDATA/pg_warren/*.csr` file for mmap? Lean: `bytea` only; measure before adding. Evidence from pgGraph (`docs/prior-art-notes.md` §1.3–1.4) supports this. Its file artifacts are copied and fully validated on every load anyway, so they are not zero-copy. They needed MVCC catalog publication, per-database namespacing and interrupted-replacement recovery added after the fact, and they have no standby support. M2 measures cold load from `warren.segments`; revisit only if it falls below ~300 MB/s.
3. **Chunk size** (4/8/32 MB). Measure.
4. **Parallel-edge deduplication**: drop with count; if weighted, sum or keep-first? **Decided (M1): sum**, in input order, counted in `versions.deduplicated_edges`; unweighted duplicates are dropped with the same count. pgGraph keeps parallel edges only because it returns per-edge source-row identities, which pg_warren does not.
5. **Self-loops**: keep. **Decided (M1): kept**; an undirected self-loop is stored once.
6. **Undirected weighted**: duplicate weight both directions. **Decided (M1): yes**, both directions carry the same weight.
7. **Default `max_depth`** on `reachable_set`: unbounded vs. cap. Lean: unbounded, documented. **Decided (M3): unbounded** (`max_depth DEFAULT NULL`); the visited set bounds the work at O(N + M), and SQL `LIMIT` in the select list stops it early (§9.3).
8. **Lanczos precision default**: f32 vectors/f64 accumulate vs. all f64. **Decided (M6): f32 by default**, every sum accumulated in f64, `Precision::F64` as the option; residuals floor near 10⁻⁷·‖M‖ in f32 (§6.5). On the 100K-node baseline f32 and f64 take the same steps (1,037 and 1,342) and their step times are within the noise; f32 halves the basis memory, which is what binds at 10⁷ nodes.
9. **Native kernels vs. SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS** (§6.10). Lean: native for v1; revisit after M6. M4 implements the kernels natively in `warren-core` (§6.3). The M7 decision is judged against §6.11's end state (Pregel and sparse algebra side by side, CPU and GPU behind one kernel seam), not against tier 1's current operations alone. **Decided (M7): native, with no binding, for v1 and for §6.11's operations.** GraphBLAS, LAGraph and OneSparse are read, not imported, as §4A requires. pg_warren adopts their vocabulary and descriptors, masked SpGEMM (Gustavson saxpy with dense or hash accumulators and the masked dot product, chosen per row from a flop estimate), sparse vectors with push/pull switching, cached per-version properties, and algorithms written over the primitives, each credited where used (§6.10). The reasons are context, not speed:
   - a binding adds a C library (two, with LAGraph), and a run-time compiler for its fast paths, to what hosted providers ship (§7);
   - it brings a second allocator and an OpenMP pool outside `warren.max_compute_memory`, `warren.threads` and the blocked-signals rule (§6.9);
   - its operations cannot be cancelled;
   - its operators cannot raise the SQL errors and orders of §6.3;
   - each version would be copied into its matrix form;
   - it has no counterpart to `gather`'s messages.

   Native kernels are not what misses §12: SpMV measured 0.42 G edges/s per thread and 1.2 G on four (re-run in M7), and the soak's `pagerank` met its row (15.2–16.7 s for 20 iterations at 10M/100M on 4 vCPUs). The misses are `eigen`'s time on random graphs (its SpMVs alone exceed the target, §14 item 21), SRF emission and a traversal frontier with no sparse form, the last of which is native follow-up work (§6.11 item 2). The kernel seam (`compute::kernels::Kernels`, §6.10) is defined for native CPU kernels now and a GPU backend later. Making it code is follow-up work. Licence was not a factor: SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS is Apache-2.0, like OneSparse.
10. **Compute threads inside the backend vs. requiring the background worker from day one.** Lean: in-backend threads for v1 (simpler lifecycle), worker in v1.1. M4 implements in-backend threads: a dedicated `rayon` pool per `compute::Workers` (§6.9). **Decided (M4):** in-backend, one pool per backend sized by `warren.threads`, its threads blocking every signal.
11. **`distance` summary statistics**: which scalar(s) each method returns by default. **Decided (M6): several per method**, returned as `jsonb` (§8): `edit` counts and Jaccard distance per relation and overall; `degree` W₁ and KS; `spectral` L2, relative and max; `subspace` projection-Frobenius distance, max angle and the angles; `embedding` mean/max L2 and cosine and the rows compared.
12. **`vector_map` executor fallback.** Build it in v1 or raise "not in compiled subset" until v1.1? Lean: build it in v1 — the one-language guarantee is the point. **Decided (M4): built** — row-wise `ExecEvalExpr` over the same analysed tree, JIT-compiled per the `jit` settings, agreeing with the compiled path bit for bit (§6.3).
13. **`gather` message source.** Accept only a SQL string, or also a relation name / vector name? Lean: all three, dispatched on the argument's form. **Decided (M5): all three** — a bound vector's name, else an identifier as a relation, else a query (§6.4); the domain kernel takes `(dense id, message)` pairs whatever the source.
14. **`warren.step` as a composite type vs. `jsonb`.** Composite gives type-checking and a clean `warren.s()` constructor; `jsonb` is easier to generate from application code. Lean: composite as the canonical IR with a `jsonb → warren.step[]` cast for convenience.
15. **`walk` path enumeration limits.** Hard cap on `max_results` for `mode = 'paths'` (lean: default 100,000, error above a GUC ceiling) and whether to support `'count'` exactly or approximately for variable-length segments (lean: exact). **Decided (M3, domain):** `count` is exact, for distinct ends and for walks (`u64`, an error on overflow); see §9.2. The domain bounds `paths` only by the `max_results` it is given; the default and ceiling are the adapter's (lean stands). **Decided (M3, adapter):** default 100,000 rows for any walk reporting a row per walk (`paths`, or `ends` without `distinct_ends`), and a `max_results` above the `warren.max_walk_paths` GUC (default 10,000,000) is refused (§9.2).
16. **Prior-art reading complete** (§4A). **Done (M0, 2026-10-19):** see [`docs/prior-art-notes.md`](docs/prior-art-notes.md) (pgGraph `b0f53df`, OneSparse `79a57cd`). Sections changed as a result (v0.6): §4A, §5.1, §5.3, §5.4, §5.5, §6.3, §6.7, §6.9, §7, §9.3, §10, §11, §12, §13, §14 items 2 and 4. Not a reuse decision (the policy is to build in full), but a gate.
17. **Relation count × node count.** Per-relation offsets grow as R·N (§5.3). **Decided (M1):** no fixed relation limit; a build is refused up front when its offsets would exceed the memory budget the adapter passes. **Decided (M2):** the budget is the `warren.max_build_memory` GUC (kB units, default `4GB`, minimum `1MB`, `USERSET` like `maintenance_work_mem`); `refresh` checks it with `GraphCsr::check_offsets` (before the edge query runs, and again whenever an edge row adds a relation), and an over-budget build fails with `configuration_limit_exceeded` before anything is written. **Decided (M7, build-peak):** the same GUC also bounds everything the build holds at once (§5.4: charged, estimated up front by `BuildPlan`, refused with 53400 before the version row is written). A streamed build holds only a group of relations' offsets at a time, but the R·N check is kept: a version whose offsets alone exceed the build budget would not load within the default cache limits either. Revisit with sparse per-relation offsets if graphs with many relations appear.
18. **Transient vectors.** Persisting every vector write costs WAL and TOAST per superstep (≈320 MB per f64 vector at 10M nodes) and pins old versions against retention. **Decided (M4 review):** per-call `persist bool DEFAULT true`; `persist := false` keeps the vector only in the backend, non-transactionally, without pinning retention (§6.2). Rejected: `_`-prefixed names (magic), and an `UNLOGGED` scratch table (still TOAST and vacuum cost, still per-superstep writes). Revisit with M7's cache bound (transient and cached vectors count against the backend's LRU then). **Revisited (M7):** transient vectors count against `warren.max_cache_memory` with cached versions and vectors, but are never evicted (they cannot be reloaded); a write that would take transient vectors alone past the cap fails with `configuration_limit_exceeded` (53400), while cached versions and vectors are evicted least recently used first (§5.5).

19. **Store the merged CSR by default?** (M7.) Leaving it out saves about 44% of a five-relation weighted graph's CSR bytes and about 40% of its build, and is what lets such a graph of 10M nodes and 100M edges (~3.44 GB) fit the default 4 GB limits alone (§12; with `pagerank`'s 0.88 GB of scratch it is ~4.32 GB, still 25–30 MB over 4 GiB: measured by the M7 soak at 3.44 GB cached, so a default-cap `pagerank` reloaded the version on every call until the statement pin, item 20, and its build peaks at 5.5 GB), but makes every all-relations read merge the relations' rows: at 2–5 relations BFS 2.1–3.2×, components 2.7–4.5×, `gather` 1.5–1.9× and SpMV 4.6–8.3× slower, and at 10 relations BFS 6×, components 10× and SpMV 11× (§5.3). **Decided (M7): stored by default** (`store_merged DEFAULT true`, and graphs created before 0.3.0 keep theirs); `store_merged := false` is the per-graph opt-in when memory binds. Results are identical either way, up to NaN payloads. Revisit with sparse per-relation offsets or a faster merge (a merged row cache, SIMD), or if most workloads turn out to be relation-masked.

20. **Which budget governs what a statement holds in the cache?** (M7 soak; restated for M9, §5.5.) The soak found §5.5's "peak ≈ `max_cache_memory` + `max_compute_memory`" was an estimate with two holes: a version that fitted the cap alone but not beside a library run's scratch was evicted after every call (30 MB over the cap turned `pagerank`'s 32 s into more than 15 minutes), and `diff` and `distance` loaded both versions outside every limit (12.8 GB, OOM-killed, on 6 GB versions). **Decided: the sum is an enforced bound on a backend's private memory** (plus `warren.max_build_memory` while a `refresh` or `contract` runs in the statement: the 0.4.0 soak's `louvain` on b-unmerged peaked at 9.50 GB, past the 8.59 GB of cache and compute; at 1M nodes, with the three limits at the least each needs, 1.065 GB together, it ran at 1.083 GB, the 15 MB past the sum being Postgres's own). As built (§5.5): every version and persisted vector a top-level statement reads is pinned until the statement ends (a `MessageContext` reset callback, so on error and cancel too) or its top-level transaction does (a `TopTransactionContext` callback); a private entry in the backend's statement set, a shared version in the registry's pins (§5.6). Trimming to `warren.max_cache_memory` evicts only unpinned private entries. What the private cache cannot evict (transient vectors, pinned entries a call uses, evicted versions calls hold through the `Weak` that shares them) and its idle pinned entries are charged past the cap to each call's `warren.max_compute_memory` budget as held; a reclaim hook evicts, least recently used first and only on the main thread, the idle entries the budget counted, when a charge would otherwise fail; kernels reclaim ahead for buffers allocated whole on the pool, and what grows on the pool unforeseeably gets only the room left (53400 even where eviction would have made room). A private load that would take what cannot be evicted past the two limits together is refused with 53400 before it is read; two-version calls take what the private or shared cache holds first and admit the missing ones together. Shared versions are counted by neither per-backend setting: charged to no call, reclaimed by none, bounded by `warren.shared_cache_memory` (item 31). Not bounded: a traversal's visited set and frontier, and calls running at once in one statement. Rejected: executor-nesting hooks (Postgres exports no nesting level, and repairing a depth count on error needs `PG_FINALLY` around Postgres calls, §11; they also miss the statement that loads the library); pinning only per transaction (the cap would not hold between a transaction block's statements); evicting idle pinned entries when a budget is made (a statement alternating between two graphs that do not fit the cap together reloaded them on every call, as 0.2.0 did, with the compute budget free), or only until their excess fits half the budget (halves every call's room); and routing worker charges to the main thread to reclaim (couples `Workers::run` to the budget for a rare case). *Measured* at a tenth of the soak's scale: §12.

21. **`eigen` at scale: which solver, and which defaults?** (M7, after the soak's `eigen` did not converge in the default 4,100 steps at 10M nodes, §12.) *Measured* (`bench/eigen.py`, `results/eigen/`; k = 20, normalised Laplacian, smallest, tol 10⁻⁶, f32; graphs of average degree 20): the step count is set by the spectrum, not by the solver. On a uniform random graph G(n, m) the 20 smallest eigenvalues crowd at the edge of the bulk (0.564…0.566 at 100K, in a spectrum 1.436 wide), the gap λ₂₁ − λ₂₀ shrinks about as 1/n (9.1·10⁻⁵, 3.0·10⁻⁵, 7.9·10⁻⁶ at 100K, 300K, 1M) and the steps grow about as √n (1,342, 2,941, 4,590; ARPACK 1,406, 2,980, 4,343), as Lanczos's √(spread/gap) predicts. On a block model with 40 equal communities they sit in a cluster near 0.18, far below the bulk (0.55), and the steps stay flat (300, 371, 356). What else was tried, at 100K, in prototypes against the same counts: Chebyshev-filtered thick-restart Lanczos took 1.45–3× the SpMVs with a filter cut from the first cycle's Ritz values, and 1.03× with an oracle cut at λ₂₁ (9× fewer basis sweeps), but an adaptive cut lost the subspace at each refilter (4× the SpMVs on 2K nodes); at the measured split of a step (about half SpMV, half basis, at 1M nodes) its best case is about 1.8× faster, and it cannot reach the target on a random graph, whose SpMVs alone exceed it (below). LOBPCG (SciPy) needed 3× (random) and 7× (block model) the operator applications; its Jacobi preconditioner is the identity here, the normalised Laplacian's diagonal being 1. A larger basis (ARPACK `ncv` 41 → 121) saved at most 17%. A looser tolerance saved little and misplaces clustered eigenvalues (tol 10⁻⁴: 20% fewer steps, λ₂₀ wrong by 9·10⁻⁵). **Decided (M7):** keep single-vector thick-restart Lanczos (§6.5) and take the cheap levers: the basis passes sum their dot products in eight lanes and rotate by row chunks; an undirected graph's operator symmetrises with one product (its reverse CSRs equal its forward ones, so `(A + Aᵀ)/2` is `A` to the bit); `tol` defaults to 10⁻⁶ for `eigen` and `distance` (it was 10⁻⁸, below the 10⁻⁷·‖M‖ floor of the default `f32` vectors: 5–16% more steps, eigenvalues within 10⁻¹¹); `max_iter` defaults to 1,000·m (it was 100·m, which stopped the 1M random graph short). The f32 residual floor is why 10⁻⁶ is the default: a tighter `tol` is honoured only with `dtype := 'f64'`. A solve that does not converge now runs about 10× longer before its `WARNING` (41,000 operator applications for k = 20, about 7 h at the soak's 0.6 s a step); `statement_timeout`, or a cancel, is the stop. `distance('spectral')` compares eigenvalues, which converge quadratically in the residual: at tol 10⁻⁶ they agree with ARPACK's to better than 10⁻⁹ (10⁻¹¹ at 100K), far below the changes it is used to detect; `distance('subspace')` needs eigenvectors, which are well defined only when the k-th eigenvalue is separated from the next (a community graph's, not a random graph's bulk edge). The §12 target is restated accordingly. Revisit with Chebyshev filtering if the basis share of a step grows (larger k), or with a preconditioned solver if uniform random graphs at 10⁷ nodes matter.

22. **Order dense ids for locality?** (Decided 2026-10-20, after M7; implemented in 0.4.0, below.) At 10M nodes SpMV and traversal are memory-bound: a row's targets stream sequentially, but each edge reads its neighbour's value (`x[j]`, a visited bit) at a random position, so it costs a cache line for 8 useful bytes, and the same SpMV that runs at ~1.2 G edges/s at 100K nodes (`x` in cache) runs at ~0.28 G at 10M (§6.5, §12). IFA makes a node's neighbour *list* contiguous; giving neighbours nearby dense ids makes their *data* share cache lines too. **Decided:** an opt-in per-graph choice, `create_graph(…, node_order := 'id' | 'locality')`, default `'id'` (today's `id_type` sort order), recorded in `graphs` (a schema change, with its upgrade script), and an override on `compact(…, node_order := …)`. Ids are ordered only where §5.2 already lets them change, the first build and `compact` (a new id space and compaction boundary, vectors moved by the renumbering), so stable dense ids, vectors, `diff` and `distance` are untouched; later refreshes keep surviving ids and append new nodes, so locality decays as the graph changes and `compact` restores it. The first build assigns provisional ids, builds, computes the order and applies the permutation to the CSRs and id map, within `warren.max_build_memory`. Start with an O(N + M) ordering (Reverse Cuthill–McKee or BFS order) and measure SpMV, BFS and `components` against `'id'` on community-structured and power-law fixtures (a uniform random graph has no locality to recover); costlier orderings (Rabbit order, Gorder) only if that measurement justifies them. Rejected: an internal permutation layer under stable user-facing ids (every vector stored and compared through it), and reordering on every refresh (breaks §5.2). Also rejected for now: SIMD kernels, since the hot loops are bound by memory, not arithmetic (discussed 2026-10-20).
    *Implemented (0.4.0).* `graphs.node_order` (`'id' | 'locality'`, default `'id'`, a `CHECK`ed column; the 0.3.0 → 0.4.0 update backfills `'id'`) is set by `create_graph(…, node_order)` and reported by `info`; anything else is refused with 22023. The domain is `warren_core::graph::node_order`: `NodeOrder`, and `Permutation::locality(&EdgeList, id_space, &BuildMemory)`, which builds the undirected view of the edges as a scratch CSR (direction, relations, weights, self-loops and parallel edges ignored; each row sorted and folded), then runs Cuthill–McKee: components are started from the node of least degree (lowest id first; isolated nodes therefore first), each node's unvisited neighbours are appended by ascending degree, then id, and the whole order is reversed (RCM), so isolated nodes, and in compaction tombstones' former slots, end last. It is O(N + M) besides sorting each scratch row, deterministic, and depends only on the edges as a set and the provisional (id-order) ids, not on row order or multiplicity. `EdgeList::renumber` and `IdMap::renumber` apply a permutation in place; the CSRs are then built from the renumbered edges by the ordinary build, so forward, reverse and merged CSRs, weights (summed in the same input order), deduplication and the CSR invariants are exactly those of any build (property tests: the permutation is a bijection; a locality build is the id build under it, every CSR's edge set and weights by external id; BFS depths, components and SpMV by external id are identical; Miri runs them, but for SpMV, whose thread pool it cannot run). *First build*: §5.4. *Compaction*: `warren_core::version::compact(version, order, memory)` renumbers live nodes in external-id order as before, then, for `'locality'`, orders that edge list, renumbers it and the id map, and follows `Renumbering` with the permutation, so `move_vectors` moves vectors by node unchanged. Because the order depends only on the live edges and external-id order, compacting a locality build without tombstones changes no id (tested). **Decided: `compact(name, node_order DEFAULT NULL)` persists an order it is given** to `graphs.node_order` (NULL uses the graph's): the column then always says how the current id space was ordered, a later plain `compact` keeps that order rather than silently reverting, and `compact` is how an existing graph changes order (owner only, like `compact` itself). *Memory*: the scratch is 8 B per node of offsets, 8 B per node of permutation, two endpoint widths per node (the order and the start list) and two endpoint widths per edge (the undirected rows), all charged to `warren.max_build_memory`: 1.04 GB at 10M nodes and 100M edges (`u32`; measured, 104 MB at 1M/10M), freed before the CSRs are planned (only the 80 MB permutation lives until the edges are renumbered; renumbering the id map allocates a new tombstone bitmap, charged, only if it has tombstones, which neither a first build nor a compaction has). A first build in locality order therefore peaks while ordering, at the edge list and id map plus that scratch: measured (as charged, `examples/locality.rs`, `int8` ids, one unweighted relation, the edge list reserved up front) 2.48 GB at 10M/100M, against 1.44 GB once the edges are read and 1.56 GB for the whole id-order build (§5.4), so still within the 4 GiB default; a refusal still precedes every write (`tests/build_memory.rs` covers locality builds: the charge covers every allocation, and the plan the charge). Neither ordering nor renumbering polls for interrupts: no part of a build or a compaction does yet (only the source cursors poll, §5.4), and making them cancellable means taking `Interrupts` through `warren-core`'s build and compaction and the adapter's build pipeline, a change of its own; ordering takes about 10 s at 10M/100M. `compact` charges only the order's scratch to the budget (its own rebuild is not charged, as before), and is refused with 53400 past it. `compact` is no longer `STRICT` (its optional argument); `compact(NULL)` is an error, not NULL. The update drops and re-creates `create_graph` and `compact` and grants again any privileges set on them, as granted by the functions' owner (the original grantor and grant-option chains are not kept), then deletes the `pg_init_privs` rows those grants make inside the extension script, as a fresh install has none for them, so `pg_dump` still emits the grants. Every operator it evaluates is an exact-match `pg_catalog` one (`OPERATOR(pg_catalog.=)` with explicit casts): the script runs as the bootstrap superuser, and a trusted install's installer owns the extension's nominal schema (`public`), where an `=` (oid, regprocedure) would otherwise have captured `oid = '…'::regprocedure` (found in review). `test/upgrade.py` checks, from 0.3.0, that privileges survive in the catalog and in a dump, that initial privileges match a fresh install's (for every extension member, on every path), and that such a planted operator never runs.

    *Measured* (`cargo run --release -p warren-core --example locality -- sbm|powerlaw NODES 10 5`; the 4-vCPU Xeon of §12, medians of five runs, the kernels alone in `warren-core`, directed graphs with reverse CSRs, one relation, average out-degree 10; `'id'` is a random permutation of the nodes, i.e. external ids that carry no structure, the case locality ordering is for; seconds, id → locality, speed-up):

    | | SBM 1M / 10M edges | SBM 10M / 100M | power law 1M / 10M | power law 10M / 100M |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | SpMV, 1 thread | 0.053 → 0.038 (1.39×) | 0.876 → 0.582 (**1.51×**) | 0.042 → 0.033 (1.27×) | 0.677 → 0.601 (1.13×) |
    | SpMV, 4 threads | 0.0121 → 0.0093 (1.30×) | 0.246 → 0.151 (**1.63×**) | 0.0099 → 0.0110 (0.90×) | 0.165 → 0.159 (1.04×) |
    | BFS (whole component, out) | 0.259 → 0.219 (1.19×) | 6.68 → 5.34 (1.25×) | 0.144 → 0.114 (1.27×) | 2.56 → 2.08 (1.23×) |
    | `components` | 0.198 → 0.143 (1.39×) | 3.20 → 1.93 (**1.66×**) | 0.147 → 0.115 (1.27×) | 2.52 → 2.02 (1.25×) |
    | ordering (renumbering) | 0.62 (0.06) | 10.0 (0.72) | 0.78 (0.08) | 12.1 (1.03) |

    SBM: a stochastic block model of 1,000-node communities, 90% of edges inside; power law: Chung–Lu, exponent 2.1. For the SBM the planted order (each community contiguous), the best a community-aware order could do, measured 0.031 / 0.0080 / 0.194 / 0.111 s at 1M and 0.588 / 0.123 / 4.22 / 1.93 s at 10M: RCM matches it on one-thread SpMV and `components` at 10M but recovers only part of it for four-thread SpMV and BFS (the mean id gap of an edge falls from 3.3M only to 2.5M, against 0.33M planted: a breadth-first order of a small-world graph interleaves communities across its wide levels). On the power-law graph the gain is small (4–25%; four-thread SpMV is within noise, and at 1M slower, its rows' work spread less evenly across the threads). The ordering costs about 10–12 s at 10M/100M, about 7% of a 153 s first build (§12), paid only at the first build and at `compact`. **Conclusion:** worth its opt-in on community-structured graphs whose ids carry no locality (1.25–1.66× on these kernels at 10M nodes), marginal on power-law graphs; `'id'` stays the default (ids that already follow the structure, such as ids assigned in crawl or insertion order of a community, lose nothing to it). Costlier orderings (Rabbit order, Gorder, or a community detection such as label propagation followed by a sort) would aim at the gap between RCM and the planted order on SBM-like graphs; they are follow-up work, not started. Locality decays as refreshes append nodes; `compact` restores it.

23. **Should `vector_map` skip tombstoned rows?** (Open, 0.4.0.) Tombstoned rows hold zero in every vector and `vector_map`'s result is zeroed there, yet it evaluates the expression at them, so `x / c` with `c` a constant vector fails on any version with a deleted node (§6.7 found it in `pagerank`). Skipping them would remove the trap for user programs as well as the library, but changes a primitive whose compiled and executor paths must agree (§6.3), and an expression's errors would then depend on which nodes are deleted.

24. **Should `pagerank` warn when it stops at `max_iter`?** (Open, 0.4.0.) The group 1 library warns (§6.7), as `eigen` does; `pagerank` (M5) returns `max_iter` silently, so its caller cannot tell a last-iteration convergence from a stop. Warning would change a released function's messages.

25. **What does 1.0 require beyond v1's scope?** (Decided 2026-10-21, after M8.) §3 put the shared cache and text ids after v1. Measured against how Postgres is deployed, that leaves a 1.0 that breaks behind a connection pooler: a version is held once per backend. **Decided:** 1.0 includes the shared (DSM) cache (M9) and `text` ids (M10), and is gated on proof on real data and deployments (M11), on a reviewed, frozen SQL surface (M12), and on the owner's sign-off after using a release candidate on a real corpus. Incremental maintenance, the compute worker, sparse algebra beyond SpMV, GPU and WASM stay in 1.x (§13).
